Theologically the verse is among the most dangerous and the most merciful in the opening of al-Baqarah. Dangerous, because a fool can hear *ʿafawnā* as a license to take another calf. Merciful, because a despairing soul can hear it as the reason not to follow Iblīs into the refusal to ask. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The one who repents from sin is like the one who has no sin” (Ibn Mājah). He said, “Allah stretches out His hand by night to accept the repentance of the one who sinned by day, and stretches out His hand by day to accept the repentance of the one who sinned by night, until the sun rises from its west” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said that Ādam’s children err, and the best of them are those who repent. 2:52 is those hadiths in a nation’s life. A whole people is pardoned *after that*. The “that” is *shirk*. If *shirk* of a calf can be followed by *ʿafw* when they turn, the smaller calves of this *ummah* are not a reason to stop turning.

Al-Qurṭubī and the jurists are careful: the pardon of 2:52 does not mean that *shirk* is a small thing, nor that it is forgiven without *tawbah*. 4:48 says Allah (الله) does not forgive that partners be associated with Him, and He forgives what is less than that for whom He wills — meaning, as Ahl al-Sunnah explain, He does not forgive *shirk* of one who dies upon it, and He forgives it if the servant turns in this world, as these turned, as 2:54 will show. 2:52 is not a cancellation of 4:48. It is its door of *tawbah* opened upon a people who had built a god of gold.

The Prophet (ﷺ) also taught the gratitude that is the verse’s purpose. He stood in prayer until his feet swelled, and when ʿĀʾishah (may Allah be pleased with her) asked why, when he had been forgiven, he said, “Shall I not be a grateful servant?” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Pardon that does not produce *shukr* has been received as a thing to be spent, not as a *niʿmah* to be remembered. Israel is told the purpose so that they will not miss it. This *ummah*, pardoned in ways it does not count, is told by that hadith the same purpose.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that *min baʿdi dhālika* is placed so that no one will think the pardon was because the crime was small. It was *after that*, after *shirk*, after a lowing statue on the shore of a split sea. The size of the *dhālika* is the size of the *ʿafw*. “Say, ‘O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of the mercy of Allah’” (39:53) is this verse made into a general address. Despair after a calf is a second *ẓulm*. Shamelessness after a calf is a third. Gratitude is the remaining road.

Ibn Kathīr adds that the pardon here is connected to the *tawbah* of 2:54, not a pardon of those who persisted. The Qur'an can state the Lord’s *ʿafw* before it states the servant’s turning, because the turning itself is a gift. “Then He turned to them so that they might turn” (9:118). 2:52 is that priority of mercy, without which 2:54’s swords would have been the end of a people and not the beginning of a forgiven remnant.

For the reader the verse is a pause between a crime and a command. You have a “that” in your life, a calf of some metal. After that, there can be *ʿafw*. The *ʿafw* is so that you will be grateful, not so that you will be skilled in taking calves. Gratitude is the proof of receipt. The next verse will name another favor that came in the same period: the Book and the *Furqān* given to Mūsā (peace be upon him), the reason he climbed the mountain, the thing the calf was a delay of. Pardon, then Book. A people who have been forgiven are a people who can still be guided.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:53

> And ˹remember˺ when We gave Moses the Scripture—the standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺ that perhaps you would be ˹rightly˺ guided.

**Commentary:**

The mountain’s purpose is now named: *wa idh ātaynā Mūsā al-kitāba wa al-furqāna laʿallakum tahtadūn*, “And when We gave Mūsā the Book and the Criterion, so that you might be guided.” *Ātaynā*, We gave. The giver of the sea and the pardon is the giver of a scripture. *Al-kitāb* is the Torah, the writing that Mūsā (peace be upon him) received on the tablets, “written tablets of admonition and explanation of all things” (7:145). He threw them down in anger at the calf and then took them up; “and in their inscription was guidance and mercy for those who fear their Lord” (7:154). 2:53 speaks of the giving as a *niʿmah* upon the people, not only upon the man. A prophet’s Book is a nation’s gift. “We gave Mūsā the Book and made it a guidance for the Children of Israel: that you not take other than Me as Disposer of affairs” (17:2).

*Wa al-furqān*, and the Criterion. *Furqān* is from *f-r-q*, the same root that split the sea in 2:50. A criterion is a splitter: it separates truth from falsehood, lawful from unlawful, the path from the calf. The mufassirūn have read *al-furqān* as another name of the Torah, or as the judgments within it, or as the help that distinguished Mūsā from Pharaoh, or as the splitting of the sea itself mentioned in sequence. Al-Ṭabarī allows that the Book is the writing and the *Furqān* is what it does: it distinguishes. Ibn Kathīr prefers that both words name the Torah from two sides, as the Qur'an is called Book and *Furqān* (25:1). “We gave Mūsā and Hārūn the *Furqān*, and a light, and a reminder for the mindful” (21:48). A people coming out of Egypt, out of a house of idols and of a drowned king, needed more than a rescue. They needed a splitter that would remain when the sea had closed.

*Laʿallakum tahtadūn*, so that you might be guided. The same *laʿalla* as *tashkurūn* in 2:52. Pardon was for gratitude; the Book is for guidance. The two purposes are the two halves of 2:2: a Book, a guide for the mindful. Israel is given, in 2:52–53, what 2:2–5 described as the condition of *falāḥ*. If they are not successful, it will not be because a Book was withheld. Ibn ʿAbbās treated the verse as a reminder to the Jews of Madinah that the Torah in their hands was a *furqān* that already distinguished the coming Prophet (ﷺ) from the liars, and that refusing him was refusing the purpose of their own *Furqān*. The Qur'an is *muṣaddiq* of that *Furqān* (2:41). To use a criterion and then refuse what it identifies is to break the tool one was given.

The Prophet (ﷺ) received a Book that names itself by the same word: “Blessed is He who sent down the *Furqān* upon His servant, that he might be a warner to the worlds” (25:1). He said, “I have left among you that which, if you hold to it, you will never go astray: the Book of Allah” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the sermon of the Farewell). He said the Torah, the Gospel, and the Qur'an are all from the one Source, and that the later confirms the earlier. 2:53 is the giving of the second of those, in time, to a people who would be asked, in Madinah, to receive the third. Guidance is the stated purpose of each giving. A Book that is recited without *ihtidāʾ* is 2:44’s recitation: *tatlūna al-kitāb* without *ʿaql*.

Theologically the verse also teaches that guidance after *shirk* is possible because a Book is given after a calf. Allah (الله) did not say: you took a calf, therefore no Torah. He pardoned, then He gave. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that this is the hope of every community that has fallen: the revelation does not pack and leave at the first idol. It remains, as a *furqān*, so that the idol can be named as an idol and left. The Sāmirī’s calf did not prevent the tablets. It delayed them, angered a prophet, and then was burned. The tablets remained.

The giving of a Book after a calf is also a judgment on anyone who says a community is too corrupt to be addressed. Allah (الله) addressed them. He wrote for them. He called the writing a *furqān*, as if to say: you who could not distinguish a statue from the Lord of the sea, here is a distinguisher. The failure, if it comes, will be a failure of *ihtidāʾ*, not of provision. “Indeed, this Qur'an guides to that which is most upright” (17:9). Israel’s Torah was that, for its time, in its purity. The Jews of Madinah are told that the purpose *laʿallakum tahtadūn* is still the purpose, and that the confirming Book is the guidance arriving again.

The Prophet (ﷺ) used to recite the stories of Mūsā (peace be upon him) as a man recites a brother’s honor, and he named ʿĀshūrāʾ a thanks, and he named the Qur'an a *furqān* that confirms. To receive 2:53 as a Muslim is to receive a debt: the Book in your hands is the later Criterion, given for the same *laʿalla*. If Israel was guided by tablets of stone, this *ummah* is guided by a recitation preserved in chests and in pages. Ingratitude toward either giving is of one family.

For the reader the verse is a question about the Book in his hands. You have a *furqān*. Does it split, for you, the true from the false, or is it a recitation that does not distinguish a calf when a calf is in the room? The Children of Israel were given the Criterion so that they might be guided. This *ummah* was given a Criterion that confirms theirs. Both givings have the same *laʿalla*. The next verse will return to the calf with the hardest of the conditions of pardon: Mūsā’s command that they turn to their Creator and that the guilty be executed by their own hands. *ʿAfw* is not cheap. *Tawbah* of a people who have worshipped a statue will look like blood as well as words.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:54

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “O my people! Surely you have wronged yourselves by worshipping the calf, so turn in repentance to your Creator and execute ˹the calf-worshippers among˺ yourselves. That is best for you in the sight of your Creator.” Then He accepted your repentance. Surely He is the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful.

**Commentary:**

The pardon of 2:52 is now given its earthly form in the mouth of Mūsā (peace be upon him): *wa idh qāla Mūsā li-qawmihi yā qawmi innakum ẓalamtum anfusakum bi-ittikhādhikumu al-ʿijla fa-tūbū ilā bāriʾikum fa-qtulū anfusakum dhālikum khayrun lakum ʿinda bāriʾikum fa-tāba ʿalaykum innahu huwa al-tawwābu al-raḥīm*, “And when Mūsā said to his people, ‘O my people, you have wronged yourselves by taking the calf, so turn in repentance to your Creator and kill yourselves. That is better for you with your Creator.’ Then He turned to you. Indeed He is the Accepter of Repentance, the Most Merciful.” The prophet does not minimize. *Yā qawmi*, O my people — a vocative of belonging, not of exile. *Ẓalamtum anfusakum*, you have wronged your own souls, the confession of 7:23 now spoken as a diagnosis. The instrument of the *ẓulm* is named: *bi-ittikhādhikumu al-ʿijl*, by your taking of the calf. No other cause is allowed to stand in front of it. Not the Sāmirī alone, not Hārūn’s inability to restrain them, not the length of the forty nights. You took it. You wronged yourselves.

*Fa-tūbū ilā bāriʾikum*, so turn to your Originator. *Al-Bārīʾ* is a Name of Allah (الله) from *b-r-ʾ*, to create, to bring into being free of a prior model, to be quit of. He is the Originator of their forms, the One from whom they must be quit of the calf. The Name is chosen, the mufassirūn have said, because they worshipped a form they had originated, and they are sent back to the Originator of their own forms. *Tawbah* is to Him, not to the statue, not to Mūsā as a person. Mūsā is the one who points.

*Fa-qtulū anfusakum*, then kill yourselves. The clause is among the hardest in the sūrah, and the *salaf* explained it with a historical command, not with a metaphor of “kill your desires” only, though that meaning is a later fruit. Ibn ʿAbbās, Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, and the mass of the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, said that those who had not worshipped the calf were commanded to execute those who had, and that the people stood in two groups, relatives killing relatives, until a great number had died, and then they were told to hold, and the dead were martyrs and the living were turned to. Some reports name seventy thousand; the numbers vary and are not the point. The point is that *tawbah* from public *shirk*, in that law, in that hour, included a killing that was an execution of the guilty by the community’s own hands — *anfusakum*, yourselves, your own, your kin. Al-Qurṭubī treats it as a prescribed punishment of that *sharīʿah*, lifted from this *ummah* in that form. This *ummah*’s *tawbah* from *shirk* is a return to *tawḥīd* without that sword between brothers; the Prophet (ﷺ) said he was forbidden to kill those who said *lā ilāha illā Allāh* except under clear legal conditions. The verse remains as Israel’s history and as a sign of how grave the calf was.

*Dhālikum khayrun lakum ʿinda bāriʾikum*, that is better for you with your Originator. Better than remaining in the *shirk*. Better than a cheap pardon that does not purge. The *khayr* is not the killing as killing. It is the *tawbah* that, in that moment, had this shape. Then the same close as 2:37: *fa-tāba ʿalaykum innahu huwa al-tawwābu al-raḥīm*. He turned to you. He is the Oft-Returning, the Merciful. The living were accepted. The command was, in the reports, lifted before the people were consumed. Mercy had the last word, as the two Names have the last word. Ibn Kathīr notes the parallel with Ādam: words received, turning granted, *al-Tawwāb al-Raḥīm*. A nation is put through a harder path than Ādam’s words, because a nation’s *shirk* is a public fire.

The Prophet (ﷺ) told of the Children of Israel in this story as a warning, and he forbade his *ummah* the calf in every form, and he made *tawbah* easy in speech and in tears and in the washing of the limbs, not in the killing of kin. “Islam wipes out what was before it, and *hijrah* wipes out what was before it, and ḥajj wipes out what was before it” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). That wiping is this *ummah*’s 2:54. Gratitude for the wiping includes not envying Israel their hardness and not pretending our calves are smaller.

Theologically the verse holds together justice and mercy without collapsing either. *Ẓulm* is named. *Tawbah* is commanded. A law is applied. Then *al-Tawwāb al-Raḥīm*. Those who see only the swords have not read the Names. Those who see only the Names have not read the swords. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that repentance from some sins, in some laws, included *ḥudūd*, and that the *ḥadd* itself can be part of the purification, as the Prophet (ﷺ) received those who came confessing and asking to be purified. 2:54 is that principle in its most severe historical form.

For the reader the verse is not a command to take a sword to a household. It is a command to take the calf as seriously as a sword, and then to take *al-Tawwāb* as seriously as the calf. You have wronged yourself by something you have taken. Turn to your Originator. Do what *tawbah* requires in the law you have been given — return the right, drop the idol, establish the prayer, pay what was due. That is better for you with your Originator. Then know that He turns. The next verses will show that even after this, some of them will ask to see Allah (الله) with their eyes, as if the calf and the swords had not been a sufficient education in the difference between the Originator and a form.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:55

> And ˹remember˺ when you said, “O Moses! We will never believe you until we see Allah with our own eyes,” so a thunderbolt struck you while you were looking on.

**Commentary:**

After a calf and a pardon, a demand of the eyes: *wa idh qultum yā Mūsā lan nuʾmina laka ḥattā narā Allāha jahratan fa-akhadhatkumu al-ṣāʿiqatu wa antum tanẓurūn*, “And when you said, ‘O Mūsā, we will never believe you until we see Allah openly,’ so the thunderbolt took you while you were looking.” *Lan nuʾmina laka*, we will not believe for you, believe you, until. The *lām* in *laka* can mean we will not believe you as a messenger, or we will not believe for your sake, until the condition is met. The condition is *ḥattā narā Allāha jahratan*, until we see Allah openly, plainly, in public view. *Jahr* is that which is not hidden, the opposite of a whisper. They want the Unseen of 2:3 to become a spectacle.

Who said this? Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, reading with 7:155 and 2:55 together, said it was the seventy whom Mūsā (peace be upon him) chose to go with him to the mountain, to apologize for the calf, to be present at the appointment. “Mūsā chose from his people seventy men for Our appointment. When the earthquake seized them, he said, ‘My Lord, if You had willed You could have destroyed them before, and me. Would You destroy us for what the foolish among us have done?’” (7:155). 4:153 lists it among Israel’s asks: “The People of the Book ask you to bring down on them a book from the heaven. They had asked Mūsā for greater than that, for they said, ‘Show us Allah openly,’ so the thunderbolt took them for their wrongdoing.” The demand is not curiosity. It is a condition placed upon faith, a making of the Lord into an object that must appear on a people’s terms. Mūsā himself had asked, in a different spirit, “My Lord, show me, that I may look at You,” and was told, “You will not see Me, but look at the mountain; if it stays in its place, you will see Me.” The mountain crumbled, and Mūsā fell down stunned, and said, “Glory to You; I turn to You, and I am the first of the believers” (7:143). The prophet asks as a lover who accepts a no. The seventy ask as a committee that will not believe without a yes.

*Fa-akhadhatkumu al-ṣāʿiqah*, so the thunderbolt took you. *Ṣāʿiqah* is the crashing strike of 2:19, the bolt that the hypocrites stuffed their fingers against. Here it is not a scare. It is a taking. Death, or a death-like stunning, as the mufassirūn said — they fell, looking. *Wa antum tanẓurūn*, while you were looking, the same clause as the drowning of 2:50. They wanted to look at Allah (الله). They were left looking at one another dying. The irony is the Lord’s, and it is a teaching: the eye that demands to see the Essence is an eye that may be closed. “Vision does not perceive Him, and He perceives all vision” (6:103). The seeing of Allah (الله) is, in the creed of Ahl al-Sunnah, a gift of the Hereafter for the believers, “faces that Day radiant, looking at their Lord” (75:22–23), and the Prophet (ﷺ) said, “You will see your Lord as you see this moon, you will not be harmed in crowding to see Him” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). It is not a gift that a committee extracts with *lan nuʾmina*.

Theologically the verse is a wall against a certain rationalism and a certain mysticism. The rationalism that will not believe until the Unseen is laboratory. The mysticism that claims to have seen, in this world, what Mūsā was told he would not see. Both are in the sentence *ḥattā narā*. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote at length, against those who claimed a seeing of the Essence in the world, that 7:143 and 2:55 close that door, and that the seeing is for the Garden. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the demand was *ẓulm*, as 4:153 says, because it placed a condition on the Lord of conditions. Faith, *īmān*, is belief in the *ghayb*. To refuse the *ghayb* until it is *jahr* is to refuse *īmān* while using its name.

The Prophet (ﷺ) was asked similar asks: “Bring us a Qur'an other than this or change it” (10:15), “Cause a spring to gush,” “Make the heaven fall,” “Bring Allah and the angels before us” (17:90–92). He did not produce the Essence as a proof. He produced the Book of 2:23, and he said, “I am only a human being and a messenger.” The thunderbolt of 2:55 is not repeated upon Quraysh in that form; the Fire of 2:24 is the later thunderbolt. The teaching is the same. Do not set terms for the Lord.

Al-Ṭabarī reports that some of the *salaf* connected this bolt to the *ṣāʿiqah* of the hypocrites’ parable in 2:19: the same sky, the same crash, two kinds of people stuffing their ears or opening their mouths to demand. The demand *jahratan* is the opposite of the Fātiḥah’s *iyyāka naʿbudu*: worship of a Lord who is unseen, not a contract with a Lord who must appear. “Those who fear their Lord unseen will have forgiveness and a great reward” (67:12). Unseen is the condition of the fear that is rewarded. 2:55’s committee wanted to cancel that condition.

For the reader the verse is a caution about the hunger to see. An age of images wants God as an image, a proof as a clip, a faith as a demonstration that can be forwarded. 2:55 says that hunger can be *ẓulm*. The humble of 2:46 are certain of a meeting they have not yet had. The seventy wanted the meeting on the mountain on their date. Certainty of the meeting is *khushūʿ*. Scheduling the Essence is a calf of another metal. The next verse will say that they were raised after this death, so that they might be grateful — a second *ʿafw*, a second chance to live as people of the *ghayb*.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:56

> Then We brought you back to life after your death, so that perhaps you would be grateful.

**Commentary:**

The thunderbolt is not the end of the seventy, or of the people addressed through them: *thumma baʿathnākum min baʿdi mawtikum laʿallakum tashkurūn*, “Then We raised you after your death, so that you might be grateful.” *Baʿathnā* is from *baʿth*, to send, to raise, to resurrect. It is the verb of the Last Day: “And that the Hour is coming, no doubt about it, and that Allah will raise those who are in the graves” (22:7). Here it is used of a raising in this world, after a death that 2:55 named. Ibn ʿAbbās and the majority of the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, took *mawt* in its ordinary sense: they died by the bolt, then they were given life again. Some said the “death” was a stunning, a loss of consciousness like Mūsā’s at the mountain (7:143). The wording is *mawt*, death, and the *baʿth* after it is a sign of the *baʿth* that 2:28 argued from the first life. A people who demanded to see, and died, and lived, have been given in their own bodies the proof they would later deny when they denied resurrection.

*Min baʿdi mawtikum*, after your death. The phrase is heavy because it makes the bolt a real end of a breath, not a metaphor of fear. Allah (الله) takes and gives in the same afternoon of a mountain. “How can you deny Allah? You were lifeless and He gave you life, then He will cause you to die and again bring you to life” (2:28). 2:56 is 2:28 as an anecdote. The sequence they were asked to believe as a species they have now tasted as a camp: death, then life. Ibn Kathīr links the two verses. Al-Qurṭubī notes that this raising does not make them prophets and does not cancel the ordinary law of dying once; it is a *āyah*, a sign, like the raising of the man killed in the story of the cow (2:73), like the people who left their homes in thousands (2:243). The Qur'an is not shy of telling that the Lord of death has reversed it when He willed, as a *niʿmah* and as a proof.

*Laʿallakum tashkurūn*, so that you might be grateful. The same purpose as 2:52. Pardon after the calf was for gratitude; raising after the bolt is for gratitude. A man who has died and lives is a man who should not find prayer *kabīrah*, who should not take another calf, who should not sell a verse for a small price. Gratitude, *shukr*, would be the life after the life: a using of the returned breath for the Originator of 2:54. Ingratitude would be to walk down the mountain and live as if the bolt had been weather. The Prophet (ﷺ) taught a *shukr* of the daily raising that every human tastes: “Praise be to Allah who gave us life after He caused us to die, and to Him is the rising” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī), the words of waking from sleep. Sleep is the brother of death; waking is a small *baʿth*. 2:56 is the large form of the morning *dhikr*.

Theologically the verse is a mercy that contains a warning. Mercy: they were not left dead in their demand. Warning: the next death will not, for the denier, be followed by a return to the world’s *mustaqarr*. “Then We raised you” is not a policy of endless rehearsals. It is a sign, *laʿalla*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that some signs are given so that the servant will not need to die in order to wake, and that the one who does not wake after such a sign is more asleep than the dead. The seventy, if the reports of their death are taken as the *salaf* took them, are a generation that cannot say “we did not know that He raises.” This *ummah* is a generation that cannot say it either, because the story is in the Book it recites.

The Prophet (ﷺ) stood at graves and wept, and he said, “Prepare for this,” and he taught that the servant is raised in the state he died upon. The raising of 2:56 was a raising back to obligation: they lived to be commanded again. The raising of the Hour is a raising to the recompense of 2:48. Between the two raisings the breath is *matāʿ ilā ḥīn*. Gratitude is the proper use of the interval.

Al-Qurṭubī discusses whether this raising is a proof used against those who deny resurrection, and he answers yes: the Lord who raised a camp on a mountain can raise the graves of the world. The deniers of Makkah said, “Who will give life to bones when they are decayed?” (36:78). 2:56 is a historical answer in another people’s body. Gratitude, then, includes believing the larger *baʿth*. A man who thanks for a returned breath and denies the returned bones has thanked for a drop and denied the sea.

Ibn Kathīr adds that the raising was also so that they would fulfill the *tawbah* they had come to the mountain to make. Death would have cut the apology. Life returned the chance to live as apologizers. *Laʿallakum tashkurūn* is therefore not only thanks for breath. It is thanks for a second chance to keep 2:40.

For the reader the verse is the morning. You were dead in sleep, or you have been near a death, or you have seen a death. You are now reading. *Laʿallakum tashkurūn*. The Children of Israel were raised on a mountain after a demand they should not have made. You have been raised on a bed after a night you did not control. The *shukr* is the same: remember Who returned the breath, and do not spend it on a calf, and do not demand to see what you have been told to believe. The next verse will list the quieter raisings of the wilderness: shade when there was no shade, food when there was no field, a table in a desert, and a sentence that they, in wasting it, wronged only themselves.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:57

> And ˹remember when˺ We shaded you with clouds and sent down to you manna and quails, ˹saying˺, “Eat from the good things We have provided for you.” The evildoers ˹certainly˺ did not wrong Us, but wronged themselves.

**Commentary:**

The wilderness is not left as a bare *mustaqarr*: *wa ẓallalnā ʿalaykumu al-ghamāma wa anzalnā ʿalaykumu al-manna wa al-salwā kulū min ṭayyibāti mā razaqnākum wa mā ẓalamūnā wa lākin kānū anfusahum yaẓlimūn*, “And We shaded you with the cloud, and We sent down upon you the manna and the quails: ‘Eat of the good things We have provided you.’ They did not wrong Us, but they were wronging themselves.” Three gifts, then a verdict. *Ẓallalnā*, We made shade. *Al-ghamām* is cloud, a covering. In a desert without roofs, shade is a *niʿmah* of the same family as the *bināʾ* of 2:22. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn said a cloud travelled with them, cooling, a portable canopy. “We shaded them with clouds and sent down upon them manna and quails” (7:160). 20:80 names the same pair as a favor after the sea: “We delivered you from your enemy, and We made an appointment with you at the right side of the mountain, and We sent down to you manna and quails.”

*Al-mann* is a food they did not plant. The reports of the *salaf*, as al-Ṭabarī gathers them, describe it as a sweet substance, like honey or like a resin, found with the dawn, a daily bread that spoiled if hoarded, so that the soul would learn *tawakkul*. *Al-salwā* is a bird, the quail, arriving in numbers, a meat they did not hunt with difficulty. The pair is a table: sweet and flesh, desert breakfast and desert supper, without a field and without a flock they owned. *Anzalnā*, We sent down — the same verb as revelation and as rain. Food, in this verse, is a sending-down. The people of 2:3 spend from what they were provided; the people of 2:57 are told to eat from the good of what they were provided. *Kulū min ṭayyibāti mā razaqnākum*. *Ṭayyib* is good, lawful, pleasant. The command is to eat, not to refuse from false asceticism, and not to demand other than the good that has arrived. 2:61 will be the demand for other: cucumbers, garlic, lentils, onions, the herbs of a settled Egypt, in place of this bread of heaven.

*Wa mā ẓalamūnā wa lākin kānū anfusahum yaẓlimūn*, they did not wrong Us, but they were wronging themselves. The Lord cannot be harmed by a people’s waste of manna. “If you disbelieve, you and whoever is on the earth together, Allah is Free of need, Praiseworthy” (14:8). *Ẓulm* of Allah (الله) is a phrase the Qur'an uses for *shirk* and for ingratitude as they return upon the servant. The calf did not injure the Throne. It injured the worshippers. The hoarding of manna, the boredom with quail, the later changing of a word at a gate, did not diminish the Provider. They wrote the loss in their own book. Ibn Kathīr notes that this sentence is a refrain of the Qur'an: “They did not wrong Us, but they were wronging themselves” (2:57, 7:160, 16:33, 30:9). It is theodicy in a clause. The Lord is not a victim of history. The servant is.

The Prophet (ﷺ) loved this verse’s ethic of food. He said, “O Allah, bless for us our dates, and bless for us our city,” and he taught, “The believer eats in one intestine, and the disbeliever eats in seven” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), a hadith of not treating provision as a god. He forbade wasting, and he praised the saying of *bismillāh* over food, which is 2:57’s *kulū* with remembrance. He said, “Look at those below you, and do not look at those above you, for it is more suitable that you not belittle Allah’s favor upon you” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Manna and quail become, in a later city, bread and a modest meat; the *ẓulm* of being bored with them remains.

Theologically the verse joins *rizq* to *ẓulm* in a way that judges consumer life. To be bored with a sending-down is a wrong against the self because it trains the self to despise *niʿmah* and to demand Egypt, the house of slaughter, as a kitchen. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the stomach is a door of *ʿubūdiyyah* or a door of *shirk*: if the food is traced to the Sender, it is worship to eat; if it is traced to the self’s cleverness or to a king’s store, it is a small calf. Israel in the wilderness was fed by a sky they did not farm. Their later demand for planted herbs (2:61) is a demand to go back to a life whose prices they knew, even if that life had included Pharaoh. Safety of the known menu can be preferred to the freedom of a desert with God. 2:57 names that preference a self-wronging.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “If the son of Ādam had a valley of gold, he would want a second, and nothing fills his mouth but the dust, and Allah turns to whoever turns” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Manna that falls every dawn is a valley of gold in desert form. Boredom with it is the second valley. 2:61 will make that boredom speech. 2:57 already names it *ẓulm* of the self. The cloud above them was a mercy they did not have in Egypt’s sun of bricks; to despise the menu of freedom is to love the menu of slavery.

For the reader the cloud, the manna, and the quail are every unearned covering and food: a roof, a wage, a fridge. *Kulū min ṭayyibāt mā razaqnākum*. Eat. Give thanks. Do not wrong yourselves by waste, by forbidden bites, by boredom that insults the Sender, by a politics of appetite that would return to Pharaoh for garlic. The next verse will offer a city, a gate, a word of humility, a forgiveness, and an increase — the settled life they will soon ask for — on the condition that they enter as those who bow and say “put down from us our burden.” Some of them will change the word.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:58

> And ˹remember˺ when We said, “Enter this city and eat freely from wherever you please; enter the gate with humility, saying, ‘Absolve us.’ We will forgive your sins and multiply the reward for the good-doers.”

**Commentary:**

The desert table is followed by a city: *wa idh qulnā udkhulū hādhihi al-qaryata fa-kulū minhā ḥaythu shiʾtum raghadan wa udkhulū al-bāba sujjadan wa qūlū ḥiṭṭatun naghfir lakum khaṭāyākum wa sanazīdu al-muḥsinīn*, “And when We said, ‘Enter this town and eat from it wherever you will, in plenty, and enter the gate bowing, and say: a putting-down. We will forgive you your faults, and We will increase the good-doers.’” *Al-qaryah* is a town. Which town? Ibn ʿAbbās and many of the *salaf* said Bayt al-Maqdis, Jerusalem, or the nearer town of the Holy Land, sometimes named as Jericho, *Arīḥā*, the first of the land they were to enter. Al-Ṭabarī lists the names and then, as with the tree of 2:35, notes that the identification is less important than the command. A people who had been under Pharaoh and under cloud are told to enter a settlement, to eat *raghadan* — the same plenty as Ādam’s Garden in 2:35 — wherever they wish. The earth of 2:29, created for them, is now a city of food.

The condition is two acts at the gate. *Udkhulū al-bāba sujjadan*, enter the gate prostrating, bowing, in a posture of humility. *Sujjad* is the plural of a state of *sujūd*. The mufassirūn said they were to bow, to enter bent, not strutting as conquerors. A people who have seen a sea split can enter a town as if they had split it. They are told to enter as servants. *Wa qūlū ḥiṭṭah*, and say *ḥiṭṭah*. The word is from *ḥ-ṭ-ṭ*, to put down, to unload. “Put down from us our sins,” a request of *ḥaṭṭ*, of unburdening. Ibn ʿAbbās said it means: put down from us our faults, forgive. It is a *duʿāʾ* in a single noun, as *āmīn* is a *duʿāʾ* in a single word. The gate is therefore a small ḥajj: a body lowered, a word of seeking pardon. The Prophet (ﷺ) entered Makkah, at the conquest, with his head so lowered that it almost touched the saddle, in the reports of the *sīrah*. That is *sujjad* at a gate. Israel is commanded the same spirit at theirs.

The reward is double. *Naghfir lakum khaṭāyākum*, We will forgive you your faults — the calf, the demand to see, the smaller *ẓulm* of the wilderness. *Wa sanazīdu al-muḥsinīn*, and We will increase the doers of good. *Iḥsān* is excellence, to worship Allah (الله) as though you see Him, as Jibrīl’s hadith defined it (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Forgiveness is for the entering with the word; increase is for those whose entering is *iḥsān*. The Lord does not pay all at one rate. “To all We give, these and those, from the gift of your Lord, and the gift of your Lord is not restricted” (17:20) — yet the *mazīd*, the increase, is for *muḥsinīn*. Ibn Kathīr notes the generosity of the offer: a town, a table like Ādam’s, a gate, a word, and in return a wiping and an increase. 7:161 is the sister verse, almost word for word, so that the story is told twice in the Qur'an, as a thing not to be missed.

Theologically the verse is the law of openings. Allah (الله) opens a city as He opens a Garden, with a condition of humility. Pride at a gate is the sin of Iblīs at a prostration. The Children of Israel are not being asked to refuse the town. They are being asked to refuse the strut. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of Andalusia, heard in *udkhulū* the ethics of entering any blessing: a post, a house, a marriage, a victory. Enter *sujjad*. Say *ḥiṭṭah*. Do not say “this is from my knowledge” as Qārūn said (28:78). The increase is for those who do not.

The Prophet (ﷺ) taught a *ḥiṭṭah* for this *ummah* in many words: *astaghfiru Allāh*, *rabbighfir lī*, the *sayyid al-istighfār*. He said, “By Allah, I seek forgiveness of Allah and turn to Him more than seventy times a day” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). He said that a servant who commits a sin, then performs *wuḍūʾ*, then prays, then asks forgiveness, is forgiven, and he recited 3:135 (Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī). The gate of 2:58 is closed as a geographic command. It is open as a method: lower the body, say the word of putting-down, receive *maghfirah* and, if you are of the *muḥsinīn*, more.

Al-Ṭabarī records that some said the command to enter was given while they were still in the wilderness, a future opening, and that the changing of the word in 2:59 happened when they reached the gate. Whether the town was entered that generation or delayed by the forty years of 5:26, the command stands as a *niʿmah* offered. A blessing can be offered and then postponed by a people’s *fisq*, as the Holy Land was postponed. The verse still teaches the manner of every later entering. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *ḥiṭṭah* is the secret of every gate of *tawbah*: you do not enter a purity carrying the old load as a boast. You ask that it be put down.

For the reader the verse is how to go in. You are always entering something — a day, a job, a feed, a city. The pagan way is to enter as Pharaoh would enter. The way of this āyah is to enter as a servant who has a calf in his past and a cloud in his past and a sea in his past, and who does not trust himself to walk upright through a gate without a word of *ḥiṭṭah*. The next verse will say that some of them changed the word. The offer was not refused by Allah (الله). It was edited by them. Editing a *duʿāʾ* of humility into a joke is the *talbīs* of 2:42 at a gate.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:59

> But the wrongdoers changed the words they were commanded to say. So We sent down a punishment from the heavens upon them for their rebelliousness.

**Commentary:**

The offer of the gate is met with an edit: *fa-baddala alladhīna ẓalamū qawlan ghayra alladhī qīla lahum fa-anzalnā ʿalā alladhīna ẓalamū rijzan mina al-samāʾi bimā kānū yafsuqūn*, “Then those who wronged changed the word to other than what was said to them, so We sent down upon those who wronged a punishment from the heaven because they were defiantly disobedient.” *Baddala* is to substitute, to replace. The object is *qawlan ghayra alladhī qīla lahum*, a saying other than the saying that was said to them. They were told to say *ḥiṭṭah*. Abū Hurayrah (may Allah be pleased with him) reported that the Prophet (ﷺ) said they said *ḥabbah fī shaʿrah*, a grain in barley, or, in the wording of Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, they entered dragging themselves on their buttocks and said *ḥinṭah*, wheat, instead of *ḥiṭṭah*. A mockery of a letter. *Ḥiṭṭah* and *ḥinṭah* are close in sound and opposite in spirit: put-down-our-sins versus a grocery. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī records, said they twisted the word in mockery. The body was also twisted: they were told to enter *sujjad*, and they entered sliding, as a joke of the command to bow.

*Alladhīna ẓalamū*, those who wronged — not the whole camp, the mufassirūn have noted. The verse does not say “you changed,” as 2:51 said “you took the calf.” It specifies the wrongdoers. Among a people there is a party that makes the joke, and a party that does not. 7:162 is the sister: “Those among them who wronged changed the word.” Justice in the telling is part of the *furqān*. The punishment, however, is named as sent down upon those who wronged: *fa-anzalnā ʿalā alladhīna ẓalamū rijzan mina al-samāʾ*. *Rijz* is a torment, a plague, a filth of punishment. The reports of the *salaf* include a plague, a death, a *ṭāʿūn*. The Prophet (ﷺ) said of plague that it is a *rijz* sent upon a people among the Children of Israel, or upon those before you (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). *Mina al-samāʾ*, from the heaven — the same sky that sent manna. The sky is not a vending machine of sweets. It is a *furqān*. What comes from it depends on what rises to it of *qawl*.

*Bimā kānū yafsuqūn*, because they used to *fasaqa*, to go out, the *fisq* of 2:26–27. The *kānū* plus imperfect is habit. The changed word was not a slip of dialect. It was a practice of exiting from the command. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as a warning against *taḥrīf* of speech: to take a revealed word and replace it with a near word that empties it. That is the *talbīs* of 2:42 done as a pun. Al-Qurṭubī, as a linguist of law, hears the danger of legal tricks that keep the sound of a ruling and lose its body. *Ḥiṭṭah* to *ḥinṭah* is the mother of such tricks.

Theologically the verse is the gravity of a joke at a gate. They did not, in the Bukhārī report, write a new Torah. They changed one word of a *duʿāʾ* and the manner of a step. The *rijz* came. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that mockery of a command is heavier than a failure to fulfill it from weakness, because mockery is a *kufr* of the command’s worth. 9:65–66 treated those who mocked as having disbelieved after their faith. 2:59 is that gravity in Israel’s story. The Lord who pardoned a calf (2:52) after a slaughter of *tawbah* (2:54) sent a *rijz* for a pun. The calf was a disaster they could be turned from. The pun was a *fisq* of the tongue that made the gate itself a stage.

The Prophet (ﷺ) guarded the words of this *ummah*’s gates. He taught the exact phrases of the prayer, the exact *tashahhud*, the exact *talbiyah* of ḥajj, and he said, “Pray as you have seen me pray.” He forbade the lying upon him that would be a *tabdīl* of his *qawl*. He said, “Whoever tells a lie upon me deliberately, let him take his seat in the Fire” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The *ḥiṭṭah* of this *ummah* is not to be edited into a *ḥinṭah* of our own — a slogan, a brand, a more comfortable syllable.

Al-Rāghib noted that *tabdīl* is a changing of the substance, not a translation. A people may need to say *ḥiṭṭah* in another tongue if the meaning is kept. What they did was to keep a sound and kill a meaning, or to kill a sound in order to kill a meaning. Either is *tabdīl*. The Qur'an itself is a *furqān* against such work: “Those who distort words from their places” (5:13). 2:59 is that distortion as a joke at a door, which is worse than a distortion in a law-book, because it adds *istihzāʾ* to *taḥrīf*.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “A servant may say a word that wins Allah’s pleasure, not giving it much weight, and by it Allah raises him degrees; and a servant may say a word that wins Allah’s wrath, not giving it much weight, and by it he falls into the Fire” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). *Ḥinṭah* in place of *ḥiṭṭah* is a word not given much weight. The *rijz* teaches the weight.

For the reader the verse is about how you handle a command that sounds small. Enter bowing. Say this word. The smallness is a test, like the mosquito of 2:26. Those who ask “what does Allah mean by such a word?” and then replace it with a funnier one are the wrongdoers of 2:59. Keep the word. Keep the posture. The heaven that sends manna also sends *rijz*. The difference is *fisq* or *shukr*. The next verse will close this batch of favors with water from a rock, twelve springs for twelve tribes, a provision that does not require a pun, and a last command of the wilderness: eat, drink, and do not go about the earth spreading corruption.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:60

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses prayed for water for his people, We said, “Strike the rock with your staff.” Then twelve springs gushed out, ˹and˺ each tribe knew its drinking place. ˹We then said,˺ “Eat and drink of Allah’s provisions, and do not go about spreading corruption in the land.”

**Commentary:**

The last favor of this twenty-verse run is water from stone: *wa idhi istasqā Mūsā li-qawmihi fa-qulnā iḍrib bi-ʿaṣāka al-ḥajara fa-infajarat minhu ithnatā ʿashrata ʿaynan qad ʿalima kullu unāsin mashrabahum kulū wa ishrabū min rizqi Allāhi wa lā taʿthaw fī al-arḍi mufsidīn*, “And when Mūsā asked for water for his people, We said, ‘Strike the rock with your staff,’ so there gushed from it twelve springs. Each people knew their drinking-place. ‘Eat and drink of the provision of Allah, and do not go about in the earth spreading corruption.’” *Istasqā* is to seek *saqy*, to ask for drink, the prayer for rain that this *ummah* still prays as *istisqāʾ*. Mūsā (peace be upon him) does not strike of his own invention. He asks, and then he is told to strike. The staff that parted the sea (26:63) and that became a serpent in a court (7:107) is now a staff upon a rock. The same stick, three offices: against a lie, against a sea, against a stone. The power is never the stick. “We said, strike.”

*Fa-infajarat minhu ithnatā ʿashrata ʿaynan*, so there burst forth from it twelve springs. *Infijār* is a splitting open, a gushing. Twelve, for the twelve tribes, the sons of Yaʿqūb (peace be upon him), the *asbāṭ*. 7:160 tells it with the same number: “We divided them into twelve tribes, nations, and We revealed to Mūsā when his people asked him for water, ‘Strike the rock with your staff,’ and there gushed from it twelve springs. Each people knew their drinking-place.” *Qad ʿalima kullu unāsin mashrabahum*, each people had known, knew, their drinking-place. The knowledge is a mercy against crowding and against the *fasād* of a fight at a well. A nation that had been one camp of thirst is made, in the moment of water, a nation of known shares. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn treated this as a *niʿmah* of order as well as of drink. Allah (الله) does not only send water. He sends a *mashrab*, a place of drinking, a portion.

*Kulū wa ishrabū min rizqi Allāh*, eat and drink of the provision of Allah. The manna, the quail, and now the water are gathered under one *rizq*, ascribed to Allah (الله) by name. The command is permission and is gratitude: eat, drink, and know the Source. It is the *kulū* of 2:57 extended to the cup. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Eat, drink, give charity, and dress, without extravagance and without pride” (al-Nasāʾī, Ibn Mājah), and he forbade the wasting of water even at a running river, in the well-known report of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar. Twelve springs do not cancel the ethics of a sip.

*Wa lā taʿthaw fī al-arḍi mufsidīn*, and do not act wickedly in the earth, spreading corruption. *ʿAthā* is to go about doing harm, a verb of reckless evil. *Mufsidīn* is the ḥāl, the state: as corruptors. The sūrah returns to the word of 2:11, 2:12, 2:27. A people who have water from a rock, food from a sky, shade from a cloud, a sea that stood, a pardon after a calf, are told the same thing the hypocrites were told: do not spoil the land. Provision is not a license. It is a trust. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *fasād* after *rizq* is uglier than *fasād* in hunger, because it is ingratitude in action. Ibn Kathīr links the clause to their later demands and rebellions in the wilderness: the water is given, and the *fasād* is forbidden in the same breath, because a full stomach can be a Pharaoh of its own.

Theologically the verse is a picture of *nubuwwah* and of *ummah*. The prophet asks; the Lord commands a strike; the water knows its tribes. Leadership is *istisqāʾ*, not the claim of the river that was Pharaoh’s. Community is known drinking-places, not a crush. Law is eat, drink, do not spoil. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that every *rizq* has a *ḥukm*, a ruling that rides with it, and that 2:60 is the ruling riding with the water. The staff is a sign that means are required — strike — and that means do not suffice — We said. The servant works; the gushing is from Allah (الله).

The Prophet (ﷺ) was himself an *istasqā* for his people. He raised his hands for rain until the sweat of his armpits was seen, and he asked for rain in the Friday sermon when a man complained of drought, and it rained for a week, and then he asked for it to be around and not upon, and the clouds moved (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He struck no rock. He was given a different staff: *duʿāʾ*, and a Book. This *ummah*’s twelve springs are the *sharīʿah*’s shares: known rights, known limits, a *mashrab* for each, so that the strong do not drink the weak’s place. Zakāt is a known drinking-place of the poor. Inheritance is a known drinking-place of heirs. The prohibition of *fasād* is the same prohibition.

For the reader the verse closes 2:41–60 as 2:21–22 opened the human address: provision, then a limit. You have eaten and drunk of a *rizq* you did not gush from a rock with your own staff. Someone asked for you — a parent, a prophet, a history — and the water came. Know your *mashrab*. Do not take another’s. Do not go about the earth as a corruptor while your mouth is still wet from a spring you did not deserve. The Children of Israel will, in the next verses, ask for the herbs of a town and kill prophets and be struck with disgrace. The spring of 2:60 will still be in the Book, as a *niʿmah* that was given, and as a command that was not always kept. Remember. Eat. Drink. Do not spoil. That is the wilderness’s last sentence before the sūrah turns to their boredom with the menu of heaven.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:61

> And ˹remember˺ when you said, “O Moses! We cannot endure the same meal ˹every day˺. So ˹just˺ call upon your Lord on our behalf, He will bring forth for us some of what the earth produces of herbs, cucumbers, garlic, lentils, and onions.” Moses scolded ˹them˺, “Do you exchange what is better for what is worse? ˹You can˺ go down to any village and you will find what you have asked for.” They were stricken with disgrace and misery, and they invited the displeasure of Allah for rejecting Allah’s signs and unjustly killing the prophets. This is ˹a fair reward˺ for their disobedience and violations.

**Commentary:**

The wilderness table of 2:57 is now refused with a menu: *wa idh qultum yā Mūsā lan naṣbira ʿalā ṭaʿāmin wāḥidin fa-dʿu lanā rabbaka yukhrij lanā mimmā tunbitu al-arḍu min baqlihā wa qiththāʾihā wa fūmihā wa ʿadasihā wa baṣalihā*, “And when you said, ‘O Mūsā, we will not endure one food, so call your Lord for us to bring out for us of what the earth grows of its herbs, its cucumbers, its garlic, its lentils, and its onions.’” *Lan naṣbira* is the language of 2:45’s *ṣabr* refused. They will not be patient upon *ṭaʿām wāḥid*, one food — the manna and the quail that had been sent down, which they flatten into “one” because the soul that is bored no longer counts. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, said they tired of the sweet and the bird and remembered the onions of Egypt. The list is a peasant’s market of the Nile: greens, cucumber, garlic or wheat (*fūm* has been read both ways), lentils, onion. None of it is forbidden. All of it is *adna*, lower, as Mūsā (peace be upon him) will say. The crime is not vegetables. It is the exchange.

*Qāla a-tastabdilūna alladhī huwa adnā billadhī huwa khayr*, he said, “Do you replace that which is lower for that which is better?” *Istibdāl* is the trade of 2:16 in a kitchen. *Adnā* is nearer, lower, cheaper, more worldly. *Khayr* is better: food without toil, from a sky, a *niʿmah* that taught *tawakkul*. They want the lower because it is the food of a settled humiliation they knew, the taste of a life that included Pharaoh. *Ihbiṭū miṣran fa-inna lakum mā saʾaltum*, go down to a town, a city, Egypt even, as some read *miṣr*, and you will have what you asked. The prophet does not refuse them herbs. He refuses the ranking. If you want the market of a town, go down to a town. Do not stand in a miracle and order it to become a grocery. Al-Qurṭubī notes the sting of *ihbiṭū*: the same verb as the descent from the Garden (2:36). To ask for Egypt’s onions in a desert of manna is a small falling.

Then the verse leaps from a kitchen to a history of blood: *wa ḍuribat ʿalayhimu al-dhillatu wa al-maskanatu wa bāʾū bi-ghaḍabin mina Allāh*, and humiliation and misery were struck upon them, and they returned with wrath from Allah. *Ḍuribat* is to be struck, stamped, as a tax is struck, as a tent-peg is struck. *Dhillah* is humiliation, *maskanah* is misery, the clinging poverty of the soul as well as of the hand. 3:112 repeats the sentence almost whole: “Humiliation was struck upon them wherever they were found, except for a rope from Allah and a rope from the people, and they incurred wrath from Allah, and misery was struck upon them, that is because they used to disbelieve in the signs of Allah and kill the prophets without right.” 2:61 is the first striking of that stamp. The kitchen complaint is not, by itself, the whole cause. The verse names the causes in the next breath: *dhālika bi-annahum kānū yakfurūna bi-āyāti Allāhi wa yaqtulūna al-nabiyyīna bi-ghayri al-ḥaqq*, that is because they used to cover the signs of Allah and kill the prophets without right.

The killing of prophets is a charge the Qur'an repeats (2:87, 2:91, 3:21, 3:181, 4:155, 5:70). Yaḥyā (peace be upon him) is of those killed; others of the Children of Israel’s prophets were killed by their own people, as the Prophet (ﷺ) said in the hadith of the man who will be the first to be kindled in the Fire, and as he said, “The most severely tried of people are the prophets, then the like and the like” (al-Tirmidhī). *Bi-ghayri al-ḥaqq* is without right: there is no right by which a prophet is killed. The phrase is not a suggestion that some killings of prophets were just. It is a sealing of the injustice. *Dhālika bimā ʿaṣaw wa kānū yaʿtadūn*, that is for their disobedience and their transgression. *ʿIṣyān* is refusal of the command; *iʿtidāʾ* is going past the limit. The onions were a small *iʿtidāʾ*; the blood of prophets is the large. The verse joins them so that a people will not think the small is unrelated to the large. A soul that cannot endure one food is a soul that may not endure a prophet who forbids a calf.

The Prophet (ﷺ) lived the opposite of *lan naṣbira ʿalā ṭaʿāmin wāḥid*. He ate what was present, he did not find fault with a food, he never criticized a dish, as Abū Hurayrah reported (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). He said, “Look at those below you” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He tied his stone upon his belly from hunger, and he praised the manna of Israel as a *niʿmah* in the telling of their story. This *ummah* was taught to say *al-ḥamdu lillāh* over a crust, not to send a prophet back to the mountain for garlic.

Theologically the verse is a warning that *shukr* of 2:52 and 2:56 can fail in the mouth. A people raised from death, fed from the sky, can still ask to go down. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the soul’s love of variety is not always a love of *ṭayyib*; it can be a love of the known lower thing, the Egypt inside the chest. *Istibdāl al-adnā bil-khayr* is a formula that fits more than food: exchanging the prayer for a screen, the *ghayb* for a spectacle, the Prophet (ﷺ) for a custom. 3:187 will say they threw the Book behind their backs and sold it for a small price. 2:61 is that sale in a cooking-pot.

For the reader the verse is a mirror at a table. What food, what life, what Egypt are you asking a prophet to call his Lord for, while manna is still on the plate? Disgrace and misery, in this world, have many forms; wrath is one. The next verse will open a door that 2:61’s stamp must not be misread as slamming upon every Jew, Christian, or Sabian who believed in his time: whoever believes in Allah (الله) and the Last Day and does good will have his reward, and will not fear or grieve.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:62

> Indeed, the believers, Jews, Christians, and Sabians—whoever ˹truly˺ believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good will have their reward with their Lord. And there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve.

**Commentary:**

After a stamp of wrath, a door of justice: *inna alladhīna āmanū wa alladhīna hādū wa al-naṣārā wa al-ṣābiʾīna man āmana billāhi wa al-yawmi al-ākhiri wa ʿamila ṣāliḥan fa-lahum ajruhum ʿinda rabbihim wa lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa lā hum yaḥzanūn*, “Indeed, those who believe, and those who are Jews, and the Christians, and the Sabians — whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good — they will have their reward with their Lord, and no fear will be upon them, nor will they grieve.” The verse has been pulled, in every age, toward two ditches. One ditch makes 2:61 a curse of blood that no Jew can exit. The other makes 2:62 a charter of remaining upon any religion after the coming of the last Messenger (ﷺ). The mufassirūn, from Ibn ʿAbbās through al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr, refuse both.

*Alladhīna āmanū* here, in the first clause, are the believers of this *ummah*, or those who claimed faith with the Prophet (ﷺ). *Alladhīna hādū* are those who became Jews, from *hāda*, to turn, to be of Hūdā / Yahūdhā. *Al-naṣārā* are the Christians, the people of Naṣrah, named in the Qur'an from the helpers of ʿĪsā (peace be upon him) or from the town of Nazareth, according to the two etymologies. *Al-ṣābiʾūn* are a people the Arabs knew as neither this nor that; the *salaf* differed: a sect between Jews and Christians; a people who worshipped angels or stars and claimed a monotheism; the baptizers of the marshes of Iraq. Al-Ṭabarī prefers that they are a religion who were not of the two Books in the known way, and that the verse includes them to widen the justice, not to define a liturgy.

Then the verse does not stop at the names of communities. It conditions: *man āmana billāhi wa al-yawmi al-ākhiri wa ʿamila ṣāliḥan*, whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day and works righteousness. *Man* is a replacement, a *badal*, that takes the four names and filters them. The reward is not for a label. It is for a faith and a work. What faith? Belief in Allah (الله) as He is to be believed in that person’s time, including — after the sending of Muḥammad (ﷺ) — belief in him. Ibn ʿAbbās, in a report Ibn Kathīr cites, said the verse is about those of the Jews, Christians, and Sabians who believed in their prophets and then believed in the Prophet (ﷺ). Salmān al-Fārisī (may Allah be pleased with him) asked about the Christians with whom he had kept company before Islam, who had described a coming prophet; this verse, in some reports of asbāb, answered his grief with the news that those who died upon true faith in their prophet, before the news of Muḥammad (ﷺ) reached them in a way that established the proof, are not of the Fire. “We do not punish until We have sent a messenger” (17:15).

The verse does not, in the reading of Ahl al-Sunnah, mean that a person may hear of the Prophet (ﷺ), refuse him, remain a Jew or a Christian or a Sabian, and have 2:38’s *lā khawfun ʿalayhim*. “Whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will never be accepted from him, and in the Hereafter he will be among the losers” (3:85). “The religion with Allah is Islam” (3:19). 5:69 repeats 2:62 almost exactly, and the mufassirūn read it with the same filter. Al-Qurṭubī writes that *īmān* in Allah (الله) and the Last Day, after the coming of the last Book, includes the last Book. To split the Lord from His last Messenger is the *talbīs* of 2:42. Ibn Taymiyyah, in his discussions of the fate of those who have not received the message, distinguished the one upon whom the proof has not been established from the one who has received it and preferred a community-name. 2:62 is mercy to the first and a condition upon all.

*Fa-lahum ajruhum ʿinda rabbihim*, they have their reward with their Lord. The *ʿinda* is a with-ness of storage: the wage is not left to a church or a tribe to pay. *Wa lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa lā hum yaḥzanūn* is the refrain of 2:38, the promise to whoever follows the guidance that comes from Him. The sūrah has now said it of the followers of Ādam’s charter and of the filtered names of the later communities. The stamp of 2:61 remains for those who killed prophets and covered signs. The door remains for those who believed and did good. Justice is this pair, not a flattening of either verse.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “By Him in whose hand is the soul of Muḥammad, no one of this community, Jew or Christian, hears of me, then dies without believing in what I was sent with, except that he will be of the people of the Fire” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). That hadith is 2:62’s condition made explicit for those who hear. He also said that his *ummah* is the best, and that half of the people of Paradise, in one wording, will be from it, which is *faḍl*, not a closing of 2:62 upon Salmān’s old companions who never heard. The two statements live together as the mufassirūn made them live.

For the reader the verse is a forbidding of two arrogances. Do not look at a people named in 2:61 and say that Allah’s (الله) mercy has a racial end. Do not look at 2:62 and say that the sending of the Prophet (ﷺ) was an optional extra for people who already had a label. Believe in Allah (الله), in the Last Day, and do good — and, after this Book, that belief includes this Book. The next verses will return to Israel’s mountain, a covenant, a lifted Ṭūr, a holding of the Book, and a turning away, so that the condition of 2:62 is seen in a people who had the condition in their hands and did not hold it.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:63

> And ˹remember˺ when We took a covenant from you and raised the mountain above you ˹saying˺, “Hold firmly to that ˹Scripture˺ which We have given you and observe its teachings so perhaps you will become mindful ˹of Allah˺.”

**Commentary:**

The covenant of 2:40 is now given a scene: *wa idh akhadhnā mīthāqakum wa rafaʿnā fawqakumu al-ṭūra khudhū mā ātaynākum bi-quwwatin wa udhkurū mā fīhi laʿallakum tattaqūn*, “And when We took your covenant and We raised above you the mountain: ‘Take what We have given you with strength, and remember what is in it, so that you may be mindful.’” *Mīthāq* is the firm pact of 2:27, the rope already knotted. *Rafaʿnā fawqakumu al-ṭūr*, We raised above you the Ṭūr. Ṭūr is the mountain of Mūsā (peace be upon him), Sinai, the mount of the appointment. 7:171 tells the same moment with a simile: “When We lifted the mountain above them as if it were a canopy, and they thought it would fall upon them: ‘Take what We have given you with strength, and remember what is in it, so that you may be mindful.’” The mountain is not a metaphor in the reading of the *salaf*. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī records, said Allah (الله) uprooted the mountain and raised it over them like a cloud, and they were told to take the Book or it would fall. They prostrated, looking with one eye at the mountain and with one eye at the Book, and the Jews’ later prostration, in some reports, still shows a tilt from that hour. Whether every detail of the later custom is sound, the Qur'an is sound: a mountain was above them, and a sentence was said.

*Khudhū mā ātaynākum bi-quwwah*, take what We have given you with strength. *Quwwah* is force, energy, a taking that is not lazy. The Torah is not to be received as a decoration or as a recitation of 2:44 that does not enter the self. It is to be taken as a man takes a load he intends to carry. “Take it with strength” is said also to Yaḥyā (peace be upon him): “O Yaḥyā, take the Book with strength” (19:12). Strength here is *ʿazm*, resolve, the opposite of the *lan naṣbira* of 2:61. Ibn Kathīr notes that *mā ātaynākum* is the Torah with its commands and its burdens, including, for that generation, the heavy *tawbah* of 2:54 and the laws of the Sabbath that 2:65 will mention. A Book taken weakly is a Book already on the way to *tabdīl*.

*Wa udhkurū mā fīhi*, and remember what is in it. *Dhikr* is memory, recitation, presence. What is in it includes the description of the unlettered prophet (7:157), the command of *tawḥīd*, the stories of the fathers, the ḥudūd. Remembering what is in it is the opposite of 2:42’s hiding and of 2:78’s knowing nothing but wishes. *Laʿallakum tattaqūn*, so that you may become mindful — the purpose of 2:21, of 2:2, now attached to a mountain and a Book. *Taqwā* is not a feeling that descends without a taking. It is the fruit of a Book taken with strength and remembered.

Theologically the verse is the pedagogy of a Lord who does not only send a Book but surrounds the sending with a sign that the sending is not optional. Some of the later theologians disliked the image of a mountain as a threat, as if *īmān* under a lifted Ṭūr were compulsion. Ahl al-Sunnah answer that the signs of the prophets include the staff, the sea, the white hand, and the mountain, and that compulsion of the heart is not the same as a sign that removes excuse. They still had to *take*. The mountain did not take for them. Al-Qurṭubī writes that the *mīthāq* is binding whether a mountain is in the sky or not; the mountain is a mercy of emphasis for a people who had just made a calf. Ibn al-Qayyim said that every community is under a mountain of a kind: the proof, once established, hangs over the one who received it. This *ummah*’s Ṭūr is a Qur'an that does not need to be lifted as rock because it has been lifted as speech: “We will cast upon you a weighty word” (73:5).

The Prophet (ﷺ) received his Book without a mountain over Makkah, and the weight was still weight. He said, “I have been given Jawāmiʿ al-kalim,” and he stood under the weight until his hair showed white from Sūrah Hūd and its sisters. He commanded this *ummah* to take the Qur'an with strength: “The likeness of the one who recites the Qur'an and is skilled in it is with the noble scribes; the one who recites it with difficulty, stammering, has two rewards” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Taking with strength is not only a scholar’s fluency. It is a lame tongue that still carries.

Al-Rāzī observed that a covenant taken under a mountain is still a covenant of the heart: they said we hear, and the body bowed, and the later turning of 2:64 showed that a mountain can hang over a person who has already decided not to carry. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The likeness of the one who recites the Qur'an is that of a citron, whose scent is good and whose taste is good,” and of the believer who does not recite, a date, taste without scent (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Taking with strength is the citron. A mountain without a taking is a scent without a fruit.

For the reader the verse is a question about how the Book sits in the house. Is it taken with *quwwah*, or is it a mountain on a shelf, raised above you in the sense of being unused and hanging? Remember what is in it. The Children of Israel were given a scene that should have made forgetting impossible. The next verse will say they turned away afterward, and that only grace and mercy kept them from being of the losers — a sentence this *ummah* should read as if its own name were in it, because 2:64’s turning is a human possibility, not a tribal monopoly.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:64

> Yet you turned away afterwards. Had it not been for Allah’s grace and mercy upon you, you would have certainly been of the losers.

**Commentary:**

The mountain is still in the sky of the last verse when the next verse records a turning: *thumma tawallaytum min baʿdi dhālika fa-law lā faḍlu Allāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuhu la-kuntum mina al-khāsirīn*, “Then you turned away after that. If not for the favor of Allah upon you and His mercy, you would have been of the losers.” *Thumma* is then, after the *mīthāq*, after the Ṭūr, after *khudhū bi-quwwah*. *Tawallaytum* is from *tawallī*, to turn the back, to walk away. The body that had one eye on the mountain turns. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn treated the turning as the return to disobedience: the calf had already happened; after the mountain came the Sabbath-breaking of 2:65, the disputing of the cow of 2:67, the distorting of 2:75, the writing of 2:79. *Min baʿdi dhālika*, after that — the same phrase as 2:52’s pardon after the calf. There, after a crime, pardon. Here, after a covenant, turning. The human being is capable of both sequences in one lifetime.

*Fa-law lā faḍlu Allāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuhu*, if not for Allah’s favor upon you and His mercy. *Law lā* is the “were it not for” that holds a people over an abyss. *Faḍl* is the surplus of 2:47, the unearned extra. *Raḥmah* is the mercy of the Fātiḥah, *al-Raḥmān al-Raḥīm*. The two together are the only reason the turning did not become the final loss. What did the *faḍl* and *raḥmah* look like? The sending of prophets after the turning; the not-erasing of the tribes; the Qur'an now addressing their children in Madinah with 2:40 still open; the presence, among them, of those who would believe, like ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām. Al-Qurṭubī writes that every remaining of a sinful people on the earth, with the door of *tawbah* still unclosed, is this *faḍl*. Ibn Kathīr notes that the verse is a *tawbīkh* that contains a *tadhkīr* of grace: you should have been *khāsirīn*; you are not, yet; therefore return.

*La-kuntum mina al-khāsirīn*, you would surely have been of the losers. *Khusrān* is the loss of 2:27, the commercial ruin of 2:16. The *lām* in *la-kuntum* is the *lām* of the oath’s answer, a confirmation. Without *faḍl* and *raḥmah*, the turning after a lifted mountain is a loss that does not recover. Theologically the verse is the middle of Ahl al-Sunnah between two claims. The claim that a covenant once seen cannot be left: they left it. The claim that leaving it is a fate Allah (الله) has no mercy for: were it not for His mercy, yes; with His mercy, the losers’ name is not yet stuck. “If not for the favor of Allah upon you and His mercy, you would have followed Satan, except a few” (4:83). The formula is a habit of the Qur'an. This *ummah* is told it in 24:10, 24:14, 24:20, 24:21, after the slander of ʿĀʾishah (may Allah be pleased with her): if not for *faḍl* and *raḥmah*, a people would have been ruined by a lie. 2:64 is Israel’s version of that formula.

The Prophet (ﷺ) lived as a man who named *faḍl* and *raḥmah* every day. “None of you will enter Paradise by his deeds.” They said, “Not even you, O Messenger of Allah?” He said, “Not even I, unless Allah covers me with His mercy” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). That hadith is 2:64 for the best of creation (ﷺ). If he is not independent of *raḥmah*, the people who turned after a mountain are not. He taught the *istighfār* that is the servant’s answer to 2:64, and he said Allah (الله) extends His hand by night and by day (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The losers are those who turn and then refuse the *faḍl* that followed the turning.

Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *tawallī* after a sign is a worse *tawallī* than a turning of one who never saw. The mountain makes the turning heavier, and the *faḍl* that still follows makes the turning more astonishing as a mercy. A Lord who lifts Ṭūr and then, after the back is turned, still sends prophets and a last Book, is a Lord whose *raḥmah* has not been deduced from human patience. “Your Lord has prescribed upon Himself mercy” (6:54). 2:64 is that prescription as a nation’s biography.

Al-Ṭabarī records that some of the *salaf* read the *faḍl* as the sending of Muḥammad (ﷺ) himself, a mercy upon the Children of Israel in Madinah as much as upon the Arabs: a last chance to fulfill 2:41. *Raḥmah* is the not-hastening of the punishment of 2:65 upon all of them. A town was made apes; a nation was not. That difference is this verse. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Were it not that I would cause hardship for my *ummah*, I would have commanded them to use the *siwāk* at every prayer” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He lived *faḍl* as a lightening. 2:64 is *faḍl* as a not-destroying.

Ibn Kathīr links the *tawallī* of this verse to later *tawallī* from the covenant of Madinah: they turned from the mountain, then from Mūsā, then from Muḥammad (ﷺ). One root, many stations. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “All of my *ummah* will enter Paradise except those who refuse.” They said, “Who would refuse?” He said, “Whoever obeys me enters Paradise, and whoever disobeys me has refused” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:64 is a people who refused after a mountain, and were not destroyed because *faḍl* still covered them. That covering is not a license. It is a last rope.

For the reader the verse is the honest history of a religious life. You have had a mountain — a Ramadan, a rescue, a Book that shook you, a night of *khushūʿ*. Then you turned. If you are not of the *khāsirīn*, it is not because the turning was clever. It is because *faḍl* and *raḥmah* are still upon you. Do not use them as a pillow. Use them as 2:52 used *ʿafw*: so that you may be grateful, and take again with strength. The next verse will name a party whose turning took a legal form: they broke the Sabbath they had been given, and they were told, “Be apes, despised.” Grace has a boundary. 2:64 is the boundary not yet crossed by a people. 2:65 is the boundary crossed by a town.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:65

> You are already aware of those of you who broke the Sabbath. We said to them, “Be disgraced apes!”

**Commentary:**

The turning of 2:64 is given a town: *wa la-qad ʿalimtumu alladhīna iʿtadaw minkum fī al-sabti fa-qulnā lahum kūnū qiradatan khāsiʾīn*, “And you have certainly known those among you who transgressed in the Sabbath, so We said to them, ‘Be apes, despised.’” *La-qad ʿalimtum*, you have known — a knowledge they cannot plead they lack. The story is in their Book and in their memory. *Iʿtadaw*, they transgressed, from the *iʿtidāʾ* of 2:61. *Minkum*, among you: a party, not the whole of Israel, as 2:59 specified the wrongdoers. *Fī al-sabt*, in the Sabbath. The Sabbath, *al-sabt*, from *s-b-t*, rest, cessation, was a law upon them: “The Sabbath was only appointed for those who differed over it” (16:124). “You have known those who transgressed among you in the Sabbath” is the Qur'an's way of saying: do not make us tell you a story you tell.

Sūrah al-Aʿrāf tells it: “Ask them about the town that was by the sea, when they transgressed in the Sabbath, when their fish came to them on their Sabbath day, appearing, and on the day they had no Sabbath they did not come. Thus We tested them because they were defiantly disobedient” (7:163). Three groups, in the reports of the *salaf* that al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr gather: a group that fished by a trick, throwing nets or digging channels on Friday so that the fish would be trapped on Saturday and taken on Sunday; a group that forbade them and witnessed; a group that was silent, saying “why do you advise a people Allah will destroy or punish?” (7:164). The fish appearing on the Sabbath was a *balāʾ*, a trial of the same family as the tree of 2:35: the forbidden thing made present, the lawful days empty. A legal trick, a *ḥīlah*, kept the letter of not fishing on Saturday and killed the meaning. It is *ḥiṭṭah* changed to *ḥinṭah* in a harbor.

*Fa-qulnā lahum kūnū qiradatan khāsiʾīn*, so We said to them, “Be apes, despised.” *Kūnū* is Be, the word of 2:117’s *kun fa-yakūn*. *Qiradah* are apes. *Khāsiʾīn* is driven away, despised, expelled from honor. The majority of the *salaf*, including Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, Qatādah, as al-Ṭabarī records, took the transformation as real: their bodies were changed, they died as apes and did not eat, drink, or have young, and they were a sign for three days, or they perished. Some later readers preferred a metaphor of character: they became like apes in imitation and greed. Ahl al-Sunnah, with Ibn Kathīr, hold to the wording and to the *salaf*: Allah (الله) who raised a mountain and raised the dead of 2:56 can say “Be apes” and they are. 7:166: “When they were insolent about what they had been forbidden, We said to them, ‘Be apes, despised.’” 5:60 mentions apes and pigs among those who incurred wrath. The verse is a *masakh*, a deforming, a punishment of form for a crime of form: they played with the shape of a law, so their own shapes were played with.

The Prophet (ﷺ) forbade this *ummah* the *ḥīlah* that killed the Sabbath. He said, “Do not commit what the Jews committed: they made the forbidden lawful by tricks” (in the sense of reports the jurists of *ḥiyal* have discussed). He forbade the selling of sample for sample with delay when it is *ribā* in disguise, and he said, “May Allah curse the Jews: fats were forbidden to them, so they melted them and sold them” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). That hadith is 2:65’s harbor in a market. The Sabbath-breaker and the fat-melter are one mind: keep the sound of the law, empty the body.

Theologically the verse is the gravity of a test that looks like opportunity. Fish on the Sabbath are a provision appearing at the wrong door, as the tree was a forever appearing at the wrong tree. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *balāʾ* often arrives as a *niʿmah* in the wrong hour, and that *taqwā* is to refuse a fish because of a day. The silent group of 7:164 is a warning inside the warning: not fishing is not enough if the fisher is not forbidden. “When they forgot what they had been reminded of, We saved those who used to forbid evil and We seized those who wronged with a wretched punishment because they were defiantly disobedient” (7:165). The forbidders were saved. The silent are discussed by the *salaf* with a fear that they were included in the punishment; the wording of 7:165 saves the forbidders by name.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, spent pages on *ḥiyal*: when a trick is a *makhraj*, a lawful exit, and when it is a Sabbath-channel. The mark, he and Ibn al-Qayyim after him said, is whether the purpose of the Lawgiver is honored or emptied. A trick that empties is 2:65 even if the fatwā is elegant. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The lawful is clear and the unlawful is clear, and between them ambiguous matters” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The fish on Saturday were not ambiguous. They were a test of a clear day. Elegance is not a third category.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Do not commit what the Jews committed, making lawful by tricks what Allah has forbidden,” in the meaning of the reports Ibn Taymiyyah and Ibn al-Qayyim gathered on *ḥiyal*. 2:65 is the mother of that warning. A channel dug on Friday is not cleverness. It is a face turned from a day Allah (الله) named.

For the reader the verse is not a license to call a people apes. It is a report of a town, a party, a *masakh*, and a law of tricks. This *ummah* has Sabbaths of its own — the prayer’s time, the zakāt’s due, the *ribā*’s door, the fast’s mouth — and it has channels dug on Friday. “Be apes” is not our command to utter. “Do not transgress in the Sabbath” is our command to hear. The next verse will say that this fate was made a *nakāl*, an example, for those in front of it and those behind it, and a *mawʿiẓah* for the mindful. The apes are not the point. The example is the point.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:66

> So We made their fate an example to present and future generations, and a lesson to the God-fearing.

**Commentary:**

The *masakh* is not left as a private ruin: *fa-jaʿalnāhā nakālan limā bayna yadayhā wa mā khalfahā wa mawʿiẓatan lil-muttaqīn*, “So We made it an exemplary punishment for what was before it and what was after it, and a lesson for the mindful.” The pronoun in *jaʿalnāhā* has been read as returning to the town, to the penalty, to the *masakh* itself. The meaning is one: this event was appointed to be a *nakāl*. *Nakāl* is from *n-k-l*, to restrain by a harsh example, a punishment that makes the hand of another pull back. The shackles that restrain are *nukl*; a *nakāl* is a warning made of pain. Allah (الله) does not deform a town for the town only. He deforms it so that the harbor next door, and the century next door, will not dig the same channel.

*Limā bayna yadayhā wa mā khalfahā*, for what is between its hands and what is behind it. The phrase is the phrase of time and of space. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī records, said: for the towns around it, and for the generations after it. Others said: for the sins they had done before, and for the people who would come after. Others said: a warning to the people of that age and to the people of this age who read the Qur'an. Al-Qurṭubī includes the readings and settles on the function: no one is meant to hear 2:65 as a curiosity of zoology. It is a *nakāl* with a front and a back.
