That removal is not 2:80’s claim. 2:80’s claim is a people’s exemption by blood, by name, by a number they invented. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “O Fāṭimah, save yourself from the Fire, for I avail you nothing against Allah” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The daughter of the Messenger (ﷺ) is not given numbered days. How then a people who refused him?

Al-Qurṭubī notes that every community is tried by a version of counted days: we are the *ummah* of Muḥammad, we will be punished a little, our shaykh will pull us, our lineage will speak. 2:48 has already closed the doors of soul, intercession without permission, ransom, and help. 2:80 is a people trying to open a door of a calendar. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as a *tabkīt*, a putting to silence. Two questions, no third. Covenant, or ignorance. They have neither a covenant nor a knowledge. They have *amānī*.

Theologically the verse is the ethics of speaking about the Fire. The Fire of 2:24 is prepared for the disbelievers. The Fire of 2:39 is remaining. To speak of it as a brief touch for a named people, without a text, is to say upon Allah (الله) what one does not know, which is of the greatest *ẓulm*. “Do not say about Allah except the truth” is the spirit of 7:33’s forbidding of saying about Allah what you do not know. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that hope without a text is *amn*, a false safety, and that fear without a text is despair, and that both are *qawl ʿalā Allāh*. 2:80 is *amn* as a slogan.

Al-Ṭabarī records that some of them said the numbered days were the days they worshipped the calf, a limited crime, a limited Fire. 2:61 has already listed crimes that are not limited: killing prophets, covering signs. A calendar that only counts a calf and does not count a prophet’s blood is a calendar of 2:79. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The bankrupt of my *ummah*…” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim) — a man who comes with prayer and has struck this one and taken that one’s wealth. Numbered days will not cover a bankruptcy of rights. 2:81 will say *balā*, no: whoever earns evil and is encompassed by it is of the Fire, remaining.

For the reader the verse is a confiscation of every “it will not touch us except…” You have not taken a covenant that your nationality is a term of days. You have a covenant of *lā ilāha illā Allāh*, which is fulfilled by a life, not by a count. Say about the Fire what He has said. Say about mercy what He has said. Do not write numbered days with the hand of 2:79. The sūrah will, in 2:81–82, state the two companies without a coupon: those encompassed by their evil, remaining in the Fire; those who believe and do good, remaining in the Garden. 2:80 is the last *umniyyah* before that clarity. This batch of twenty verses began with onions and a stamp of wrath, opened a door of justice for whoever believes and does good, lifted a mountain, named apes, slaughtered a cow, sat a corpse up, hardened a heart, tilted a speech, wrote a book for a price, and ends with a calendar of Fire that does not exist. The next run will take that calendar away.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:81

> But no! Those who commit evil and are engrossed in sin will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.

**Commentary:**

The coupon of numbered days is torn: *balā man kasaba sayyiʾatan wa aḥāṭat bihi khaṭīʾatuhu fa-ulāʾika aṣḥābu al-nāri hum fīhā khālidūn*, “No! Whoever earns an evil and his sin encompasses him — those are the companions of the Fire; they will remain therein.” *Balā* is the particle that answers a negative with a positive of contradiction. They said *lan tamassanā*, it will not touch us. The Lord says *balā*: yes it will; no, your calendar is not a covenant. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mass of the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, read *sayyiʾah* here as *shirk*, the evil that is not forgiven if one dies upon it (4:48), and *khaṭīʾah* as the sin that surrounds a man until it becomes his house. Others, including Mujāhid, said *sayyiʾah* is the great sin and the encompassing is persistence until death without *tawbah*. Both readings serve one verdict: a Fire without a numbered exit for the one whose crime has become his climate.

*Kasaba* is to earn, the same root as 2:79’s *yaksibūn*. Evil is not a weather that falls. It is a wage. The servant works, and the work is stored. *Aḥāṭat bihi khaṭīʾatuhu*, his sin has encompassed him. *Iḥāṭah* is the surround of 2:19’s *Allāhu muḥīṭun*: a circle with no door. Al-Rāghib wrote that a sin encompasses when it wraps the servant on every side — belief, deed, death — so that no patch of *tawḥīd* remains uncovered. Ibn Kathīr prefers that the encompassing is *kufr* that has closed over the heart, because *khulūd*, remaining, is the Qur'an's word for the disbeliever’s stay, not for the sinful monotheist whose stay, if it happens, is a term of justice then a term of *faḍl*. Ahl al-Sunnah hold both ends of that rope. “Whoever dies associating nothing with Allah will enter Paradise,” and “whoever dies associating something with Allah will enter the Fire” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the meanings of the hadiths of Jābir and of Muʿādh). The people of 2:80 claimed a stay of days by blood. 2:81 answers with a stay of forever by encompassing.

*Aṣḥāb al-nār*, companions of the Fire, is a companionship of identity, not of a visit. *Hum fīhā khālidūn*, they in it remaining. The pronoun is repeated for *ḥaṣr*: they, in it, forever. 2:39 has already said it of those who disbelieve and deny Our signs. 2:81 says it of those whose evil has become a surround. The two verses are one company described from two sides: from the door of *kufr*, and from the door of a sin that has eaten the rest of the life. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of the Hereafter, pauses on the sinful Muslim. The hadiths of intercession in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim are mass-transmitted in meaning: people will come out of the Fire who had said *lā ilāha illā Allāh*, after they have been burned, by the *shafāʿah* of the Prophet (ﷺ) and by the mercy of the Most Merciful who takes out of the Fire whoever He wills. That coming-out is not 2:81. 2:81 is the one whom the sin has surrounded so that no *tawḥīd* remains to be a rope. The numbered days of 2:80 tried to steal the language of that coming-out and give it to a people who refused the last Messenger (ﷺ). The theft is refused.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “O Fāṭimah bint Muḥammad, save yourself from the Fire, for I avail you nothing against Allah” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “Whoever has a mustard seed of pride in his heart will not enter Paradise,” and he also said that whoever has a mustard seed of faith will not remain in the Fire (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the hadiths of pride and of intercession). The mustard of faith is the opposite of encompassing. A sin that leaves a seed is not 2:81. A sin that has become the whole field is. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that sins are a siege, and that *tawbah* is a breaking of the siege, and that death upon the siege without a break is the *iḥāṭah* of this verse. “Say, ‘O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of the mercy of Allah; indeed Allah forgives all sins’” (39:53). 39:53 is the door while breath remains. 2:81 is the house after the door is no longer taken.

Theologically the verse is the justice of *jazāʾ*. The Fire is not a mood of the Lord. It is a home earned. *Aṣḥāb* is a word of settling, as one settles with a tribe. The people of 2:5 are upon guidance; the people of 2:81 are companions of a Fire. Two residences, two companies. The modern ear, trained to hear forever as cruelty, is asked to hear it as truth-telling. A surround that was chosen — *kasaba* — is not an accident visited upon a victim. The Lord who said “My mercy encompasses all things” (7:156) is the Lord who also said this. Mercy is not the cancellation of a surround the servant refused to leave.

Al-Rāzī noted that *khaṭīʾah* in the singular, with a possessive, is the sin as a single climate, not a list of slips. A man may have many slips and a climate of *tawḥīd*. A man may have a reputation of religion and a climate of *shirk* or of a pride that has closed. The verse looks at the climate. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “A man may do the deeds of the people of the Fire while he is of the people of Paradise, and a man may do the deeds of the people of Paradise while he is of the people of the Fire, and the deeds are according to the endings” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). Endings are the *iḥāṭah* made visible. Ask for a good ending while the surround is still a threat and not yet a wall.

For the reader the verse is the end of every slogan that makes the Fire a weekend. You have not been given numbered days by lineage, by a *madhhab*, by a shaykh, by a passport. You have been given a warning and a door. Earn what you would like to be surrounded by. The next verse will name the other companionship: those who believe and do good, residents of the Garden, remaining. 2:81 and 2:82 are the two houses of the sūrah’s opening, now restated after a market of wishes. Choose the house by the earning, not by the wish.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:82

> And those who believe and do good will be the residents of Paradise. They will be there forever.

**Commentary:**

The other house is named with the same grammar of remaining: *wa alladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāti ulāʾika aṣḥābu al-jannati hum fīhā khālidūn*, “And those who believe and do righteous deeds — those are the companions of the Garden; they will remain therein.” The pairing with 2:81 is deliberate and almost architectural. Evil earned, sin encompassing, Fire, remaining. Faith, righteous work, Garden, remaining. Two *ulāʾika*, two *aṣḥāb*, two *khulūd*. The sūrah that opened with the *muttaqūn* upon guidance (2:5) and the sealed (2:6–7) and the hypocrites (2:8–20) now, after Israel’s calendar of Fire, restates the two ends without a third coupon. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī records, said this is the reply to their claim of numbered days: remaining is for two companies, and neither remaining is a tribal lease.

*Āmanū* is the faith of 2:3–4: unseen, prayer, spending, this Book and the Books before, certainty of the Last Day — and, after the sending of Muḥammad (ﷺ), faith in him. *ʿAmilū al-ṣāliḥāt* is the work that is *ṣāliḥ*, sound, fit, the opposite of *fasād*. The two are not rivals. The Murjiʾah who later tried to make faith speech without deed are already answered by the *wa* that will not drop the work. The Khawārij who later tried to make every great sin an encompassing of 2:81 are answered by the *īmān* that remains a seed even under a burn, as the hadiths of intercession state. Ahl al-Sunnah hold the pair: faith in the heart and on the tongue, deeds as its necessary fruit, *khulūd* in the Garden for the believer, *khulūd* in the Fire for the disbeliever, and a middle of punishment and then exit for the sinful monotheist whom 2:81 did not describe.

*Al-jannah* is the Garden, from *j-n-n*, to conceal, because its trees conceal, or because it is the unseen reward that 2:3’s people believed before they saw. *Aṣḥāb al-jannah* is companionship as settling: not a visit, a citizenship. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “A place in Paradise the size of a whip is better than the world and all that is in it” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī), and he said, “Allah said: I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and has not occurred to a human heart” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Remaining in that is the *falāḥ* of 2:5 made into an address. Ibn Kathīr notes that the verse does not say “those who believed among the Children of Israel only” or “those who believed among the Arabs only.” *Alladhīna āmanū* is whoever has believed and worked, the filter of 2:62 now given a house.

*Hum fīhā khālidūn* matches 2:81 word for word in the last clause, so that no one will think the Garden is less lasting than the Fire, or the Fire less lasting than the Garden. The symmetry is a theology. The Lord who remains does not give a remaining to one company and a rumor to the other. “As for those who are wretched, they will be in the Fire… remaining therein as long as the heavens and the earth endure, except as your Lord wills” (11:106–107), and “as for those who are blessed, they will be in the Garden, remaining therein as long as the heavens and the earth endure, except as your Lord wills — a gift uninterrupted” (11:108). Ahl al-Sunnah read the exception in the Fire as the sinful monotheists who exit, and the gift uninterrupted as the believers’ stay that is not cut. 2:82 is that gift in al-Baqarah’s shorter tongue.

The Prophet (ﷺ) described the Garden until the Companions wept with longing. He said the lowest of its people will have what is equal to the world ten times, and that they will look upon the Face of their Lord, which is the increase of 10:26. He taught *duʿāʾ* for the Garden and refuge from the Fire in every prayer. 2:82 is not information for a file. It is a destination that rearranges a day. Al-Qurṭubī writes that *ṣāliḥāt* includes the rights of Allah (الله) and the rights of the servants, so that a faith that prays and cheats is not this verse’s pair. “Do you order piety of the people and forget yourselves?” (2:44). The work must be sound in both directions.

Theologically the verse is hope with a spine. Hope without work is 2:80’s *umniyyah*. Work without faith is the deed that becomes dust (25:23). The *wa* between *āmanū* and *ʿamilū* is the religion. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the heart’s *īmān* is a tree and the *ṣāliḥāt* are its fruit, and that a tree without fruit is sick and a fruit without a tree is a theft. 2:82 is a healthy orchard remaining. Ibn Taymiyyah returned often to this balance against the Murjiʾah and the Waʿīdiyyah: do not empty faith of deeds, do not empty the sinful believer of hope. The verse names the successful end; it does not name every station on the way.

For the present age the Garden has been made a cartoon or a silence. Some preach it as a lounge of appetites; some are too shy to mention it, as if remaining with Allah (الله) were an embarrassment in a material century. The verse is neither shy nor cartoonish. Companions of the Garden, remaining. The *muttaqūn* of 2:2–5 were promised *falāḥ*; this verse names the house of that *falāḥ*. The reader who has just been denied numbered days in the Fire is not left in a vacuum. He is given a remaining that is better than a thousand years of 2:96’s clinging. Believe. Do what is sound. The next verse will return to Israel’s covenant with a list that looks like this verse’s work: worship none but Allah, kindness to parents and kin and orphans and the needy, good speech, prayer, zakāt — and then a turning, except a few. 2:82’s company is the few and whoever joins them. 2:83 will show the list and the turning in one breath.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:83

> And ˹remember˺ when We took a covenant from the children of Israel ˹stating˺, “Worship none but Allah; be kind to parents, relatives, orphans and the needy; speak kindly to people; establish prayer; and pay alms-tax.” But you ˹Israelites˺ turned away—except for a few of you—and were indifferent.

**Commentary:**

The two houses of 2:81–82 are now given a covenant that should have filled the second: *wa idh akhadhnā mīthāqa banī Isrāʾīla lā taʿbudūna illā Allāha wa bil-wālidayni iḥsānan wa dhī al-qurbā wa al-yatāmā wa al-masākīni wa qūlū lil-nāsi ḥusnan wa aqīmū al-ṣalāta wa ātū al-zakāta thumma tawallaytum illā qalīlan minkum wa antum muʿriḍūn*, “And when We took the covenant of the Children of Israel: ‘Worship none but Allah, and to parents goodness, and to the near of kin and the orphans and the needy, and speak to people good, and establish the prayer, and give the zakāt.’ Then you turned away, except a few of you, while you were turning aside.” *Mīthāq* is the firm pact of 2:27, 2:40, 2:63. Here it is unpacked into a list that is almost the whole of a revealed ethic. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, said this is the covenant in the Torah, the same substance this *ummah* was given in its own tongue.

*Lā taʿbudūna illā Allāh* is *tawḥīd al-ulūhiyyah*, the first of the Fātiḥah’s “You alone we worship.” The verb is imperfect in a covenant of command: you shall not worship except Allah. Everything after it is the *iḥsān* that *tawḥīd* requires toward creatures. *Bil-wālidayni iḥsānan*, to the two parents, goodness — the pairing the Qur'an never tires of: “Your Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him, and to parents, goodness” (17:23). *Iḥsān* is more than not-harming. It is excellence, the beautiful doing. Then *dhī al-qurbā*, the possessor of nearness, kin; *al-yatāmā*, the orphans, those whose parent-shield is gone; *al-masākīn*, the needy, those whose need has sat them down. The circle widens from the Lord to the parents to the blood to the broken to the poor. A religion that stops at a creed and does not reach an orphan is not this *mīthāq*.

*Wa qūlū lil-nāsi ḥusnan*, and say to people a good saying. *Al-nās* here is people, not only one’s tribe. Ibn ʿAbbās said: speak the truth and command the good. Abū al-ʿĀliyah said: say to them the beautiful, and among it is the call to Allah (الله). Al-Qurṭubī includes gentleness of tongue and the refusal of indecency and of a word that cuts. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “The Muslim is the one from whose tongue and hand the Muslims are safe.” 2:83 makes good speech a covenant, not a personality. Then *aqīmū al-ṣalāh wa ātū al-zakāh*, the two pillars of 2:3, body and property. The list is therefore creed, family, the weak, the tongue, the prayer, the poor-due. It is 2:82’s *īmān* and *ṣāliḥāt* itemized.

*Thumma tawallaytum illā qalīlan minkum*, then you turned away except a few of you. *Thumma* after a list is a shame: after all of that, a back. *Tawallī* is the turning of 2:64. *Illā qalīlan* is the Qur'an's justice inside a rebuke, the same justice as 2:59’s “those who wronged” and 2:75’s “a party.” A few took the *mīthāq*. Among them, in later generations, those who believed in Mūsā (peace be upon him) truly, and in Madinah those like ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām (may Allah be pleased with him). *Wa antum muʿriḍūn*, and you are turning aside — a ḥāl of a present identity, not only a past event. *Iʿrāḍ* is to present the side, to look away. The turning was not a slip of a year. It became a posture.

The Prophet (ﷺ) was given the same list in this *ummah*’s law. “The most beloved of deeds to Allah is prayer at its time, then kindness to parents, then *jihād* in the path of Allah” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “I and the one who cares for an orphan will be like these two in the Garden,” and he held two fingers together (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). He made zakāt a pillar whose refusal is a war in Abū Bakr’s *ijtihād*. 2:83 is not an antique of Israel. It is the shared spine of the prophetic books. This *ummah* was warned it would follow those before it span by span (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A turning from parents, from orphans, from a kind word, from prayer, from zakāt, after a *shahādah*, is 2:83 with new names.

Theologically the verse teaches that *tawḥīd* is not a slogan that excuses a hard house. Worship none but Allah — then the parents. A man who recites *lā ilāha illā Allāh* and cuts his mother has not taken this *mīthāq*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the rights of the servants are the proving-ground of the right of the Lord, because the Lord is not in need and the servant is. 2:83 puts the un-needing first and the needing second through sixth, so that the first is not used as a veil over the rest. Al-Rāzī noted the order: the Creator, then the two who were the means of your coming into the world, then the womb, then those without a womb’s remaining shield, then those without wealth, then the tongue that can wound all of them, then the prayer that trains the body, then the zakāt that trains the hand.

For the reader the verse is a mirror of a Muslim’s week. Worship, parents, kin, an orphan you could have checked on, a needy person at a door, a tongue in a chat, a prayer, a due. The Children of Israel turned except a few. This *ummah* still has a few, and still has a turning. Do not read *tawallaytum* as a closed file of another people. Read it as a posture you might be in. The next verse will add a clause of the same covenant that Madinah would make painfully local: do not shed one another’s blood, do not expel one another from homes — a pledge they avowed and witnessed, and then, as 2:85 will say, broke in the wars of Aws and Khazraj while still ransoming captives as if the Torah were a menu.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:84

> And ˹remember˺ when We took your covenant that you would neither shed each other’s blood nor expel each other from their homes, you gave your pledge and bore witness.

**Commentary:**

A second clause of the *mīthāq* is now isolated because Madinah will need it: *wa idh akhadhnā mīthāqakum lā tasfikūna dimāʾakum wa lā tukhrijūna anfusakum min diyārikum thumma aqrartum wa antum tashhadūn*, “And when We took your covenant: you shall not shed your blood, nor expel yourselves from your homes; then you avowed, and you bear witness.” *Lā tasfikūna dimāʾakum*, you shall not pour out your bloods. The blood is called *your* blood though it is a brother’s, because the nation is one body in the covenant. *Lā tukhrijūna anfusakum min diyārikum*, you shall not expel yourselves from your homes — again *anfusakum*, your own selves, because the expelled is you in another house. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī records, said the Torah forbade them killing one another and driving one another from towns. Al-Qurṭubī notes the grammar of *anfus* and *dimāʾ* as a teaching: the other’s blood is your blood in law and in *taqwā*.

The scene in Madinah, which 2:85 will narrate as a present tense, is the occasion that makes this memory a blade. The Jews of the oasis — Banū Qaynuqāʿ, Banū al-Naḍīr, Banū Qurayẓah — had lived as allies of the two Arab tribes, Aws and Khazraj. In the wars of the Jāhiliyyah, Qaynuqāʿ stood with Khazraj, Naḍīr and Qurayẓah with Aws. Jew killed Jew across those alliances. Homes were emptied. Then, when captives were taken, the Torah’s law of ransoming the captive was applied, and money was paid to free the very people they had helped to expel. Ibn Kathīr, following Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Isḥāq, treats 2:84–85 as that file opened in the Prophet’s (ﷺ) city. The covenant was not unknown. *Thumma aqrartum*, then you avowed, you admitted, you said yes. *Wa antum tashhadūn*, and you are witnessing — still, now, in Madinah, with a Book in the house and a Messenger (ﷺ) in the town.

*Safk al-dam* is the pouring of blood, a verb of waste as well as of violence. The Qur'an uses it of the first human crime’s fear in the angels’ mouth (2:30) and of the prohibition that 5:32 will universalize: whoever kills a soul, it is as if he had killed mankind. 2:84 is that prohibition as a *mīthāq* upon a named people. *Ikhrāj min al-diyār* is a slower killing: a life without a house, a family on a road. The modern world has both verbs in industrial form. The verse does not need a new noun. It needs the old covenant remembered as binding.

*Aqrartum* is from *iqrār*, confession, the word of a contract that has been acknowledged. They cannot plead that the clause was obscure. *Tashhadūn* is witness in the present. Al-Rāzī heard in the present tense a humiliation: you are, while this Qur'an is recited upon you, still witnesses of a pact you are breaking. Witness who does not act is 2:44’s reciter who does not do. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The destruction of the Children of Israel began when they let the noble go and punished the weak for the same crime,” in the meanings of the reports on selective ḥadd. Blood that is noble is still blood. A home that is a rival’s is still a home.

Theologically the verse is the sanctity of blood and dwelling as part of *tawḥīd*’s fruit, not as a secular extra. A people who worship none but Allah (الله) in 2:83’s first clause are a people who do not pour a brother’s blood in 2:84. When the first clause is kept as a slogan and the second is broken as a policy, the slogan is already 2:8. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that rights of blood are among the last things a dying religion drops, and that dropping them is the sign that *īmān* has become genealogy. The Prophet (ﷺ) said in the Farewell Sermon, “Your blood, your property, and your honor are sacred among you as the sacredness of this day of yours, in this month of yours, in this town of yours” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). That sermon is 2:84 given to this *ummah* as a law of the ḥajj’s plain.

Al-Ṭabarī emphasizes *anfusakum*: Allah (الله) made the expelled of your people your own soul, so that the expeller would feel the knife in his own chest. A nationalism that loves a flag and empties a neighbor’s house has not read this *anfus*. A sect that recites and then sheds the reciter of another school has not read this *dimāʾ*. The covenant is older than Madinah’s tribes and younger than every new militia that names itself with a verse.

The Prophet (ﷺ) found in Madinah a compact that tried to honor this clause: the Ṣaḥīfah, the constitution of the city, binding Muhājirūn, Anṣār, and the Jewish tribes to a shared security of blood and quarter. 2:84 is the revealed ground of that compact. What happened after — the breaches, the sieges, the expulsions that 33:26 and 59:2 would later record — is another file, judged by the same Lord who took this *mīthāq*. The verse in front of us is the taking, the avowal, the witness. The next verse is the breaking in detail: killing, expelling, aiding in sin and aggression, then ransoming, then the question that burns: do you believe in part of the Book and cover part?

For the reader the verse is a covenant you have also avowed. *Lā ilāha illā Allāh* includes a brother’s blood. A Muslim’s house is not a prize of a feud. You gave your pledge in a *shahādah* and you bear witness every time you say *ashhadu*. Do not be a witness who pours. Do not be a witness who expels with a rumor, a boycott of kinship, a post that drives a family from a street. 2:84 is short because the law is not subtle. The subtlety, which is the shame, is 2:85’s mix: breaking and keeping in the same week, a piety of ransom laid upon a crime of exile.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:85

> But here you are, killing each other and expelling some of your people from their homes, aiding one another in sin and aggression; and when those ˹expelled˺ come to you as captives, you still ransom them—though expelling them was unlawful for you. Do you believe in some of the Scripture and reject the rest? Is there any reward for those who do so among you other than disgrace in this worldly life and being subjected to the harshest punishment on the Day of Judgment? For Allah is never unaware of what you do.

**Commentary:**

The avowal of 2:84 is met with a present-tense indictment: *thumma antum hāʾulāʾi taqtulūna anfusakum wa tukhrijūna farīqan minkum min diyārihim taẓāharūna ʿalayhim bil-ithmi wa al-ʿudwāni wa in yaʾtūkum usārā tufādūhum wa huwa muḥarramun ʿalaykum ikhrājuhum a-fa-tuʾminūna bi-baʿḍi al-kitābi wa takfurūna bi-baʿḍ*, “Then you are these: you kill yourselves, and you expel a party of you from their homes, backing one another against them in sin and transgression; and if they come to you as captives you ransom them, though their expulsion was forbidden to you. Do you then believe in part of the Book and cover part?” The verse is long because the hypocrisy of the file is long. Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Isḥāq, al-Ṭabarī, and Ibn Kathīr narrate the Madinan practice: in the wars of Aws and Khazraj the Jewish allies fought, killed, and expelled their own, then, when the battle ended, collected money and redeemed the captives, saying, “We are commanded to ransom.” They were asked, “Why then do you fight them and expel them?” They said, “We should be shamed if our allies needed us and we sat.” Two laws of one Torah: the law that suited honor in a feud, kept; the law that forbade the feud’s blood, dropped.

*Taqtulūna anfusakum* again: you kill yourselves. The killed Jew is the killer’s self in the covenant’s grammar. *Tukḥrijūna farīqan minkum*, you expel a party of you — *minkum*, from you, so that the expelled cannot be named foreign. *Taẓāharūna ʿalayhim bil-ithmi wa al-ʿudwān*, you back one another against them with sin and aggression. *Taẓāhur* is mutual aid, the *ẓahr*, the back given to an ally. Aid is a virtue in 90:17; here the object of the aid is a crime. *Ithm* is the sin that slows the soul; *ʿudwān* is the transgression that crosses a limit. Together they are a policy. Then the piety: *wa in yaʾtūkum usārā tufādūhum*, if they come to you as captives you ransom them. *Fidāʾ* is a price paid to free. The Torah’s command of ransom is real. The crime is to keep it after you have created the captivity. *Wa huwa muḥarramun ʿalaykum ikhrājuhum*, and their expulsion was forbidden to you. The verse names the forbidden act so that the kept act cannot wash it.

*A-fa-tuʾminūna bi-baʿḍi al-kitābi wa takfurūna bi-baʿḍ*, do you believe in some of the Book and disbelieve in some? This is the verse’s knife, and it is the verse’s gift to every later century. A Book is not a menu. *Īmān* in a part that costs little, *kufr* in a part that costs a feud’s honor — that split is not a smaller faith. It is a different religion wearing the Book’s name. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, heard in it every selective *fiqh*: a ḥadd applied to the weak and not the noble, a *ribā* renamed, a *ḥijāb* kept and a *ghibah* unleashed, a prayer established and a neighbor’s house emptied. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The destruction of those before you was that they would apply the ḥadd to the lowly and leave the noble” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the hadith of the Makhzūmiyyah). 2:85 is that destruction as a theology of parts.

The worldly wage is named: *fa-mā jazāʾu man yafʿalu dhālika minkum illā khizyun fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā*, the repayment of one who does that among you is nothing but disgrace in the worldly life. *Khizy* is disgrace, the public stripping of honor. Ibn al-Jawzī recorded readings of this *khizy* as the *jizyah*, as the killing of Qurayẓah and the expulsion of Naḍīr, as a general humiliation. The wording is general. A people who split a Book will be split in reputation. Then *wa yawma al-qiyāmati yuraddūna ilā ashaddi al-ʿadhāb*, and on the Day of Rising they will be returned to the most severe punishment. *Ashadd*, most intense — a comparative that does not need a named rival. The Fire of 2:81 is waiting for a split *īmān* that was a *kufr* of parts. *Wa mā Allāhu bi-ghāfilin ʿammā taʿmalūn*, and Allah is not unaware of what you do — the close of 2:74, now over a policy of blood. The ransoming did not hide the expelling. The Witness does not watch only the pious half of a week.

Theologically the verse is the oneness of the Book as a demand of the oneness of the Lord. A Lord who is One sent a speech that is one in its claim upon the servant. To take ransom and leave the sanctity of homes is to treat the Speaker as a committee of voices, some of which can be outvoted by a clan. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *īmān* with *kufr* in the same chest toward the same revelation is the door of *nifāq* and of heresy. 2:85 is that door as a battlefield custom. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the most dangerous *kufr* is the *kufr* that keeps a ritual to soothe the part of the soul that still fears, while the rest of the soul takes what it wants. Ransom soothes. Expulsion profits. The Book is used as water after a fire one has set.

The Prophet (ﷺ) unified this *ummah*’s Book upon it. He did not allow a Madinan custom to outvote a verse. When the woman of Makhzūm stole, he refused intercession and swore he would cut Fāṭimah’s hand if she stole (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). That oath is the opposite of 2:85. This *ummah* is tried by the same split every time a verse is heavy: we keep the verses of hope and drop the verses of ḥadd; we keep the verses of identity and drop the verses of justice to the outsider; we keep the recitation and drop the neighbor. “Do you believe in some of the Book and reject the rest?” is a question that does not expire.

For the reader the verse is a search of your own parts. Which āyah do you ransom, and which do you expel? Disgrace in this life is already a harvest of splits; the harshest punishment is the rest. Allah (الله) is not unaware. The next verse will name the trade underneath the split: they have purchased the worldly life at the price of the Hereafter, so the punishment is not lightened, and they are not helped. 2:85’s mix is 2:16’s trade in a legal key.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:86

> These are the ones who trade the Hereafter for the life of this world. So their punishment will not be reduced, nor will they be helped.

**Commentary:**

The split of the Book is now priced: *ulāʾika alladhīna ishtarawū al-ḥayāta al-dunyā bil-ākhirati fa-lā yukhaffafu ʿanhumu al-ʿadhābu wa lā hum yunṣarūn*, “Those are the ones who have purchased the worldly life with the Hereafter, so the punishment will not be lightened from them, nor will they be helped.” *Ulāʾika* again, the far pointer of 2:5 and 2:16, now aimed at the people of parts. *Ishtarawū* is the trade-verb of 2:16, 2:41, 2:79, 2:90. They bought *al-ḥayāt al-dunyā*, the nearer life, and the price was *al-ākhirah*. The *bāʾ* is the *bāʾ* of the price. What did the nearer life look like in 2:85? The honor of an alliance, the keeping of a feud’s face, the custom of a town, a ransom that bought a reputation of piety after a crime. All of that is *dunyā*. The Hereafter was the whole Book kept, the blood not poured, the home not emptied, a Day when parts are not accepted.

Ibn ʿAbbās said they chose this world over what Allah (الله) promised. Al-Ṭabarī: every who prefers a worldly good to a command of the Hereafter is this trader. Ibn Kathīr links the verse to the Jews of Madinah who fought for tribal honor and thought a ransom would settle the file in heaven as it settled it in the market. The file does not settle. *Fa-lā yukhaffafu ʿanhumu al-ʿadhāb*, so the punishment is not lightened from them. *Takhfīf* is a making-light, a reduction of load. 2:80 wanted a reduction of days. 2:86 says there will be no lightening for this trade. *Wa lā hum yunṣarūn*, nor will they be helped. 2:48 has already closed the four doors: no soul for another, no intercession without leave, no ransom taken, no help. 2:86 repeats the last two in the key of a purchase: the ransom they paid on earth for captives will not be a ransom for themselves; the allies they helped in sin will not help them in the Fire.

The Prophet (ﷺ) used the same commerce in the winning direction. “Allah has purchased from the believers their lives and their properties in exchange for Paradise” (9:111). He said, “The world is a prison for the believer and a paradise for the disbeliever” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “Be in this world as a stranger or a traveler” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). The people of 2:86 reversed the purchase: they sold the Garden for a town’s custom. The Prophet (ﷺ) also said, “Two hungry wolves sent among sheep are not more destructive than a man’s greed for wealth and status is to his religion” (al-Tirmidhī). Status in a feud is a wolf. 2:86 is the flock after the wolf.

Theologically the verse is the impossibility of a mixed portfolio at death. A man may mix in the week — ransom and expulsion, prayer and *ghibah* — and imagine the mix will be weighed as a blend. The verse names the mix as a purchase of one side. The Hereafter was the capital; it was spent. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that every sin is a small purchase of *dunyā* with a slice of *ākhirah*, and that 2:86 is the purchase when it has become the whole business. Al-Rāzī noted *lā yukhaffafu* as a denial of 2:80’s hope in another form: not numbered days, not a lightened Fire, not a helper from a clan. The three denials — no lightening, no help, and in 2:48 no ransom — are one theology: the Lord of the Day of Judgment is not a court that takes a tribal lawyer.

Al-Qurṭubī draws a legal caution that is also spiritual: a good deed done to decorate a bad deed does not cancel the bad deed. Ransom after expulsion is not *tawbah*; *tawbah* would have been to stop expelling and to restore. A charity after a theft, while the theft continues, is 2:86’s ransom. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Allah is good and accepts only what is good” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A price paid from a crime’s pocket is not the purchase He accepts.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Every people go forth in the morning selling themselves: some free themselves, and some destroy themselves” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:86 is the morning of a sale that destroys. A feud’s honor looked like freedom of a clan; it was a chain on a neck. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that preferring a custom of the fathers to a verse is the *dīn* of 2:170, and that 2:86 is that preference as a purchase of a life. The fathers of Aws and Khazraj had a custom of alliance; the Torah had a custom of blood’s sanctity. They bought the first.

Al-Ṭabarī records that some of the *salaf* read *lā yunṣarūn* as a denial of helpers in the Fire: no clan, no ransom-brother, no angel of 2:98’s Mīkāʾīl whom they claimed as a friend. Friends of a sin are not friends of a Fire. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “A man is upon the religion of his intimate friend” (Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī). 2:85’s *taẓāhur* in sin becomes 2:86’s no-help. The intimate of aggression will not be an intimate of rescue.

For the modern reader the trade is rarely named as a feud of Aws. It is named as a career that needs a fatwā of silence, a nationality that needs a verse dropped, a feed that needs a piety of captions and a private of harm. The Hereafter is still the price. The punishment is still not lightened by the caption. Help will not come from the audience that applauded the split. 2:48’s closed doors remain closed. The next verse will widen the file from a town’s wars to a history of messengers: Mūsā given the Book, messengers in succession, ʿĪsā given clear proofs and supported with the holy spirit — and every time a messenger brought what they did not like, arrogance, a rejection of some, a killing of others. 2:86’s trade is the motive; 2:87 is the biography of how a people treated the ones who brought the Book they split.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:87

> Indeed, We gave Moses the Book and sent after him successive messengers. And We gave Jesus, son of Mary, clear proofs and supported him with the holy spirit. Why is it that every time a messenger comes to you ˹Israelites˺ with something you do not like, you become arrogant, rejecting some and killing others?

**Commentary:**

The biography of the split is now a biography of messengers: *wa la-qad ātaynā Mūsā al-kitāba wa qaffaynā min baʿdihi bil-rusuli wa ātaynā ʿĪsā ibna Maryama al-bayyināti wa ayyadnāhu bi-rūḥi al-qudusi a-fa-kullamā jāʾakum rasūlun bimā lā tahwā anfusukumu istakbartum fa-farīqan kadhdhabtum wa farīqan taqtulūn*, “And We certainly gave Mūsā the Book, and We caused messengers to follow after him, and We gave ʿĪsā son of Mary the clear proofs and We supported him with the holy spirit. Is it then that whenever a messenger comes to you with what your souls do not desire, you grow arrogant, so a party you deny and a party you kill?” *Qaffaynā* is from *qafā*, the nape: we made them follow at his nape, a succession, a queue of *rusul* after the Torah. The Lord did not leave Israel with one Book and a silence. He sent, and sent, and sent. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn count among them Yūshaʿ, Shamwīl, Dāwūd, Sulaymān, Shaʿyā, Armiyā, Zakariyyā, Yaḥyā, and ʿĪsā, peace be upon them — a line, not a freeze.

*Ātaynā ʿĪsā ibna Maryama al-bayyināt*, We gave ʿĪsā son of Mary the clear proofs: the speech from the cradle (19:29–33), the bird of clay, the healing of the born-blind and the leper, the raising of the dead, the Table, as 5:110 gathers. *Wa ayyadnāhu bi-rūḥi al-qudus*, and We supported him with the holy spirit. The *salaf*, including Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Suddī, and Qatādah, as al-Ṭabarī records, said *rūḥ al-qudus* here is Jibrīl (peace be upon him). 16:102 makes the identification: “The holy spirit has brought it down from your Lord in truth.” 2:253 and 5:110 repeat the support. The verse is among the Qur'an's corrections of a later Trinity: the spirit is a servant who supports a servant, not a third of a god. ʿĪsā (peace be upon him) is *ibn Maryam*, named by his mother so that a people who said “son of God” and a people who said slander of Maryam would both be answered. He is given; he is supported; he is not the Giver.

Then the question that is a slap: *a-fa-kullamā jāʾakum rasūlun bimā lā tahwā anfusukum istakbartum*, is it so that whenever a messenger comes to you with what your souls do not desire, you grow arrogant? *Kullamā* is every time, a habit, not a tragedy of one century. The criterion of reception is *hawā*, the soul’s liking. A messenger who confirms a taste is endured; a messenger who forbids a calf, a Sabbath-channel, a feud, a pride of lineage, is met with *istikbār*, the growing of *kibr*, Iblīs’s disease of 2:34. *Fa-farīqan kadhdhabtum wa farīqan taqtulūn*, so a party you denied, and a party you kill. The verbs split as the messengers split in their ends: some were called liars — ʿĪsā (peace be upon him) by many of them, Muḥammad (ﷺ) now — and some were killed — Yaḥyā, Zakariyyā in the reports, others of Israel’s prophets, as 2:61 already charged. *Taqtulūn* is imperfect: you kill, a present of a past that has not died, because the killing of a prophet’s claim in Madinah is the heir of the killing of a prophet’s body in Jerusalem.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The most severely tried of people are the prophets, then the like, then the like” (al-Tirmidhī). He said he is the nearest of people to ʿĪsā son of Mary, and that there is no prophet between them (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). He recited this history not as a racial curse but as a warning to his own *ummah*: “You will follow the ways of those before you…” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A later generation that likes a preacher who soothes and hates a reminder that pinches is 2:87’s *kullamā* without a knife. Killing has more than one tool. Denial is a killing of a message. Mockery is a killing of a dignity. 2:87 names both tools.

Theologically the verse is the ethics of *hawā* as a filter of revelation. Revelation is not sent to confirm the soul. It is sent to correct it. “He does not speak from *hawā*” (53:3) is said of the Prophet (ﷺ); 2:87 is said of a people who made *hawā* the speaker. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *istikbār* before a messenger is *istikbār* before the Sender, because the messenger is a path, not a rival. Al-Rāzī noted the generosity in *qaffaynā*: succession is mercy, a new door after a closed one; arrogance turns the mercy into a series of trials failed. The holy spirit’s support of ʿĪsā (peace be upon him) did not prevent denial. Signs do not compel. 2:74’s stones still listen better than some chests.

Al-Qurṭubī, against Christian reading, insists that *rūḥ al-qudus* as Jibrīl closes the door of a divine third, and that *ibn Maryam* closes the door of a divine son. The verse honors ʿĪsā (peace be upon him) more than the honor that inflates him, because it keeps him a messenger in a line, given, supported, bringing *bayyināt*, meeting the same *hawā* Mūsā met. This *ummah* is taught to say, “We believe in Allah and what was revealed to us and what was revealed to Ibrāhīm… and what was given to Mūsā and ʿĪsā” (2:136). 2:87 is that faith as history, and Israel’s refusal as a question.

For the reader the verse is a test of what you do with a reminder you do not like. A khutbah that names your *ribā*, a parent who names your screen, a verse that names your feud — do you grow tall, and deny, and kill with a joke? The messengers you will meet are fewer; the *hawā* is the same. The next verse will quote a slogan of that arrogance: “our hearts are unreceptive,” wrapped, covered, *ghulf* — a claim of a closed vessel that the Lord will rename as a curse for *kufr*, and a faith that is little.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:88

> They say, “Our hearts are unreceptive!” In fact, Allah has condemned them for their disbelief. They have but little faith.

**Commentary:**

The arrogance of 2:87 now speaks a medical lie: *wa qālū qulūbunā ghulfun bal laʿanahumu Allāhu bi-kufrihim fa-qalīlan mā yuʾminūn*, “And they say, ‘Our hearts are wrapped.’ Rather, Allah has cursed them for their disbelief, so little is it that they believe.” *Ghulf* is the plural of *aghlaf*, a heart in a *ghilāf*, a wrapper, a sheath, like a sword in a scabbard or a fruit in a rind. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī records, said they meant: our hearts are covered, they do not understand what you say, O Muḥammad. Mujāhid: covered. ʿIkrimah: in shields. 4:155 repeats the slogan: “And their saying, ‘Our hearts are wrapped.’ Rather, Allah has sealed them for their disbelief, so they do not believe except a little.” The wrapper is offered as an excuse: we cannot receive; we are built closed. The verse refuses the excuse and names a curse.

*Bal*, rather — the particle that cancels the previous claim. Not a wrapper from birth. *Laʿanahumu Allāhu bi-kufrihim*, Allah has cursed them for their disbelief. *Laʿn* is expulsion from mercy, a driving-away. The cause is *bi-kufrihim*, because of their *kufr*, not because of a nature they did not choose. The seal of 2:7, the rust of 83:14, the hardness of 2:74, the wrapper they boast — all are judgments upon a refusal, not alibis. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as a rebuttal of a people who claimed a special heart, too full of knowledge to need a new prophet, or too covered to be blamed. Both boasts collapse into *laʿn*.

*Fa-qalīlan mā yuʾminūn*, so little they believe, or little is what they believe, or few of them believe. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn al-Jawzī gather the faces: few among them have faith; they believe in a small portion of their Book; they believe for a short time; they do not believe at all, in an Arabic that uses “rarely” as “never.” Ibn Kathīr prefers the smallness of their faith: a faith that accepts some of Mūsā and rejects the unlettered prophet is a little faith, a faith of parts, the faith of 2:85. The Prophet (ﷺ) said faith has seventy-odd branches (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A people who keep a branch and cut the tree should not call the remainder a garden.

The Prophet (ﷺ) met this slogan in Madinah as a pastoral fact. He recited; they said, in effect, we are already full. He was told, “You do not guide whom you love, but Allah guides whom He wills” (28:56). He was told of hearts between two fingers of the Most Merciful (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He taught the *duʿāʾ*, “O Turner of the hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion.” 2:88 is the heart that has asked not to be turned, and has named the asking a wrapper of dignity. The dignity is a curse.

Theologically the verse is a warning against every doctrine of a closed chest. There is a true covering: Allah (الله) seals after refusal. There is a false covering: we cannot be addressed, our tradition is finished, our identity is a rind that no new āyah may pierce. The second is 2:88’s *qālū*. The first is 2:88’s *bal*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that among the tricks of the soul is to call its refusal an inability, so that sin becomes fate and fate becomes innocence. The verse strips the trick. *Bi-kufrihim* is earned. Al-Rāzī noted that a heart truly unable would not be cursed; it would be excused. The curse proves the ability that was not used.

Al-Qurṭubī hears a lesson for this *ummah*’s learned. A scholar may say “our hearts are full” when a verse threatens a fatwā, a custom, a salary. *Ghulf* then is not a Jewish monopoly. It is a professional deformation. The Prophet (ﷺ) feared the hypocrite with a learned tongue (Aḥmad). A learned tongue in a wrapped heart is 2:88 in a turban. “They have hearts with which they do not understand” (7:179). The instrument is there. The wrapper is a choice that became a judgment.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “When the believer commits a sin, a black speck appears on his heart…” (al-Tirmidhī). A wrapper can be built of specks. 2:88’s *ghulf* is not only a slogan of a tribe; it is a possible end of a Muslim who recites and does not polish. He taught, “Piety is here,” and pointed to his chest (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A chest that announces itself closed has already decided not to be that place of *taqwā*.

Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that among the errors of the People of the Book was to treat guidance as a completed nationality, so that a new prophet was an insult to a finished heart. 2:88 is that finished heart as a wrapper. This *ummah*’s heart is not finished until death. “O you who believe, believe…” (4:136) is a command to people who already believe, because *īmān* can increase (8:2) and a wrapper can still be refused.

Al-Rāghib noted that *ghilāf* is a cover that can be removed; their slogan treated it as a nature. 2:88’s *bal* treats it as a verdict. Removable things that have been judged sealed are 2:7’s end of a road. Ask for a turning while the fingers still turn.

For the reader the verse is a refusal of two comforts: the comfort of “I cannot change, this is my nature,” and the comfort of “I already know enough.” Unwrap. The curse is for *kufr*, and *kufr* is a covering you can stop. Little faith is not a small praise; it is a warning that a remainder of assent is not *īmān* of 2:3. The next verse will show the wrapper’s history in Madinah: they used to ask Allah (الله) for victory over the polytheists by the coming prophet; then the Book they recognized came; they covered it; so curse upon the disbelievers. 2:88’s *laʿn* will be repeated as a *duʿāʾ* against those who recognized and rejected.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:89

> Although they used to pray for victory ˹by means of the Prophet˺ over the polytheists, when there came to them a Book from Allah which they recognized, confirming the Scripture they had ˹in their hands˺, they rejected it. So may Allah’s condemnation be upon the disbelievers.

**Commentary:**

The wrapper is given a backstory of hope turned to refusal: *wa lammā jāʾahum kitābun min ʿindi Allāhi muṣaddiqun limā maʿahum wa kānū min qablu yastaftiḥūna ʿalā alladhīna kafarū fa-lammā jāʾahum mā ʿarafū kafarū bihi fa-laʿnatu Allāhi ʿalā al-kāfirīn*, “And when there came to them a Book from Allah confirming what was with them — and they used before to seek opening, victory, over those who disbelieved — when there came to them what they recognized, they covered it. So the curse of Allah is upon the disbelievers.” Ibn ʿAbbās, as Ibn Isḥāq, al-Ṭabarī, and Ibn Kathīr record, said the Jews of Madinah, when at war with Aws and Khazraj in the Jāhiliyyah, would say: a prophet is about to be sent; we will follow him and kill you with him as ʿĀd and Iram were killed. They sought *fatḥ*, an opening of victory, by means of the awaited messenger. The Anṣār later reminded them of this, as the reports of the *sīrah* tell. Then Muḥammad (ﷺ) came, from Banū Hāshim, not from Banū Isrāʾīl, and the recognition did not become *īmān*. It became *kufr*.

*Yastaftiḥūn* is to seek *fatḥ*, to ask for an opening. The root is the root of 2:76’s *mā fataḥa Allāhu ʿalaykum*, what Allah opened to you of knowledge. Here the opening sought is victory in a feud. A prophet as a weapon. Theologically the ugliness is double: they believed, in a manner, in a coming, and they wanted the coming as a sword against Arab pagans, not as a lordship over their own *hawā*. When the Book came *muṣaddiqan limā maʿahum*, confirming what was with them — the description, the *tawḥīd*, the remaining truth of the Torah — the confirmation was the test. A false prophet would not have confirmed. A true one who was not of their tribe was not wanted. *Fa-lammā jāʾahum mā ʿarafū kafarū bihi*, when what they knew came, they covered it. *ʿArafū* is recognition, ʿilm of a face. 2:75’s party understood then distorted. 2:89’s people recognized then covered. Knowledge as a road to *kufr* is the verse’s terror.

*Fa-laʿnatu Allāhi ʿalā al-kāfirīn*, so the curse of Allah is upon the disbelievers. The *fāʾ* is of consequence: because they covered what they knew, curse. 2:88’s *laʿn* is restated as a sentence upon a defined crime. Ibn Kathīr notes that *al-kāfirīn* here are those who disbelieved after recognition, a *kufr* heavier than the *kufr* of an idolater who had no Book. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “By Him in whose hand is the soul of Muḥammad, no one of this community, Jew or Christian, hears of me, then dies without believing in what I was sent with, except that he will be of the people of the Fire” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:89 is that hadith as a Madinan story: they heard, they had asked for him, they knew, they covered.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām (may Allah be pleased with him) is the counter-biography. He recognized, as the hadith of the three questions in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī tells, and he believed, and he said the Jews were a people of *buht*, slander, if he believed openly at once. He took the *fatḥ* they had asked for and did not use it as a tribal club. The verse’s curse is not upon a blood. It is upon a covering. The few of 2:83 are this man and his like.

Theologically the verse is the danger of wanting revelation as a tool. A community may pray for a revival, a victory, a “Muslim” outcome, and then refuse the Book when the Book names their *ribā*, their racism, their feud. *Yastaftiḥūn* is not always a Jew of 622. It is a movement that wants Islam as a flag and not as a *mīthāq* of 2:83–84. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that among the punishments of *hawā* is that a man is given what he asked and hates it when it arrives, because what he asked was a name and what arrived was a right. Al-Rāzī noted the cruelty of *mā ʿarafū*: recognition without following is a witness against the recognizer, a *ḥujjah* he himself supplied.

Al-Qurṭubī hears a warning to this *ummah* when it boasts of a prophet and then splits his *sunnah* as 2:85 split a Book. We sought opening by him at Badr; we must not cover him in a market of parts. The curse is upon *al-kāfirīn*, those who cover. A covering of a *ḥadīth* that pinches, of a verse of justice to a non-Muslim neighbor, of a law of *ribā*, is a cousin of 2:89 even if the tongue still sends *ṣalāh* upon the Prophet (ﷺ).

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The most truthful word a poet said is the word of Labīd: ‘Indeed, everything except Allah is false’” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The prophet they asked for was not a tribal true; he was a true that made their falsehoods visible. Recognition without following is the *ʿilm* of 2:75 with a Madinan date. Al-Ṭabarī records that some of them said, before the Hijrah, we will be his inner circle. Inner circles do not cover. They take.

Ibn Taymiyyah distinguished the *kufr* of ignorance from the *kufr* of *juhūd*, denial after knowledge. 2:89 is *juhūd*. The curse matches the species. A caller who meets *juhūd* should not thin the message to win a circle that asked for a weapon. He should leave the curse where Allah (الله) placed it and keep the door of 2:62 open for whoever still recognizes and does not cover.

For the reader the verse is a question about your old prayers. You asked for guidance. It came as a command you did not like. Did you recognize and cover? The Anṣār believed the prophet their neighbors had advertised. Do not be the advertiser who misses the guest. The next verse will price the covering: miserable is what they sold themselves for, denying what Allah sent down, out of resentment that Allah grants His *faḍl* to whom He wills of His servants — wrath upon wrath, and a humiliating punishment.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:90

> Miserable is the price they have sold their souls for—denying Allah’s revelation and resenting Allah for granting His grace to whoever He wills of His servants! They have earned wrath upon wrath. And such disbelievers will suffer a humiliating punishment.

**Commentary:**

The covering of 2:89 is given its motive and its price: *biʾsa mā ishtarawū bihi anfusahum an yakfurū bimā anzala Allāhu baghyan an yunazzila Allāhu min faḍlihi ʿalā man yashāʾu min ʿibādihi fa-bāʾū bi-ghaḍabin ʿalā ghaḍabin wa lil-kāfirīna ʿadhābun muhīn*, “Miserable is that for which they have sold themselves — that they disbelieve in what Allah has sent down, out of resentment that Allah should send down of His favor upon whom He wills of His servants. So they returned with wrath upon wrath. And for the disbelievers is a humiliating punishment.” *Biʾsa* is the verb of dispraise, the opposite of *niʿma*. *Ishtarawū bihi anfusahum*, they purchased with it their souls — or they sold their souls for it; the verb *sharā* faces both ways. Mujāhid, as Ibn Kathīr cites, said they sold the truth for falsehood. Al-Suddī: they sold themselves. Either way the soul was on the counter. The price was *kufr* in what Allah sent down. A soul for a covering. 2:16’s trade of guidance for misguidance is here a trade of the self.

*Baghyan* is the motive: insolence, envy, resentment, a transgression of the chest. *An yunazzila Allāhu min faḍlihi ʿalā man yashāʾu min ʿibādihi*, that Allah should send down of His favor upon whom He wills of His servants. The *faḍl* is prophecy, the Book, the choosing of Muḥammad (ﷺ) from a people not theirs. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn named it *ḥasad*, envy of the Arabs, of Banū Hāshim, of a prophet whose lineage was not Israel’s. 2:90 is Iblīs’s “I am better than him” (7:12) in a scholarly key. Iblīs envied a clay; they envied a house of Ismāʿīl. The Lord who said “Allah knows best where He places His message” (6:124) is resented as if placement were a committee’s right. *Man yashāʾu min ʿibādihi* is *tawḥīd al-rubūbiyyah* in the field of gifts. To resent the Giver for a gift is to sit on a throne that is not yours.

*Fa-bāʾū bi-ghaḍabin ʿalā ghaḍab*, so they returned with wrath upon wrath. *Bāʾū* is to come back with a load, the verb of 2:61. Wrath stacked on wrath. Ibn al-Jawzī listed the *salaf*’s pairs: wrath for the calf and wrath for denying Muḥammad (ﷺ); wrath for ʿĪsā and wrath for Muḥammad; wrath for altering the Torah and wrath for denying the unlettered prophet; wrath for “Allah’s hand is chained” (5:64) and wrath for this covering. The verse does not need the pairs to be exclusive. It needs the image of a people who did not meet one anger and repent, but added a second. 2:61’s stamp is not a closed historical file. It can be doubled.

*Wa lil-kāfirīna ʿadhābun muhīn*, and for the disbelievers a humiliating punishment. *Muhīn* is that which humiliates, from *hawn*, lowliness. 2:85 promised *khizy* in the world and the harshest punishment in the next. 2:90 names the next as humiliating. Envy wanted a high place; the repayment is a lowering. *Jazāʾ min jins al-ʿamal*. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Beware of envy, for envy eats good deeds as fire eats wood” (Abū Dāwūd). He said he does not want the *ḥasad* of the one who envies a gift of Allah (الله) except in two: a man given Qur'an who stands with it, and a man given wealth who spends it (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:90 is envy of the Qur'an itself as a gift placed next door.

Theologically the verse is the creed of *al-ikhtiyār*, the Lord’s choosing. Prophecy is not an inheritance that a grandson can claim in court. “Is it they who distribute the mercy of your Lord?” (43:32). Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *ḥasad* is among the most destructive sins because it objects to the Decree while pretending to object to a person. The person is Muḥammad (ﷺ); the objection is to Allah (الله). *Baghyan an yunazzila Allāh* makes the resentment theological. Al-Rāzī noted that they did not say “we hate this man”; the Qur'an translated their hate into its true sentence: they hate that Allah sends down. A hate of a prophet is always a hate of a sending.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of the heart, warns this *ummah* against envying one another’s *faḍl*: knowledge, a *rizq*, a child, a *qirāʾah*. The Jews’ envy of prophecy is the mother; the Muslim’s envy of a brother’s gift is the daughter. Both object to *man yashāʾ*. The Prophet (ﷺ) taught, “Look at those below you, and do not look at those above you, lest you despise Allah’s favor upon you” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:90 is the looking-up that became *kufr*.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “None of you believes until he loves for his brother what he loves for himself” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Envy is the opposite love: he loves that a gift not be his brother’s. 2:90’s gift was the greatest gift, *nubuwwah*. If envy of that gift is wrath upon wrath, envy of a smaller *faḍl* is a spark of the same fire. Al-Ghazālī counted *ḥasad* among the destroyers, and 2:90 is the destroyer named as a sale of the self.

Ibn ʿAbbās said they knew he was a prophet and envied that the *risālah* left the Children of Israel. Knowledge plus envy is a more poisonous drink than ignorance plus envy. 2:93 will speak of a calf drunk into the heart; 2:90 is envy drunk into the heart. Both drinks command. Miserable is the commander.

For the reader the verse is a search for *baghy* in the chest. Do you resent a gift that went to another house, another ethnicity, another student, another imam? Miserable is the sale. Wrath can stack. The humiliating punishment is not only theirs. Envy is a fire that does not stay in the seventh century. The next verse will quote the slogan that envy wears as scholarship: “We believe in what was sent down to us,” and we cover what is beyond it, though it is the truth confirming what is with them — then the question: why did you kill the prophets of Allah before, if you are believers?


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:91

> When it is said to them: “Believe in what Allah has revealed,” they reply, “We only believe in what was sent down to us,” and they deny what came afterwards, though it is the truth confirming their own Scriptures! Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Why then did you kill Allah’s prophets before, if you are ˹truly˺ believers?”

**Commentary:**

Envy now has a creed-sentence: *wa idhā qīla lahum āminū bimā anzala Allāhu qālū nuʾminu bimā unzila ʿalaynā wa yakfurūna bimā warāʾahu wa huwa al-ḥaqqu muṣaddiqan limā maʿahum qul fa-lima taqtulūna anbiyāʾa Allāhi min qablu in kuntum muʾminīn*, “And when it is said to them, ‘Believe in what Allah has sent down,’ they say, ‘We believe in what was sent down upon us,’ and they cover what is beyond it, though it is the truth, confirming what is with them. Say: ‘Why then did you kill the prophets of Allah before, if you were believers?’” *Āminū bimā anzala Allāh* is the call of 2:4, general: whatever He sent down. Their answer restricts: *bimā unzila ʿalaynā*, upon us, to us, our sending, our language, our line. *Innamā* is not in the Arabic, but the restriction is in the claim. Ibn ʿAbbās said they meant the Torah and what came to the prophets of Israel, and they refused the Qur'an. Al-Ṭabarī: they made faith a geography of revelation.

*Wa yakfurūna bimā warāʾahu*, and they cover what is behind it, beyond it, after it. *Warāʾ* is the beyond of a wall they have built at the last page of their canon. *Wa huwa al-ḥaqq muṣaddiqan limā maʿahum*, and it — the beyond — is the truth, confirming what is with them. The confirmation is 2:41, 2:89, 2:97. A Book that confirms cannot be “beyond” in the sense of foreign. It is the same speech’s next sentence. To refuse a confirmation is to refuse the confirmed. Ibn Kathīr notes the self-contradiction: they claim faith in what is with them, and the thing they refuse testifies for what is with them. The testimony is not accepted because the witness is not of the tribe.

Then the Prophet (ﷺ) is told *qul*, say: *fa-lima taqtulūna anbiyāʾa Allāhi min qablu in kuntum muʾminīn*, why then do you kill the prophets of Allah before, if you are believers? The verb is imperfect, *taqtulūn*, you kill, though the killings are past, because the heirs of a killing who approve it are in the grammar of the doers, as 2:61’s “you killed” addressed a later generation. The prophets killed were Israel’s own — Yaḥyā, and others the *salaf* named — sent down “upon us,” inside the wall they now claim. If faith in what was sent down to them were real, the killers of those prophets would be disowned, not inherited as a silence. The question is a *ilzām*, a forcing of a contradiction. You do not believe in what was sent down to you. You believe in a selection that never includes a prophet who pinches. 2:87’s *farīqan kadhdhabtum wa farīqan taqtulūn* is this question’s evidence.

The Prophet (ﷺ) lived the slogan. They asked him to judge by the Torah in a case of *zinā*, then hid the stoning-verse with a hand (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). “We believe in what was sent down to us” did not include a ḥadd upon a noble. He had the hand lifted. 2:91 is that courtroom as a theology. He also said, “Do not believe the People of the Book and do not deny them, but say: we believe in Allah and what was revealed to us and what was revealed to you” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). This *ummah*’s faith is the general of *bimā anzala Allāh*, not the wall of *ʿalaynā*.

Theologically the verse is against a confessional nationalism. Faith is not “our revelation” as property. Faith is “what Allah sent down” as speech. A Christian who says we believe only in the Gospel, a Muslim who says we believe only in the verses that match a party, a modernist who says we believe only in the ethics and not the unseen — all speak 2:91’s *nuʾminu bimā unzila ʿalaynā* with new walls. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that every *hawā* builds a canon inside the canon. The killing of prophets is the extreme of that building: when the living word will not fit the wall, the speaker is removed. Al-Rāzī noted *in kuntum muʾminīn* as a conditional that denies the condition: if you were believers you would not have killed; you killed; you are not.

Al-Qurṭubī hears the verse as a warning to this *ummah* about its own scholars who “believe in” the Qur'an and then kill a reminder with a weak *taʾwīl*, a silencing, a *tabdīʿ* of a man who recites an unwelcome āyah. Killing has degrees. The question *lima taqtulūn* searches them all. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever revives a *sunnah* that had died…” and he also said this *ummah* would follow the Jews and Christians span by span. 2:91 is one span: our Book, not the next command.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “I am the nearest of people to the son of Mary, and the prophets are paternal brothers; their mothers differ and their religion is one” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). *Bimā anzala Allāh* is that one religion as a chain. *Bimā unzila ʿalaynā* is a mother treated as a wall. Al-Ṭabarī records that they refused ʿĪsā (peace be upon him) with the same slogan they refused Muḥammad (ﷺ): not sent down upon us in the way we define us. Two refusals, one grammar. 2:87 already listed both tools: denial of some, killing of others. 2:91 asks the killers to explain themselves as believers in their own sending.

For the reader the verse is a check of your wall. What do you mean by “I believe in Islam”? All that Allah (الله) sent down, or what was sent down upon your taste? The killing of prophets is not your biography; the selection might be. The next verse will nail the slogan with a scene they cannot outsource: Mūsā came to you with clear proofs, then you took the calf in his absence, while you were wrongdoers — a crime inside “what was sent down to us,” with no Arab prophet in the room.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:92

> Indeed, Moses came to you with clear proofs, then you worshipped the calf in his absence, acting wrongfully.

**Commentary:**

The wall of “we believe in what was sent down to us” is met with a crime inside the wall: *wa la-qad jāʾakum Mūsā bil-bayyināti thumma ittakhadhtumu al-ʿijla min baʿdihi wa antum ẓālimūn*, “And Moses had certainly come to you with the clear proofs, then you took the calf after him, while you were wrongdoers.” *La-qad* is the oath’s emphasis: it is not disputed. *Jāʾakum Mūsā bil-bayyināt*, Mūsā came to you with the clear proofs — the staff, the white hand, the sea, the mountain, the tablets, the twelve springs, the manna. 2:51 has already told the taking of the calf; 2:54 the *tawbah*; 2:92 returns to it as a witness against 2:91. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn treat the return as *ilzām*: you claim faith in your prophet; your prophet came with *bayyināt*; you worshipped a calf when he was gone. The faith that kills later prophets is the faith that could not survive a prophet’s absence.

*Thumma ittakhadhtum al-ʿijl min baʿdihi*, then you took the calf after him. *Min baʿdihi* is after him, after his going to the appointment, the forty nights of 2:51. Absence is a test of whether the messenger or the Sender was worshipped. When the messenger is the idol, his absence is a vacancy for another idol. When the Sender is worshipped, absence is a patience. They took a calf of jewelry, a lowing statue, as 7:148 describes. *Ittakhadh* is the verb of taking a god, the same verb as taking the calf in 2:51. *Wa antum ẓālimūn*, and you were wrongdoers — a ḥāl: the taking was not a confusion of innocents; it was *ẓulm*, the placing of worship in the wrong place, the *ẓulm* that 31:13 said is *shirk*.

The Prophet (ﷺ) feared a calf for this *ummah* of another metal. He said, “Do not sit on graves and do not pray toward them” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), closing a door of absence-worship. He said, “Do not exaggerate in praising me as the Christians exaggerated in praising the son of Mary; I am a servant, so say: the servant of Allah and His messenger” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). Absence of a prophet is not a license for a statue, a grave, a party, a *hawā*. 2:92 is the mother of that fear. He also told the story of the Children of Israel’s calf as this sūrah tells it, not as a taunt of blood but as a *mawʿiẓah* of a heart that needs a visible god when the reminder is on a mountain.

Theologically the verse is about *bayyināt* that do not automatically prevent *shirk*. Clear proofs came. Then a calf. 2:74’s hardness after a corpse sat up is the same law: signs are not grace if the chest is a *hawā*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the calf is every object the heart takes when the command is delayed, when the prophet is “on the mountain,” when the *sharīʿah* feels absent from a room. A screen, a leader, a nation, a self. Al-Rāzī noted *thumma* as a scandal of sequence: after proofs, not before. Ignorance before a sign is a lesser *ẓulm*. *Ẓulm* after *bayyināt* is 2:92.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, hears the verse against those who say “we follow our *imām*” in a way that makes the *imām* a calf when the texts are on the mountain. *Min baʿdihi* is any after of a reminder. The few of 2:83 who did not turn are the ones who waited for Mūsā (peace be upon him) without a statue — Hārūn (peace be upon him) forbade them (7:150) and they overcame him. The *ẓālimūn* are the majority that 2:83 already named as the turning.

The calf will, in the next verse, be said to have been drunk into their hearts. 2:92 is the act; 2:93 will be the absorption. Together they answer 2:91: you did not believe in what was sent down to you. You believed in a taste that could kill a prophet later and worship a calf now. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The thing I fear most for you is the minor *shirk*.” They said, “What is it?” He said, “Showing off” (Aḥmad). Showing off is a small calf of the gaze. 2:92 is the large calf of the desert. Both are *ittikhādh* in an absence of sincerity.

Al-Ṭabarī gathers that the *bayyināt* included the tablets with the writing of Allah (الله), so that the calf was taken against a still-warm Book. A Book in the camp and a statue in the camp is 2:85’s parts in a liturgical key: we believe in the tablets; we dance around the gold. Ibn Kathīr notes that *min baʿdihi* is a shame of timing. They did not wait a generation. They waited a mountain-trip.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Every nation has a *fitnah*, and the *fitnah* of my *ummah* is wealth” (al-Tirmidhī). Israel’s calf was wealth melted. This *ummah*’s calf may be wealth unmelted, still in a pocket, worshipped without a statue. 2:92 is not safe to read as archaeology.

Hārūn (peace be upon him) said, “O my people, you are only being tested by it, and your Lord is the Most Merciful, so follow me and obey my command” (20:90). They said they would not stop until Mūsā returned. 2:92 is that wait as a *ẓulm*. A test named by a prophet in the camp was still taken as a god.

For the reader the verse is a question about your prophet’s absence. When the recitation stops, when Ramaḍān ends, when the shaykh is on a journey, what do you take? A calf is not always gold. It is whatever you worship because you cannot wait. Mūsā (peace be upon him) came with *bayyināt*. This *ummah* has a Book that does not leave. The absence is only the absence of your attention. Do not fill it with a statue of a habit. The next verse will raise the mountain again, as 2:63 did, and will record their answer this time as “we hear and we disobey,” and will say the calf was made to drink into their hearts by their *kufr* — then: say, how evil is what your faith commands you, if you are believers.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:93

> And when We took your covenant and raised the mountain above you ˹saying˺, “Hold firmly to that ˹Scripture˺ which We have given you and obey,” they answered, “We hear and disobey.” The love of the calf was rooted in their hearts because of their disbelief. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “How evil is what your ˹so-called˺ belief prompts you to do, if you ˹actually˺ believe ˹in the Torah˺!”

**Commentary:**

The mountain of 2:63 returns with a worse answer: *wa idh akhadhnā mīthāqakum wa rafaʿnā fawqakumu al-ṭūra khudhū mā ātaynākum bi-quwwatin wa ismaʿū qālū samiʿnā wa ʿaṣaynā wa ushribū fī qulūbihimu al-ʿijla bi-kufrihim qul biʾsa mā yaʾmurukum bihi īmānukum in kuntum muʾminīn*, “And when We took your covenant and raised the mountain above you: ‘Take what We have given you with strength, and hear.’ They said, ‘We hear and we disobey.’ And they were made to drink the calf into their hearts by their disbelief. Say: ‘How miserable is what your faith commands you, if you are believers.’” 2:63 commanded *khudhū* and *udhkurū*; here *khudhū* and *ismaʿū*, take and hear. Hearing is the gate of 2:75. They answer with a pairing that is almost a mockery of the later *samiʿnā wa aṭaʿnā* of this *ummah* (2:285): *samiʿnā wa ʿaṣaynā*, we heard and we disobeyed. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī records, said some uttered it, and some uttered it in their deeds though their tongues said otherwise. The Qur'an records the deed as a saying, because a life of ʿiṣyān is a sentence.

*Wa ushribū fī qulūbihimu al-ʿijl*, and they were made to drink the calf in their hearts. *Ishrāb* is to be given drink, to be soaked. The calf is not only a statue in a valley. It is a liquid in a chest. Al-Rāghib and the mufassirūn loved the image: love of the calf entered as drink enters, mixed with blood, becoming temperament. *Bi-kufrihim*, because of their disbelief — the cause of the soaking. *Kufr* makes a space; the calf fills it. Ibn Kathīr cites the *salaf*: their love of the calf was mixed into their hearts. Yasmin of a later century would not be needed to see that an addiction is a drink. The Qur'an said it of a god of gold.

The Prophet (ﷺ) is told *qul biʾsa mā yaʾmurukum bihi īmānukum in kuntum muʾminīn*, say: miserable is what your faith commands you, if you are believers. The *īmān* they claimed in 2:91 — faith in what was sent down to us — is now asked to look at its fruits: a calf in the heart, a hearing that disobeys, a killing of prophets. Faith is tested by what it commands. A faith that commands a calf is a miserable commander. *In kuntum muʾminīn* again denies the condition. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The lawful is clear and the unlawful is clear” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A faith that commands the unclear as a calf is not the faith of a Book taken with strength.

This *ummah* was taught the opposite pairing. “The Messenger has believed… and they say, ‘We hear and we obey’” (2:285). The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “When I command you a thing, do of it as much as you can; when I forbid you a thing, avoid it” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). *Samiʿnā wa aṭaʿnā* is the Fātiḥah’s worship in two verbs. 2:93 is the Fātiḥah inverted. He also said, “There is a piece of flesh in the body; if it is sound, the body is sound” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A heart that has drunk a calf is not sound. The drink must be expelled by *tawbah* of 2:54’s kind: killing the calf in the chest, even if the statue is already dust.

Theologically the verse is about interiorization of *shirk*. A public repentance can leave a private drink. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that idols migrate from valleys to hearts, and that the last idol is the self. *Ushribū* is that migration. Al-Rāzī noted that *bi-kufrihim* prevents a reading of compulsion: they were not forced to love the calf; their *kufr* was the cup. Allah (الله) creates the consequence; they chose the cause. Ahl al-Sunnah hold both. Al-Qurṭubī hears a warning to every community that recites “we hear” and lives “we disobey”: the recitation becomes 2:93’s first verb only.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “There will be a people who recite the Qur'an, but it will not pass their throats” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). *Samiʿnā* without *aṭaʿnā* is a recitation that stops at the throat. 2:93’s people heard a mountain and disobeyed; this *ummah* may hear a miḥrāb and disobey. The drink in the heart will not be gold; it will be whatever *kufr* of a blessing has mixed. He taught the *duʿāʾ* of purifying the heart, and he said, “O Allah, wash my sins with water, snow, and hail” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A calf that has been drunk needs a washing, not a slogan of 2:91.

Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that love, when it enters as a drink, becomes a commander of the limbs. 2:93 asks whose faith is commanding: the mountain’s, or the calf’s. The answer is in the deeds, as 2:94 will demand a deed of a wish.

Al-Qurṭubī records that some of the *salaf* said *ushribū* is a metaphor of love’s mixing, and some said it is the punishment of a love they chose. Both stand. A love chosen becomes a climate, and a climate is a commander. 2:93 asks the claimed *īmān* to look at its calf-commands and be ashamed.

For the reader the verse is a cup to inspect. What have you been made to drink — not by a desert statue, by a repetition, a feed, a resentment of 2:90, a selection of 2:91? A calf in the heart will command. Miserable is that commander. If you believe, let belief command what the mountain said: take with strength, and hear. The next verse will challenge another fruit of the claimed faith: if the Home of the Hereafter with Allah is exclusively yours among the people, then wish for death, if you are truthful. A heart that has drunk a calf will not wish to meet the Lord of the mountain.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:94

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “If the ˹eternal˺ Home of the Hereafter with Allah is exclusively for you ˹Israelites˺ out of all humanity, then wish for death if what you say is true!”

**Commentary:**
