The claims of numbered days, of a wrapped heart, of a faith in “what was sent down to us,” are now summoned to a simple court: *qul in kānat lakumu al-dāru al-ākhiratu ʿinda Allāhi khāliṣatan min dūni al-nāsi fa-tamannawū al-mawta in kuntum ṣādiqīn*, “Say: if the Last Home with Allah is exclusively yours, apart from the people, then wish for death if you are truthful.” *Al-dār al-ākhirah* is the Last Abode, the Garden as a house. *Khāliṣatan*, pure, unshared, refined for you. *Min dūni al-nās*, apart from the people, without the rest of humanity. This is the slogan of 2:80 and of 5:18 — “we are the children of Allah and His beloved” — made into a real-estate claim. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, said they claimed no Garden except for them. The Prophet (ﷺ) is told to test the claim with a wish.

*Fa-tamannawū al-mawt*, then wish death. *Tamannī* is to desire, the *umniyyah* of 2:78 now demanded as a proof. If the House is yours, the door to it is death; the truthful would ask for the door. The mufassirūn, including Ibn ʿAbbās in a report Ibn Isḥāq carried, said this was a *mubāhalah* of a kind, a calling-down: if they wished death, they would die. They did not wish. The next verse will say they will never wish it because of what their hands have sent ahead. The test is therefore already a disclosure. A people who cling to a feud’s honor in 2:85 and a calf in 2:93 will not ask to leave a world they have purchased with the Hereafter (2:86).

The Prophet (ﷺ) forbade his *ummah* to wish for death because of a harm: “None of you should wish for death because of a harm that has befallen him. If he must, let him say: O Allah, keep me alive as long as life is better for me, and take me if death is better for me” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The prohibition is for the believer who is tired, not for the pretender who claims a private Garden. 2:94 is a challenge to a slogan, not a *sunnah* of despair. He also said, “Whoever loves to meet Allah, Allah loves to meet him; whoever hates to meet Allah, Allah hates to meet him” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). ʿĀʾishah (may Allah be pleased with her) feared that this meant hating death, and he explained that the believer, at death, is given good news and loves to meet, and the disbeliever is given bad news and hates to meet. 2:94 asks them to love a meeting they have described as theirs. They will not.

Theologically the verse is the ethics of exclusive hope. Hope that shuts the door on *al-nās* is not the hope of 2:62. 2:62 opened a door by faith and work, not by blood. 2:94 closes a false door by a wish they cannot make. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the soul’s love of this world is the most honest commentator on its claims about the next. A tongue that says “the Garden is ours” and a hand that will not let go of a thousand years (2:96) are two witnesses who disagree. The hand is believed. Al-Rāzī noted *khāliṣatan min dūni al-nās* as the grammar of pride: not “we hope to be of the Garden” but “the Garden is a private house.” Pride cannot wish for a door that equalizes: death is a door every lineage uses.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of *daʿwah*, hears a method: when a people claim a salvation that costs them nothing, ask for a cost. The cost here is a wish. This *ummah* is not asked to wish for death as a proof; it is asked to live as those who would not be ashamed to meet. “Say, ‘Indeed, my prayer, my sacrifice, my living and my dying are for Allah, Lord of the worlds’” (6:162). That sentence is the believer’s answer to 2:94 without the challenge’s sarcasm.

Al-Ṭabarī records from Ibn ʿAbbās that the Prophet (ﷺ) said to them, “If you are truthful, then say: O Allah, take us,” and they refused, because they knew. The refusal is 2:95. 2:94 is only the offer of a proof they themselves could have supplied. A claim that cannot bear a wish is a claim of 2:78’s *amānī*.

The Prophet (ﷺ) used to say, “By Allah, I seek Allah’s forgiveness and repent to Him more than seventy times a day” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). The best of creation (ﷺ) did not claim a private House that made death a bargain. He sought a meeting as a servant. 2:94’s addressees claimed a House as a property. Property-talk about the Garden is a sign that the Garden has been misunderstood as a club.

Ibn Kathīr cites that some of the Christians made a similar exclusive claim, and 5:18 answers both: “Say, why then does He punish you for your sins?” Exclusive hope that cannot face a punishment is 2:80 again. 2:94 asks for a wish instead of a number of days. Both tests fail the same chest.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “None of you believes until I am more beloved to him than his father, his child, and all people” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Love of a meeting with Allah (الله) is the root of that love. 2:94’s people loved a House as a title and did not love a meeting as a servant. Titles do not wish to die. Servants can.

For the reader the verse confiscates every “we are saved because we are we.” If the Home were exclusively yours, you would not fear a meeting. You fear, so inspect what your hands have done. The next verse will make the refusal of the wish a statement of knowledge: they will never wish it because of what their hands have sent forth, and Allah knows the wrongdoers. 2:94 is the challenge. 2:95 is the reason the challenge is not taken.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:95

> But they will never wish for that because of what their hands have done. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of the wrongdoers.

**Commentary:**

The challenge is already lost: *wa lan yatamannawhu abadan bimā qaddamat aydīhim wa Allāhu ʿalīmun bil-ẓālimīn*, “And they will never wish it, ever, because of what their hands have sent forward. And Allah is Knowing of the wrongdoers.” *Lan* with *abadan* is a negation that stretches: they will not, at any time, wish death as a door to a private Garden. Ibn ʿAbbās said: they know what they have done of denying Muḥammad (ﷺ), of altering, of killing prophets, of the calf, and a soul that knows its file does not ask for the court. Al-Ṭabarī: *mā qaddamat aydīhim* is their sins. The hands of 2:79 that wrote a Book for a price are the hands here that have sent a file ahead. Hands are named because the crimes were not only thoughts: a knife on a prophet, a pen on a verse, a channel on a Sabbath, a feud’s spear.

*Qaddamat* is to send forward, the verb of 82:5, “what a soul has sent forward and left behind.” Deeds arrive before the doer. The wish for death would be a wish to catch up with the file. They will not wish it because the file is a *ẓulm*. *Wa Allāhu ʿalīmun bil-ẓālimīn*, and Allah is Knowing of the wrongdoers. *ʿAlīm* is the Name of 2:29, 2:77. The *ẓālimūn* of 2:92’s calf are known. Knowledge here is not information. It is a threat: the file they will not wish to meet is already read. Ibn Kathīr links the verse to the fact that none of them took the *mubāhalah*; the Prophet (ﷺ) said, in reports the mufassirūn carry, that had they wished death they would have died and seen their seats in the Fire. Whether every wording of those reports is independently the highest rank, the Qur'an’s *lan…abadan* is enough. History confirmed it: they did not wish.

The Prophet (ﷺ) taught the believer a different relation to what the hands have sent. “Take account of yourselves before you are taken to account” (the meaning of ʿUmar’s saying, lived as a *sunnah* of *muḥāsabah*). He said, “Follow a bad deed with a good deed and it will wipe it” (al-Tirmidhī). Hands can send forward a *tawbah* as they sent a *ẓulm*. 2:95 is about hands that will not. He also said, “The bankrupt of my *ummah* is one who comes on the Day of Resurrection with prayer, fasting, and zakāt, but he has insulted this one, slandered that one, taken that one’s wealth, shed that one’s blood, and struck that one; so this one is given from his good deeds, and that one, and if his good deeds are exhausted, their sins are taken and placed upon him, and he is thrown in the Fire” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). That bankruptcy is *mā qaddamat aydīhim* in this *ummah*’s tongue. Numbered days will not cover it; a wish for a private Garden will not cover it.

Theologically the verse is the honesty of fear. Fear of death is not always cowardice. Sometimes it is knowledge. The wrongdoers of 2:95 know enough to refuse a door. Their knowledge is not *īmān*; it is the *ʿaql* of 2:75 that did not become following. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that among the punishments of sin is that it makes the meeting hated, and among the rewards of *tawbah* is that it makes the meeting loved. 2:95 is the first. Al-Rāzī noted *abadan* as a seal upon 2:80: they will not even wish for a quick Fire of days, because wishing death to reach a Garden is not the same as wishing a brief burn; they will do neither, because both require a meeting.

Al-Qurṭubī hears *ʿalīmun bil-ẓālimīn* as a close that forbids despair of the Lord’s knowledge and forbids hope in hiding. The hands are public in His sight even when a town calls the feud honor. 2:77 asked if they knew He knows the secret. 2:95 says He knows the wrongdoers as a class and as persons. The next verse will show the other face of refusing death: not only fear of a file, but greed of a life — clinging more than any people, even more than polytheists, each wishing a thousand years, which would not save from the punishment. 2:95 is the file. 2:96 is the clutch.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The grave is the first stage of the Hereafter; whoever is saved from it, what comes after is easier, and if he is not saved, what comes after is harder” (al-Tirmidhī, Aḥmad). Hands send forward to a grave before they send to a Garden. 2:95’s people will not wish a door that begins with a grave they have furnished with *ẓulm*. He also said, “Remember much the destroyer of pleasures: death” (al-Tirmidhī). Remembrance of death is the believer’s *muḥāsabah*; refusal even to wish it as a test is the wrongdoer’s *kitmān* of his own file.

Al-Rāghib noted that *qaddamat aydīhim* makes the hands a subject so that no one will say “it was only a thought.” 2:79’s hands wrote; 2:95’s hands sent. Writing is a sending. Killing is a sending. A feud’s spear is a sending. The Knowing of the wrongdoers has the inventory.

Ibn Kathīr notes that this refusal of a wish is a *muʿjizah* of a kind: a people told to wish, in public, a thing that would have cost them if they were lying, and they did not wish. The Qur'an recorded the *lan* before the years confirmed it. Hands that know are hands that stay.

For the reader the verse is a reason to love a meeting or to fear one honestly. If you will not wish death because of a file, do not claim a private House. Repair what the hands have sent while the hands still move. Allah (الله) is Knowing of the wrongdoers; He is also *Tawwāb* to those who return. 2:95 does not name the *Tawwāb* because it is addressing a slogan of exclusivity, not a sinner who asks. You, if you ask, have another verse: 39:53. Use it before *lan yatamannawhu abadan* becomes a description of you.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:96

> You will surely find them clinging to life more eagerly than any other people, even more than polytheists. Each one of them wishes to live a thousand years. But even if they were to live that long, it would not save them from the punishment. And Allah is All-Seeing of what they do.

**Commentary:**

The refusal of a wish for death is now a greed of remaining: *wa la-tajidannahum aḥraṣa al-nāsi ʿalā ḥayātin wa min alladhīna ashrakū yawaddu aḥaduhum law yuʿammaru alfa sanatin wa mā huwa bi-muzazḥziḥihi min al-ʿadhābi an yuʿammar wa Allāhu baṣīrun bimā yaʿmalūn*, “And you will surely find them the greediest of people for a life, and from those who associated; each one of them wishes he could be given a thousand years. And it would not budge him from the punishment that he is given long life. And Allah is Seeing of what they do.” *La-tajidannahum* is an oath of a finding: you will find them, O Prophet, O reciter. *Aḥraṣa al-nās ʿalā ḥayāh*, the most eager of people upon a life — *ḥayāh* indefinite, a life, any prolongation, not a life of meaning. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn said the Jews of that claim clung to this world because they knew their file. Al-Ṭabarī: greed for life is a sign of hating the meeting.

Then a comparison that stings: *wa min alladhīna ashrakū*, and than those who associated. The polytheist of Makkah, who denied a resurrection, might cling because he thinks death is the end; clinging is his creed. The people of a Book, who describe a Garden as theirs, cling more. Ibn al-Jawzī recorded that some of the *salaf* said *alladhīna ashrakū* here are the Magians, who wished long life, or the Arab pagans. The point is the comparative: a people of resurrection-talk greedier than a people of dust-talk. 2:96 is a psychological miracle of a verse. Knowledge of a Fire, without *tawbah*, does not loosen the hand from the world. It tightens it. One more year is one more year not in the file’s court.

*Yawaddu aḥaduhum law yuʿammaru alfa sanah*, each one of them would love to be given life of a thousand years. A thousand is a round plenty, the number of 32:5’s day, of 97:3’s night in another scale. *Wa mā huwa bi-muzazḥziḥihi min al-ʿadhāb an yuʿammar*, and he is not one to budge him from the punishment, that he is given long life. *Zazḥzaha* is to shift, to nudge a thing from its place. A thousand years do not nudge a Fire by a handspan. 2:80’s numbered days of Fire are answered by numbered years of life that do not save. The calendar they wanted in the next world they now want in this one; neither calendar is a covenant.

The Prophet (ﷺ) taught the opposite greed. “Be in the world as a stranger or a traveler” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). “The smart one is he who takes his soul to account and works for what is after death” (al-Tirmidhī). He said, “The heart of an old man remains young in two: love of the world and long hope” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:96 is that youth of the heart as a people. He also said that this world compared to the next is as a finger dipped in the sea (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A thousand years of dipping do not empty a Fire. *Wa Allāhu baṣīrun bimā yaʿmalūn*, and Allah is Seeing of what they do. *Baṣīr* after *ʿalīm* of 2:95: knowledge of the wrongdoers, seeing of the deeds. The clinging is seen. The extra year is seen. The not-saving is already decreed.

Theologically the verse is about *ṭūl al-amal*, long hope, as a disease. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that long hope is a tree whose water is love of the world, and whose fruit is postponement of *tawbah*. A thousand years is the tree’s fantasy. Al-Rāzī noted that the polytheist’s clinging is consistent with his denial; the Book-people’s clinging is a contradiction of their slogan, and contradictions are heavier. Al-Qurṭubī, as a preacher, hears a warning to the rich of this *ummah*: a long building, a long saving, a medicine that buys a decade, without a file repaired, is 2:96’s thousand in a smaller number. The medicine is not forbidden. The fantasy that a decade nudges a Fire is.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “I have not seen the like of the Fire: the one who flees it sleeps, and the like of the Garden: the one who seeks it sleeps” (al-Tirmidhī). 2:96 is a people who neither flee nor seek; they clutch a life. Sleep of the heedless is a thousand years in the imagination. He said, “Take advantage of five before five: your youth before your old age, your health before your illness, your wealth before your poverty, your free time before your busyness, and your life before your death” (al-Ḥākim). The five are the opposite of *alfa sanah* as a fantasy. They are a life used, not a life hoarded.

Ibn ʿAbbās said some of the Magians would say “ten thousand years” in blessing a man. The Qur'an chose a thousand as a plenty that still fails. Plenty fails. *Baṣīr* sees the failing wish as it sees the deed.

Al-Qurṭubī writes that greed for a life without obedience is a greed for more sin-time. A thousand years of 2:85’s split would be a thousand years of parts. The punishment would only wait. Seeing of deeds includes seeing of delays.

For the reader the verse is a mirror in a clinic, in a bank, in a midnight of plans. How many years do you want, and for what? If the years are to repent, they are a mercy of 2:64’s *faḍl*. If they are to postpone a meeting, they will not *zazḥzih* you. Allah (الله) is Seeing. The next verse will turn from their greed of life to their hate of a means: Jibrīl, who brought the Book to the Prophet’s (ﷺ) heart by Allah’s leave, confirming, a guidance and a good news for the believers. A people who want a thousand years and hate the angel of revelation have chosen a life without a message.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:97

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Whoever is an enemy of Gabriel should know that he revealed this ˹Quran˺ to your heart by Allah’s Will, confirming what came before it—a guide and good news for the believers.”

**Commentary:**

A new objection, petty and theological at once, is answered: *qul man kāna ʿaduwwan li-Jibrīla fa-innahu nazzalahu ʿalā qalbika bi-idhni Allāhi muṣaddiqan limā bayna yadayhi wa hudan wa bushrā lil-muʾminīn*, “Say: whoever is an enemy of Jibrīl — then indeed he has sent it down upon your heart by the permission of Allah, confirming what is before it, and a guidance and good news for the believers.” Al-Ṭabarī records the agreement of the mufassirūn that 2:97–98 were sent down because some of the Jews of Madinah said Jibrīl was their enemy — the angel of severity, of ruin, of drought, of a Book that named their faults — and that Mīkāʾīl was their friend, the angel of rain and mercy. Ibn ʿAbbās, in reports Ibn Kathīr cites, said they told the Prophet (ﷺ) that they would follow him if his angel were Mīkāʾīl. They made *nubuwwah* a matter of which secretary they liked. The verse refuses the preference. The secretary of this Book is Jibrīl. The permission is Allah’s (الله).

*Nazzalahu ʿalā qalbika*, he sent it down upon your heart. The intensive *nazzala* is the Qur'an's staged descent. The place of descent is the *qalb* of the Prophet (ﷺ), not a page first, a chest first. “The Trustworthy Spirit has brought it down upon your heart, that you may be of the warners” (26:193–194). Enmity of Jibrīl is enmity of what landed on that heart. *Bi-idhni Allāh*, by Allah’s leave: Jibrīl does not freelance. An enemy of the messenger-angel is an enemy of the One who gave leave. Ibn Kathīr notes that they thought they could love the Lord and hate His envoy of *waḥy*. 2:97 makes the thought impossible.

*Muṣaddiqan limā bayna yadayhi*, confirming what is before it — the Torah’s remaining truth, the Gospel’s remaining truth, the line of 2:41 and 2:89. *Wa hudan wa bushrā lil-muʾminīn*, and a guidance and good news for the believers. The Book that Jibrīl brought is 2:2’s *hudā* and a *bushrā* of the Garden of 2:82. The beneficiaries are named: the believers. A people who hate the carrier will not take the good news. Al-Qurṭubī writes that *lil-muʾminīn* is a restriction of benefit, not of truth: the Book is true upon all; it guides and gladdens those who believe. 2:26’s parable that guides many and leads many astray is this restriction in another image.

The Prophet (ﷺ) described Jibrīl (peace be upon him) as he saw him: six hundred wings, filling the horizon (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said Jibrīl came to him in the form of a man, as in the hadith of *īmān* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), and in his true form twice. He loved Jibrīl as a believer loves the carrier of his Lord’s speech. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām asked, among his three questions, about the first sign of the Hour, and about the first food of the people of the Garden, and about the child resembling father or mother; the Prophet (ﷺ) said Jibrīl has just informed him, and Ibn Salām said: Jibrīl is the enemy of the Jews (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:97 is that enmity answered from heaven.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd* of the means of revelation. You do not choose the angel. You do not filter *waḥy* by the temperament you project upon a spirit. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that hating a means Allah (الله) chose is hating the choice. Al-Rāzī noted *ʿalā qalbika* as an honor of the Prophet (ﷺ): the heart that received is a heart Allah (الله) expanded (94:1). To be an enemy of the one who poured upon that heart is to stand outside the *bushrā*. This *ummah* is tried by a smaller enmity: hating a scholar who brings a ḥadīth, a verse that pinches, because the carrier is not of our party. 2:97’s logic still holds. The leave is Allah’s.

Al-Bukhārī records that ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām said Jibrīl is the enemy of the Jews because he brings fighting and severity. The verse does not debate the job-description. It names the leave. If Jibrīl brings a severity, he brings it *bi-idhni Allāh*. Hating a severity that is permitted is hating the Permitter. Ibn Kathīr adds that some of them said Jibrīl revealed their secrets to Muḥammad (ﷺ). 2:77 has already said the Lord knows the secrets without a leak. Jibrīl is not a spy they can fire. He is a servant of a Lord who does not ask their human resources.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Jibrīl came to me and gave me the choice of half of my *ummah* entering the Garden or the intercession; I chose the intercession” in reports of that meaning, and he said Jibrīl prayed with him at the House, teaching the times (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The angel they hated is the angel of this *ummah*’s prayer-clock and of its hope of *shafāʿah*. 2:97 is therefore a verse of love for the believers: *bushrā*. Enmity of Jibrīl is a walking-out of that good news.

Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the heart of the Prophet (ﷺ) is the most protected of chests, and that what landed there is protected with it (15:9). To be an enemy of the landing is to fight a guarded thing. The fight fails. The enemy remains an enemy of Allah (الله) as 2:98 will say.

For the reader the verse is a command to love the carriers of what Allah (الله) sent down: Jibrīl, the Prophet (ﷺ), the reciter who does not flatter you. Enmity of the carrier with a claim of love of the content is 2:97’s Jews in a new coat. The next verse will widen the enmity: whoever is an enemy of Allah, His angels, His messengers, Jibrīl and Mīkāʾīl — then Allah is the enemy of the disbelievers. You cannot keep Mīkāʾīl as a pet and hate Jibrīl as a foe. The court of angels is one court.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:98

> Whoever is an enemy of Allah, His angels, His messengers, Gabriel, and Michael, then ˹let them know that˺ Allah is certainly the enemy of the disbelievers.

**Commentary:**

The preference among angels is closed with a list: *man kāna ʿaduwwan lillāhi wa malāʾikatihi wa rusulihi wa Jibrīla wa Mīkāla fa-inna Allāha ʿaduwwun lil-kāfirīn*, “Whoever is an enemy of Allah, and His angels, and His messengers, and Jibrīl and Mīkāl — then indeed Allah is an enemy of the disbelievers.” The list begins with Allah (الله) so that no one will think enmity of an angel is a side-matter. It includes *malāʾikah* as a genus, *rusul* as the human envoys, then names the two angels they tried to split. Jibrīl and Mīkāʾīl are not rivals in a ministry of rain and war. They are two servants of one Lord. Ibn ʿAbbās said Mīkāʾīl is the angel of rain and plants; Jibrīl of *waḥy* and support of prophets. Both are *malak*. Loving one and hating the other is a child’s game in a court of *tawḥīd*.

Al-Ṭabarī notes the naming of the two after the genus as a *tashrīf*, an honor of mention, and as a reply: you will not keep Mīkāʾīl. The one who hates Jibrīl has hated the genus, the messengers, and the Sender. *Fa-inna Allāha ʿaduwwun lil-kāfirīn*, then Allah is an enemy of the disbelievers. The title they gave Jibrīl — enemy — is returned as a title of the Lord toward them. *Jazāʾ min jins al-ʿamal* again, as 2:15 returned mockery. Enmity from Allah (الله) is not a passion. It is a judgment of exclusion from mercy, a *laʿn* of 2:88–89 in the language of war. Ibn Kathīr: whoever opposes one of these has opposed all, because they are a single *wilāyah*, a single allegiance.

The Prophet (ﷺ) taught the love of all the angels as part of *īmān*. The hadith of Jibrīl defines faith as belief in Allah, His angels, His books, His messengers, the Last Day, and the Decree (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:98 is that definition as a warning: enmity of a part is *kufr*. He forbade insulting time, because Allah (الله) is time in the sense of the hadith qudsī (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī); he forbade a theology of picking among the unseen servants. This *ummah* says in the Fātiḥah it worships the Lord of all worlds, which includes the world of angels. A Muslim who mocks the unseen of 2:3 is nearer to 2:98 than he thinks.

Theologically the verse is the unity of *wilāyah* and *ʿadāwah*. “Allah is the ally of those who believe” (2:257). The ally of the believers is the enemy of those who make themselves enemies of His angels and messengers. There is no third camp that loves Allah (الله) and hates Jibrīl, or loves Mīkāʾīl and hates Muḥammad (ﷺ), or loves the Prophet (ﷺ) and hates a verse he brought. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that allegiance is indivisible in its origin: *al-walāʾ wa al-barāʾ* begins with Allah (الله) and then follows what is His. Al-Rāzī noted that naming Jibrīl and Mīkāʾīl after the messengers is a reply to a specific slogan, and that the *fāʾ* of *fa-inna Allāh* is the *fāʾ* of a legal consequence: enmity of these is *kufr*; Allah’s enmity is upon the *kāfirīn*. The name *kāfirīn* at the end identifies the angel-hater as a coverer, not as a critic of a temperament.

Al-Qurṭubī hears a rebuke of sectarian picking in this *ummah*: a group that loves ʿAlī and hates a companion, or loves a companion and hates another, in a way that becomes religion, is walking toward a split Allah (الله) did not authorize. The companions are not angels; the warning is about the habit of splitting a single *wilāyah*. 2:98’s habit is the mother.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “When Allah loves a servant, He calls Jibrīl: I love so-and-so, so love him; then Jibrīl loves him and calls in the heaven…” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The chain of love is Allah, then Jibrīl, then the people of heaven, then acceptance on the earth. 2:98 is the chain of enmity inverted: enmity of Jibrīl is a break of the chain of love, and Allah’s enmity is the consequence. You cannot have the love-hadith and the hate of Jibrīl. They are one theology.

Al-Ṭabarī notes that some reciters read *Mīkāl*, some *Mīkāʾīl*, some *Mīkāʾil*, all names of one servant. The Jews of the slogan did not dispute the pronunciation. They disputed the alliance. The verse gathers the names so that no ally remains to them in heaven if they persist. Mīkāʾīl will not rain a covenant upon a people who have declared war on his brother.

Ibn Kathīr cites that ʿUmar (may Allah be pleased with him) heard a Jew speak of Jibrīl as an enemy, and this verse was of the replies. Whether the *sabab* is ʿUmar’s hearing or Ibn Salām’s report or a deputation’s condition, the meaning is stable: split the angels and you have split with their Lord.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Angels do not enter a house in which there is a dog or an image” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). This *ummah* is taught to want the angels in a house. 2:98’s people wanted one angel as a mascot and one as a foe. You cannot host Mīkāʾīl on a condition that Jibrīl stay out. The house of *īmān* has one door.

Belief in the angels is a pillar; enmity of one angel is a demolition of the pillar. 2:3’s unseen begins to fall when a servant of the unseen is hated.

For the reader the verse is a census of your enmities. Do you hate a means of guidance because it came in a voice you did not hire? Allah (الله) is the enemy of the disbelievers, not of a tribe. Do not borrow His enmity for a feud. Love what He sent, including the angel you did not choose. The next verse will turn to the Prophet (ﷺ) with a consolation: We have sent down to you clear revelations, and none deny them except the defiantly disobedient. 2:97–98 answered the angel-excuse. 2:99 will name the remaining excuse as *fisq*.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:99

> Indeed, We have sent down to you ˹O Prophet˺ clear revelations. ˹But˺ none will deny them except the rebellious.

**Commentary:**

The Prophet (ﷺ) is addressed over their heads: *wa la-qad anzalnā ilayka āyātin bayyinātin wa mā yakfuru bihā illā al-fāsiqūn*, “And We have certainly sent down to you clear signs, and none cover them except the defiantly disobedient.” *Āyāt bayyināt* are signs that have been made clear: the Qur'an itself, its challenge of 2:23, its news of Israel that a people of a Book recognized, its confirmation of what was with them, the Prophet’s (ﷺ) character, the victories that 2:89’s *istiftāḥ* had asked for. Al-Ṭabarī said the clear signs are the proofs of his prophethood in this sūrah and in the Book. Ibn Kathīr: manifest verses that the honest of the People of the Book knew. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām’s recognition is the practical definition of *bayyinah*. The signs are not the problem. The chest is.

*Wa mā yakfuru bihā illā al-fāsiqūn*, and none cover them except the *fāsiqūn*. *Fisq* is to go out, a date leaving its skin, a servant leaving the obedience of a covenant. 2:26–27 named the *fāsiqūn* as those who break Allah’s covenant after its binding, cut what He commanded to be joined, and spoil the land. 2:99 returns the name after a stretch of Israel’s file: the split Book, the killed prophets, the calf drunk, the hated angel, the unused wish for death. Covering a clear sign is not a mistake of scholarship. It is *fisq*. The restriction *illā* is a consolation to the Prophet (ﷺ): do not read the covering as a fog in the message. Read it as an exit in the hearer.

The Prophet (ﷺ) lived this consolation. He grieved; 26:3 told him he would perhaps destroy himself that they did not believe. 2:99 tells him the deniers are *fāsiqūn*, not evidence that the *āyāt* failed. He said, “There is no prophet but was given signs the like of which people believed; and what I was given is revelation Allah sent to me, so I hope to be the most followed on the Day of Resurrection” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The Qur'an as *āyah bayyinah* is his hope of a wide following, and 2:99 is the explanation of the ones who will not follow. He recited, and the honest believed, and the *fāsiq* covered.

Theologically the verse is the clarity of *ḥujjah*. “We do not punish until We send a messenger” (17:15). The messenger has been sent; the signs are clear; remaining *kufr* is *fisq*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *bayān* removes excuse and that *fisq* after *bayān* is a different species from *fisq* before it. Al-Rāzī noted *ilayka* as an honor: the signs were sent down *to you*, O Muḥammad, so the covering is a covering of you, and the Lord has named the coverers. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of testimony, hears that a sign can be clear and still denied, so that courts of this world should not think a clear proof always yields a confession. The Day will yield it.

This *ummah* is given the same *āyāt* and the same split of hearers. A verse of *ribā* is *bayyinah*; the one who covers it with a trick of 2:65’s kind is *fāsiq* in that covering. A verse of parents in 2:83 is *bayyinah*; the one who covers it with a career is in the reach of 2:99’s *illā*. The name *fāsiq* in later *fiqh* has degrees; the Qur'an's name here is the covering of a clear sign, which is the mother of those degrees.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The example of what Allah has sent me with of guidance and knowledge is a rain that falls on a land…” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim): a plot that takes water and yields, a plot that holds water for others, a hard plot that does not take. 2:99’s *fāsiqūn* are the hard plot. The rain is not at fault. He also said, “I leave you two things; you will not go astray so long as you hold to them: the Book of Allah and my *sunnah*” in the well-known report. The *āyāt bayyināt* include both. Covering either is *fisq* of this verse’s kind.

Ibn ʿAbbās said some of the rabbis knew the signs as they knew their children (2:146) and still covered. 2:99 names that covering. Knowledge is not the opposite of *fisq*; *fisq* can be learned. 2:75 already said they distort after they understand. 2:99 says they cover after the signs are clear. The two verses are one chest at two hours.

Al-Rāzī noted *la-qad anzalnā* as an oath to soothe the Prophet (ﷺ): We did send; the sending is not in question; the *kufr* is not a verdict on you. 93:6–8 had already told him of a Lord who found him and guided. 2:99 tells him the guided Book will still be covered by a people of *fisq*. Carry. Do not thin.

The Prophet (ﷺ) recited, “Say, ‘The truth has come, and falsehood has vanished’” (17:81), and falsehood still had mouths. Clarity does not mean silence of *fisq*. It means *fisq* has no excuse. 2:99 is that meaning as a name. Carry the clear. Leave the covering on the coverer.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that *fāsiqūn* here are those who have exited the covenant of 2:27, so that 2:99 and 2:100 are one diagnosis in two verses: covering clear signs, throwing covenants. The names differ; the chest is one.

For the reader the verse is a consolation and a fear. Consolation if you call and are refused: the signs are clear; the refusal is *fisq*. Fear if you are the one covering: you have not found a hole in the Book; you have exited. The next verse will ask why, every time they make a covenant, a party of them throws it away, and will say most of them do not believe. 2:99 named the coverer. 2:100 will name the habit of throwing a *mīthāq*, so that 2:83–84’s covenants and 2:27’s rope are seen as a series of tosses, not as one accident.


## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:100

> Why is it that every time they make a covenant, a group of them casts it aside? In fact, most of them do not believe.

**Commentary:**

The file of twenty verses closes on a habit: *a-wa kullamā ʿāhadū ʿahdan nabadhahu farīqun minhum bal aktharuhum lā yuʾminūn*, “Is it not that whenever they made a covenant, a party of them threw it away? Rather, most of them do not believe.” *A-wa kullamā* is the hamzah of astonishment plus *kullamā*, every time — the same *kullamā* as 2:87’s every time a messenger came. There, every messenger; here, every covenant. The two habits are one: *hawā* does not keep a rope. *ʿĀhadū ʿahdan*, they covenanted a covenant — the *mīthāq* of 2:40, 2:63, 2:83, 2:84, the pledges with the Prophet (ﷺ) in Madinah, the Torah’s own yes. *Nabadhahu farīqun minhum*, a party of them threw it. *Nabadh* is to cast, to fling, as one flings a thing behind the back. 3:187 will say they flung the Book behind their backs and sold it for a small price. 2:101, in the next run, will say a party flung Allah’s Book behind their backs as if they did not know. 2:100 is the fling as a repeated gesture.

*Farīqun minhum* is again not all. The Qur'an's justice in a rebuke remains. A party throws. The few of 2:83 do not. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām does not. *Bal aktharuhum lā yuʾminūn*, rather, most of them do not believe. *Bal* turns from the party to a majority. The throwing is explained by a lack of *īmān*. A covenant kept is a fruit of faith; a covenant flung is a fruit of its absence. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī records, said they made pledges and broke them. Ibn Kathīr includes the breaking of the compact of Madinah by some of the Jewish tribes, and the older breakings this sūrah has listed. Al-Qurṭubī notes *aktharuhum* as a report, not a license to erase a remnant. Most is not all. 2:62’s door is still a door.

The Prophet (ﷺ) named breaking of covenants as a sign of *nifāq*: “when he pledges he betrays” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). He said, “There are four traits; whoever has them is a hypocrite…” and among them the betrayal of a ʿahd. He kept his own covenants until those who made them broke, as the story of Ḥudaybiyah and of the tribes of Madinah shows. He said, “Whoever has killed a muʿāhad will not smell the scent of Paradise” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī), a law of a covenant with a non-Muslim. 2:100 is the other side: a people of a Book who throw. This *ummah* was warned it would follow them span by span. A Muslim who signs and flings — a marriage, a debt, a treaty, a *bayʿah*, a promise to a child — is in the gesture of *nabadhahu*, even if his tongue still recites al-Baqarah.

Theologically the verse is the link between *ʿahd* and *īmān*. 2:27 made the breakers of covenant the *fāsiqūn* who lose. 2:100 says the throwers are, in the majority, not believers. Faith is not a feeling that can live with a flung rope. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the first covenant is the covenant of *alastu bi-rabbikum* (7:172), and that every later *ʿahd* is a remembering or a throwing of that first. Al-Rāzī noted *kullamā* as a despair of reform by mere new contracts: a people who throw will throw the next. What they need is *īmān*, not a better clause. *Bal aktharuhum lā yuʾminūn* is that diagnosis.

Al-Ṭabarī treats the hamzah as a rebuke upon the believers’ remaining naivety, cousin of 2:75’s *a-fa-taṭmaʿūn*: you still hope they will keep a new compact, when every compact has a party that flings? Hope in a person is not forbidden; hope that ignores a habit is. The Prophet (ﷺ) hoped, called, made compacts, and was not naive. 2:100 is part of that education of Madinah.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The signs of the hypocrite are three: when he speaks he lies, when he promises he breaks, and when he is entrusted he betrays” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A covenant is a promise and a trust. *Nabadhahu* is the third sign as a national habit. He said on the day of the Farewell, “Do not return after me to being disbelievers, striking the necks of one another” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). That striking would be a throwing of the *ʿahd* of 2:84. 2:100 is Israel’s throwing as a warning of ours.

Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *īmān* is *qawl* and *ʿamal*, and that a flung covenant is an *ʿamal* that belies a *qawl* of faith. *Bal aktharuhum lā yuʾminūn* is that belying named. The majority is not a despair of *daʿwah*; it is a realism of history. The few still bind. Bind with them.

Al-Rāzī noted that *kullamā* makes the throwing a custom, and that customs of breaking are harder to repent from than a single break, because the soul names them politics. 2:100 unnames them. They are unbelief of a majority, not politics of a state.

For the reader this batch of twenty began by tearing a calendar of Fire, named two remaining houses, listed a covenant of *tawḥīd* and *iḥsān*, forbade blood and exile, exposed a piety of ransom upon a crime of expulsion, priced the split as a purchase of the world, told a line of messengers met with arrogance, unwrapped a heart, recalled a prayer for a prophet who was then covered, named envy of *faḍl*, broke the slogan of “our revelation only” with a calf and a mountain and a drink in the heart, challenged a private Garden with a wish for death they would not make, showed a greed of a thousand years, defended Jibrīl, unified the angels, consoled the Prophet (ﷺ) that only the *fāsiq* covers clear signs, and ends with a party that throws a covenant, most of them not believing. The next run will begin with 2:101’s fling of the Book behind the back when a messenger from Allah came confirming what was with them. Keep your own ʿahd. Do not be the *farīq* that throws. Believe, so that a covenant is a rope in the hand, not a thing behind the back.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:101

> Now, when a messenger from Allah has come to them—confirming their own Scriptures—some of the People of the Book cast the Book of Allah behind their backs as if they did not know.

**Commentary:**

The throwing of 2:100 is now given its object and its hour: *wa lammā jāʾahum rasūlun min ʿindi Allāhi muṣaddiqun limā maʿahum nabadha farīqun min alladhīna ūtū al-kitāba kitāba Allāhi warāʾa ẓuhūrihim ka-annahum lā yaʿlamūn*, “And when a messenger from Allah came to them, confirming what was with them, a party of those who were given the Book flung the Book of Allah behind their backs as if they did not know.” *Lammā* is the when of a test that has arrived, not of a rumor. The messenger is Muḥammad (ﷺ). *Min ʿindi Allāh* is from the presence of Allah, the same *ʿinda* that 2:80 asked them to produce as a covenant and could not. *Muṣaddiqun limā maʿahum* is the confirmation of 2:41, 2:89, 2:91, 2:97: he does not arrive as a foreigner to their Torah; he arrives as its yes. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, said the party flung the Torah by refusing the prophet the Torah had described. Others said they flung the Qur'an. Both readings live, and they are one crime: a Book behind a back. The Torah, if kept, would have named him; the Qur'an, if taken, would have confirmed it. They put both where a man puts a thing he does not intend to use.

*Nabadha* is the verb of 2:100. The habit of throwing covenants has found its largest rope. *Farīqun min alladhīna ūtū al-kitāb* is again a party, not the whole of those given the Book. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām (may Allah be pleased with him) did not throw. The few of 2:83 did not throw. The Qur'an's justice inside a rebuke is itself a *furqān*. *Kitāba Allāh*, the Book of Allah — definite, ascribed. What is flung is not a commentary, not a rabbi’s opinion, not a custom of a town. It is the speech ascribed to the Lord. *Warāʾa ẓuhūrihim*, behind their backs. The image is physical. A back is the side that does not see. 3:187 will say they took a covenant to make the Book clear to people and not to hide it, then they flung it behind their backs and sold it for a small price. 2:101 is that fling at the hour of a messenger. *Ka-annahum lā yaʿlamūn*, as if they did not know. *Ka-anna* is the as-if of a performance. They know. 2:75 said a party heard the speech of Allah then distorted after they understood, while they know. 2:89 said when what they recognized came they covered it. 2:101 says they throw as if ignorant. Feigned illiteracy upon a literacy is a worse *ummīyyah* than 2:78’s.

The Prophet (ﷺ) came to Madinah as the confirmation they had advertised to Aws and Khazraj. The Anṣār believed the advertisement. A party of the advertisers put the Book where a traveler puts a worn cloak. He recited upon them what was with them and what was new, and some of them said, in the grammar of 2:91, we believe in what was sent down to us. 2:101 names that sentence a fling. He said, “You will follow the ways of those before you, span by span and cubit by cubit” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A later *ummah* that recites a *muṣḥaf* and then lives as if the *muṣḥaf* were behind a back is in the gesture. He said, “The Qur'an is a proof for you or against you” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A Book behind a back is a proof against.

Theologically the verse is the ethics of confirmation. A messenger who confirms what you have is the easiest messenger to receive, if what you have is beloved as speech of Allah (الله) and not as property of a tribe. Confirmation threatens a property. It does not threaten a faith. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the most painful test of a people of a Book is a Book that agrees with theirs and then demands a following, because agreement removes the excuse of foreignness and leaves only *hawā*. Al-Rāzī noted *ka-annahum lā yaʿlamūn* as a satire of 2:78: the unlettered who do not know except wishes, and the lettered who know and then act unlettered. Two diseases, one hour.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, hears in *nabadh* every judge who has a verse and rules by a custom, every teacher who has a ḥadīth and teaches a mood, every state that has a *sharīʿah* in a constitution and a *siḥr* of policy behind it — for 2:102 will say that what they followed, having flung the Book, was the magic of the satans. The sequence is the pedagogy: first the back, then the substitute. A chest that has emptied a Book will not remain empty. It will take a teaching of *shayāṭīn*.

Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *īmān* in a Book is not a heritage-name; it is a taking with the *quwwah* of 2:63. A people given a Book who fling it at the coming of its confirmation have not believed in the Giver. They have believed in a library. The Prophet (ﷺ) was *ummī* in the praise of 7:157, and he brought a Book he did not write; they were lettered, and they threw a Book they had. The contrast is 2:78 inverted and 2:101 completed.

Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *muṣaddiqun limā maʿahum* as a mercy that became a ḥujjah. Confirmation is a kindness to a people of a Book: you are not asked to orphan Mūsā (peace be upon him). You are asked to greet the one Mūsā’s Lord sent. A party made the kindness a reason to throw. 46:10 will ask a witness from Banū Isrāʾīl who believed; 2:101 is the other witness, the back. This *ummah* is spoken to with the same *nabdh* whenever a *sunnah* confirms a *āyah* and a custom throws both. The messenger still confirms. The back is still a crime.

For the reader the verse is a hand-check. Where is the Book in your house — in front of the face, or behind the back? A messenger has come, confirming. Do not perform ignorance. The next verse will name what a party followed instead: the recitation of the satans over the kingdom of Sulaymān (peace be upon him), a slander upon a prophet, a school of magic in Babylon, a rift between spouses, a purchase with no share in the Hereafter. 2:101 is the empty back. 2:102 is what crawled into the emptiness.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:102

> They ˹instead˺ followed the magic promoted by the devils during the reign of Solomon. Never did Solomon disbelieve, rather the devils disbelieved. They taught magic to the people, along with what had been revealed to the two angels, Hârût and Mârût, in Babylon. The two angels never taught anyone without saying, “We are only a test ˹for you˺, so do not abandon ˹your˺ faith.” Yet people learned ˹magic˺ that caused a rift ˹even˺ between husband and wife; although their magic could not harm anyone except by Allah’s Will. They learned what harmed them and did not benefit them—although they already knew that whoever buys into magic would have no share in the Hereafter. Miserable indeed was the price for which they sold their souls, if only they knew!

**Commentary:**

The emptiness of a flung Book is filled at once: *wa ittabaʿū mā tatlū al-shayāṭīnu ʿalā mulki Sulaymāna wa mā kafara Sulaymānu wa lākinna al-shayāṭīna kafarū yuʿallimūna al-nāsa al-siḥra wa mā unzila ʿalā al-malakayni bi-Bātila Hārūta wa Mārūta*, “And they followed what the satans recited over the kingdom of Sulaymān. Sulaymān did not disbelieve, but the satans disbelieved, teaching the people magic, and what was sent down upon the two angels at Babylon, Hārūt and Mārūt.” *Ittabaʿū* is they followed — the verb of a *dīn*. Having thrown the Book of Allah (الله) behind their backs, a party took as imam the recitation of *shayāṭīn*. *Tatlū* is to recite, to follow, to read upon. The satans recited over *mulk Sulaymān*, the kingdom of Solomon (peace be upon him): they wrote books of magic, the *salaf* said, and buried them under his throne, or they whispered that his rule of jinn and wind was sorcery, so that when he died the books were brought out and a prophet was slandered. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī gathers with differing chains, is associated with more than one telling. The Qur'an does not need the archaeology of the chests. It needs the clearing of a prophet’s name: *wa mā kafara Sulaymānu*. Sulaymān did not cover. He did not disbelieve. The kingdom of a prophet is not *siḥr*. The satans disbelieved. *Kafarū* is named of them, and their *kufr* was a teaching: *yuʿallimūna al-nāsa al-siḥr*.

*Al-siḥr* is magic, from a root that includes the turning of a thing from its face, the tying of a knot, the deception of the eye. Ahl al-Sunnah hold that *siḥr* is real as a means Allah (الله) has permitted to exist, as He has permitted poison, and that some of it is *kufr* — that which seeks the satans, writes what is not allowed, claims a lordship of harm — and some of it is a crime below *kufr* if it is trickery and harm without that worship. The Prophet (ﷺ) counted *siḥr* among the seven destroyers: “Associate nothing with Allah, do not kill the soul Allah has forbidden except in right, do not consume *ribā*, do not consume the orphan’s wealth, do not flee the battlefield, do not slander chaste believing women, and avoid *siḥr*” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the meanings of the seven). He said, “Whoever goes to a soothsayer and believes him has disbelieved in what was sent down upon Muḥammad” (Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī, in a well-known report). 2:102 is that disbelief as a school in Babylon.

*Wa mā unzila ʿalā al-malakayni bi-Bātila Hārūta wa Mārūt*, and what was sent down upon the two angels in Babylon, Hārūt and Mārūt. The *salaf* differed: were they two angels sent as a *fitnah*, teaching magic as a test with a warning; or two kings (*malikayn* has been read) among men; or a report of what the satans claimed was sent down? Al-Ṭabarī prefers they were two angels, and that they did not teach anyone until they said *innamā naḥnu fitnatun fa-lā takfur*, “We are only a trial, so do not disbelieve.” The Qur'an records the warning in full, so that no one will say the teachers hid the cost. A test that names itself a test is still a test. Babylon is the city of exile in their memory, a low land of a later kingdom. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as a rebuke of those who left the Torah for knots.

They learned what *yufarriqūna bihi bayna al-marʾi wa zawjih*, what they cause separation with between a man and his spouse. The first target of this *siḥr* is the house. 30:21 made the spouse a sign of *mawaddah* and *raḥmah*. Magic that splits a bed is a war on a sign. The Prophet (ﷺ) sought refuge from the evil of those who blow on knots (113:4), and ʿĀʾishah (may Allah be pleased with her) narrated that a spell was worked upon him until he imagined he had done a thing he had not, and Allah (الله) healed him, and the knot was found in a well (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The story is a proof that *siḥr* can touch a body and that it cannot, except by His leave, hold. *Wa mā hum bi-ḍārrīna bihi min aḥadin illā bi-idhni Allāh*, and they are not harmers with it of anyone except by Allah’s permission. The clause is *tawḥīd* in a verse of magic. No knot is a lord. No satan is a lord. The harm, when it happens, is inside a Decree. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that this clause saves the servant from two ditches: the ditch of denying *siḥr*, and the ditch of fearing a magician as one fears the Lord.

*Wa yataʿallamūna mā yaḍurruhum wa lā yanfaʿuhum*, they learn what harms them and does not benefit them. Knowledge is not always a mercy. 2:102 is a knowledge that is a wound. *Wa la-qad ʿalimū la-mani ishtarāhu mā lahu fī al-ākhirati min khalāq*, they had already known that whoever buys it has no share in the Hereafter. *Khalāq* is a portion, a lot. The purchase is 2:16, 2:86, 2:90 again: a soul on a counter. They knew. 2:75’s knowledge, 2:89’s recognition, 2:101’s as-if-they-did-not-know, now a known purchase of no share. *Wa la-biʾsa mā sharaw bihi anfusahum law kānū yaʿlamūn*, miserable is that for which they sold themselves, if they had been knowing. Two knowledges in one verse: they knew there was no share; they did not know as those who know. *ʿIlm* that does not restrain is 2:44’s recitation that does not do.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a judge, drew the legal fruit: *siḥr* that is *kufr* is a *riddah* in many of the *fuqahāʾ*; harm by magic is a crime of blood if it kills; going to a magician is a door the hadith shut. This *ummah*’s *ruqyah* is the Fātiḥah, the *muʿawwidhatān*, the Names, a permitted asking of the Lord, not a knot bought in a market. Ibn al-Qayyim distinguished the medicine of *tawḥīd* from the medicine of *shirk*: both may be sought for a pain; only one leaves a *khalāq*.

For the reader the verse is a warning about every substitute you learn after you have put a Book behind you. A flung Qur'an is often followed by a feed of charms, a “healer” who does not pray, a spouse-split dressed as empowerment, a fear of a person that should be a fear of Allah (الله). Harm is only by His leave. Buy nothing that costs a share. The next verse will say the lost bargain in the other direction: if they had believed and been mindful, a reward from Allah would have been better, if they had known.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:103

> If only they were faithful and mindful ˹of Allah˺, there would have been a better reward from Allah, if only they knew!

**Commentary:**

The miserable sale is answered with a *law*, a wish of the Lord that is also a verdict: *wa law annahum āmanū wa ittaqaw la-mathūbatun min ʿindi Allāhi khayrun law kānū yaʿlamūn*, “And if they had believed and been mindful, a reward from Allah would have been better, if they had known.” 2:102 ended *law kānū yaʿlamūn*; 2:103 ends with the same clause. Two “if they had known,” two objects. There, if they had known the misery of the price. Here, if they had known the better of the alternative. The grammar is almost a kindness. Allah (الله) names what would have been better after He has named what they bought. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, read *āmanū* as faith in Allah and His messengers, including Muḥammad (ﷺ), and *ittaqaw* as *taqwā* that leaves *siḥr* and the flung Book. The pair is 2:2–4 in a verse of regret: believe, be mindful — the Book would have been a guide, not a cloak behind a back.

*Mathūbah* is a reward, a return, from *th-w-b*, to come back. A deed comes back as a wage. *Min ʿindi Allāh*, from the presence of Allah — the *ʿinda* they could not produce as a numbered-days covenant in 2:80 is here a treasury of wage. *Khayrun*, better: better than the magic, better than the split of a marriage, better than a kingdom’s slander, better than the small price of 2:79, better than a thousand years of 2:96. The comparative does not need to name the worse. 2:102 has named it. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *khayr* here is *nakirah* for greatness: a good from Allah not specified because it cannot be exhausted. “If you should count the favors of Allah, you could not enumerate them” (14:34).

The double *law* is the verse’s sting and its mercy. *Law annahum āmanū wa ittaqaw* is a condition they did not fulfill. *Law kānū yaʿlamūn* is a knowledge they did not use. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as a *taḥrīḍ*, an urging: the door named in the past tense of a missed condition is not closed upon a living reader. 39:53 remains. The *salaf* loved to pause on *law* when it is spoken by the Lord, because it teaches the value of the missed without yet naming a despair. A Lord who says “if only” has not yet said “too late” to every chest.

The Prophet (ﷺ) made *īmān* and *taqwā* the two wings of a wage. He said, “Be mindful of Allah wherever you are, and follow a bad deed with a good deed and it will wipe it, and deal with people with good character” (al-Tirmidhī). He said, “The most beloved of deeds to Allah are the most constant, even if small” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “Allah has written *iḥsān* upon every thing” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:103’s better reward is not a rumor of a private Garden of 2:94. It is *min ʿindi Allāh*, stored, seen, given to whoever believes and is mindful — the filter of 2:62, the house of 2:82. He also said, “This world is cursed, and what is in it is cursed, except the remembrance of Allah and what attends it, a teacher and a student” (al-Tirmidhī). Magic is of the cursed what; *taqwā* is of the excepted.

Theologically the verse is the economics of 2:16 reversed. They purchased misguidance with guidance; they would have been given a *mathūbah* better than the merchandise. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that every sin is a bad purchase and that *taqwā* is the only trade that cannot lose, because its capital is from Him and its profit is with Him. Al-Rāzī noted the order *āmanū wa ittaqaw*: faith first, then the shield, because a mindfulness without faith is a custom, and a faith without mindfulness is 2:8’s sentence. Both are required for the *mathūbah*.

Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *law kānū yaʿlamūn* as a knowledge of the Hereafter’s measure. They measured by a split house, a feared enemy, a rumor of Sulaymān. The better cannot be seen by that measure. “You prefer the worldly life, while the Hereafter is better and more lasting” (87:16–17). 2:103 is 87:17 as a *law* over a school of knots.

This *ummah* is spoken to with the same *law* whenever it leaves a Book for a charm, a prayer for a shortcut, a *tawakkul* for a soothsayer. The better is still *min ʿindi Allāh*. The knowing is still the missing piece. The Prophet (ﷺ) taught *istighfār* as a knowledge that returns: he sought forgiveness seventy times in a day (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:103’s *law* becomes, in a mouth of *tawbah*, a *lawlā* of 2:64: were it not for *faḍl* and *raḥmah*, the missed condition would have been the last word. It is not, yet.

Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī both linger on the pairing of *īmān* and *taqwā* as the lost bargain’s opposite: not a new knot, not a new teacher of *shayāṭīn*, a chest that believes and a limb that stops. 65:2–3 promised whoever has *taqwā* a way out and a provision from where he does not reckon. 2:103 says the same provision in the language of a *mathūbah*. The magician promised a split of a house and a harm by a will he does not own. The Lord promised a better from His presence. The knowing that would have seen the better is 2:4’s *yaqīn* of a Hereafter, not 2:102’s knowing of a market. This *ummah* is still offered the same *law* as a door: believe, be mindful, and the wage is *min ʿindi Allāh*, not min ʿindi a soothsayer. 8:29: if you have *taqwā*, He will make for you a *furqān*. 2:103 is that *furqān* named as a better the sellers did not buy.

For the reader the verse is a price-tag turned around. You have seen the miserable sale. Here is the better. Believe. Be mindful. Know. The next verses will turn from that party’s magic to this *ummah*’s tongue: do not say *rāʿinā* as they twisted it; say *unẓurnā*, and listen. A flung Book began in a flung word. 2:104 will guard a word in a sitting with the Prophet (ﷺ).

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:104

> O believers! Do not say, “Râ’ina.” But say, “Unẓurna,” and listen ˹attentively˺. And the disbelievers will suffer a painful punishment.

**Commentary:**

The sūrah turns its face to the believers with a command about a single word: *yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū lā taqūlū rāʿinā wa qūlū unẓurnā wa ismaʿū wa lil-kāfirīna ʿadhābun alīm*, “O you who believe, do not say *rāʿinā*, but say *unẓurnā*, and listen; and for the disbelievers is a painful punishment.” *Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū* is the address that will become this sūrah’s habit of law. Faith is named first so that the tongue is asked as a believer’s tongue. *Rāʿinā* in Arabic is from *r-ʿ-y*, to tend, to watch: look after us, attend to us, give us a moment. The Companions used it to ask the Prophet (ﷺ) to slow, to wait, to let the back rows hear. Some of the Jews of Madinah, Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, Qatādah, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, twisted the sound toward a Hebrew or a dialect of insult — *raʿīna*, our evil one, a fool, a shepherd of shame — or they stressed it as a *fāʿilnā* of mutual insolence: tend us as we tend you, a bargain of listening. 4:46 names the crime in full: “Among those who are Jews are those who distort words from their places and say, ‘We hear and we disobey,’ and ‘Hear, may you not hear,’ and *rāʿinā*, twisting their tongues and slandering the religion.” 2:104 shuts the word upon the believers so that a sitting with the Messenger (ﷺ) will not be a stage for a pun.

*Wa qūlū unẓurnā*, but say *unẓurnā*: look at us, grant us a look, wait for us. The root *n-ẓ-r* is a looking that does not carry the twist. Al-Qurṭubī, as a linguist of law, hears the verse as a principle: when a lawful word becomes a vehicle of harm, the Lawgiver may retire the word and leave the need. The need was attention. The retired word was *rāʿinā*. The replacement was *unẓurnā*. *Adab* with the Prophet (ﷺ) is not a decoration. It is a *ḥukm*. “Do not raise your voices above the voice of the Prophet” (49:2). “Do not make the calling of the Messenger among you as your calling of one another” (24:63). 2:104 is that *adab* in a vocabulary of two verbs.

*Wa ismaʿū*, and listen. Hearing is the gate of 2:93’s *ismaʿū* that Israel answered with *samiʿnā wa ʿaṣaynā*. Here the believers are told to hear as those who intend to obey. The command is not “debate,” not “pun,” not “catch a sound for a joke.” It is listen. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever believes in Allah and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “A servant may say a word that wins Allah’s pleasure, not giving it much weight, and by it Allah raises him degrees; and a servant may say a word that wins Allah’s wrath, not giving it much weight, and by it he falls into the Fire” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). *Rāʿinā* in a twisted mouth is a word not given much weight. The painful punishment named at the end is the weight.

*Wa lil-kāfirīna ʿadhābun alīm*, and for the disbelievers a painful punishment. The clause lifts from a vocabulary-lesson to a threat upon those whose twist was *kufr* of a tongue — mockery of the Messenger (ﷺ), slander of the religion, 9:65’s playing that was named disbelief. Ibn Kathīr treats the ending as a warning that word-games with revelation are not cleverness. They are a path to *ʿadhāb alīm*, the painful punishment of 2:10’s hypocrites. The believers are pulled off the path by a synonym.

Theologically the verse is the sanctity of speech around the Prophet (ﷺ) and, by extension, around the speech he brought. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the tongue is the interpreter of the heart and that a heart of *īmān* will not enjoy a sound that can be heard as an insult of its Prophet. Al-Rāzī noted that Allah (الله) did not only forbid the intention of insult; He forbade a word that could carry it, so that the sitting would be clean even of a doubt. This is *sadd al-dharīʿah*, blocking the means, in a single vocabulary.

The Prophet (ﷺ) loved good names and hated ugly ones; he changed names that meant war or bitterness (the reports in Abū Dāwūd and elsewhere). 2:104 is that love as a command to a community. This *ummah* is still in sittings — a lesson, a *ḥalaqah*, a comment under a recitation — where a joke at a verse, a nickname of a scholar, a pun on a Name, is *rāʿinā* in a new dialect. Say *unẓurnā*. Ask for a look without a twist. Listen.

Al-Ṭabarī records that some of the *salaf* said the believers were not themselves insulters; they were imitators of a word they heard. Imitation of a hostile vocabulary is still a danger. “Do not imitate a people” is the spirit of the hadith, “Whoever imitates a people is one of them” (Abū Dāwūd). 2:104 is imitation shut at a syllable.

Ibn Kathīr notes that 4:46 makes the twist a *ṭaʿn* in the religion, not a joke in a market. A sitting with the Messenger (ﷺ) is a sitting with a *waḥy*. 2:104 trains the *ummah* for every later sitting with that *waḥy* in a *muṣḥaf*. Al-Ṭabarī records that some of the believers were pained to leave a word they had meant well. The Lawgiver still retired it. Meaning well does not keep a vehicle that enemies have stolen. This is a mercy to a community that will live among tongues that hunt sounds.

For the reader the verse is a mouth-check in every religious room. Which words do you borrow? Which sounds do you leave in a joke? The disbelievers have a painful punishment; you have been given a replacement word and a command to hear. Use both. The next verse will expose a wish behind some of those twisted sittings: the disbelievers of the Book and the polytheists do not want any *khayr* sent down to you from your Lord. Envy of 2:90 now looks at the believers’ gift. Allah (الله) chooses for His mercy whom He wills, and He is the Lord of great bounty.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:105

> The disbelievers from the People of the Book and the polytheists would not want you to receive any blessing from your Lord, but Allah selects whoever He wills for His mercy. And Allah is the Lord of infinite bounty.

**Commentary:**

The twisted word is given its motive in a wider company: *mā yawaddu alladhīna kafarū min ahli al-kitābi wa lā al-mushrikīna an yunazzala ʿalaykum min khayrin min rabbikum wa Allāhu yakhtaṣṣu bi-raḥmatihi man yashāʾu wa Allāhu dhū al-faḍli al-ʿaẓīm*, “Neither those who disbelieve from the People of the Book, nor the polytheists, would love that any good be sent down to you from your Lord. But Allah selects for His mercy whom He wills, and Allah is the Possessor of great bounty.” *Mā yawaddu*, they would not love — the verb of 2:96’s wishing a thousand years, now a wishing against. The subject is two: *alladhīna kafarū min ahli al-kitāb*, those who have covered from the People of the Book, and *al-mushrikūn*, the associators. A shared envy joins a lettered *kufr* and an unlettered *shirk*. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, said they hated that the Qur'an, the prophecy, the *nubuwwah* should come down upon an Arab, upon this *ummah*. 2:90 named the resentment *baghyan an yunazzila Allāh*. 2:105 names the wish as a not-loving of any *khayr*.

*An yunazzala ʿalaykum min khayrin min rabbikum*, that there be sent down upon you of good from your Lord. *Min khayr* is partitive and diminutive of quantity: any good, a bit of good. They do not hate only a throne. They hate a verse, a ruling, a consolation, a rain of 2:22. Envy that has matured hates the small gift because the small gift proves the Giver is still giving next door. Al-Qurṭubī notes the pairing of People of the Book and polytheists as a warning to the believers not to seek the pleasure of either camp as a condition of receiving. 2:120 will say it plainly.

*Wa Allāhu yakhtaṣṣu bi-raḥmatihi man yashāʾu*, and Allah singles out for His mercy whom He wills. *Ikhtiṣāṣ* is a specifying, a selecting. *Raḥmah* here, Ibn al-Jawzī gathered, has been read as prophecy, as Islam, as every gift of *tawfīq*. ʿAlī (may Allah be pleased with him) and Mujāhid are associated with *nubuwwah*; Ibn ʿAbbās with Islam. The readings are concentric. Mercy in the Fātiḥah is general and particular; here it is the particular of a choosing. *Man yashāʾu* is the *mashīʾah* of 2:90, the Lord’s freedom to place His message where He knows (6:124). Envy is a vote against that freedom. The verse does not argue with the vote. It states the choosing.

*Wa Allāhu dhū al-faḍli al-ʿaẓīm*, and Allah is the Possessor of the great bounty. *Faḍl* is the surplus of 2:47, 2:64, 2:90. *ʿAẓīm* is great as 2:7’s punishment was great — a matching adjective for a matching scale. Bounty that is great cannot be rationed by a tribe’s wish. Ibn Kathīr treats the close as a consolation to the Prophet (ﷺ) and the believers: their gift is not stolen from someone else’s pocket. It is from a *faḍl* that does not diminish. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Allah’s Hand is full, and it is not diminished by the night-and-day spending” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “Look at those below you” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), the medicine of envy. He said, “None of you believes until he loves for his brother what he loves for himself” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), the opposite of *mā yawaddu*.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd al-faḍl*. Gifts are His to place. A religion that treats prophecy as an inheritance office will always say *mā yawaddu* when the office moves. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that envy is objecting to the Decree while naming a person as the problem. The person here is an *ummah* receiving *khayr*; the objection is to the Lord of *faḍl*. Al-Rāzī noted that polytheists and a party of the Book meet in this not-loving, so that the believers will not imagine that a shared Scripture is a shared love. 2:105 is realism of the chest, not a license of hate. 2:109 will command pardon until Allah’s command.

This *ummah* is tried by a smaller *mā yawaddu*: a student who hates another’s *qirāʾah*, a masjid that hates another’s crowd, a people who hate that guidance be given to a convert of the “wrong” ethnicity. *Yakhtaṣṣu bi-raḥmatihi man yashāʾu* still stands. The Prophet (ﷺ) said Salmān is of us, the people of the House, in the honor of a Persian who believed; he said Bilāl’s footsteps were heard in the Garden. *Faḍl ʿaẓīm* is not an Arab ration.

Al-Ṭabarī reads *min khayr* as including every descent of a verse, a ruling, a consolation, even a rain. Envy that hates “any” has left the scale of a kingdom and entered the scale of a neighbor’s crumb. 4:54 asked if they envy the people for what Allah (الله) gave them of His bounty; He gave the family of Ibrāhīm the Book and the *ḥikmah* and a great kingdom. 2:105 is 4:54 aimed at this *ummah*’s crumb and this *ummah*’s Book together. Ibn Kathīr treats *dhū al-faḍl al-ʿaẓīm* as a Name to sit with when a door is shut in a human face: the bounty is not in that face. It is in a Lord who selects.

For the reader the verse is a census of what you cannot bear to see given. If a *khayr* upon another chest pains you, you have sat for a moment with the subject of *mā yawaddu*. Ask for a love of the Giver’s freedom. The next verse will answer another of their attacks upon the gift: they mocked *naskh*, the replacing of a verse, as if a Lord who chooses mercy could not choose a ruling’s time. 2:106 will say: if We abrogate a verse or cause it to be forgotten, We bring better or like it. Do you not know that Allah is over all things capable?

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:106

> If We ever abrogate a verse or cause it to be forgotten, We replace it with a better or similar one. Do you not know that Allah is Most Capable of everything?

**Commentary:**

The gift they did not love is now defended in its inner life as speech that the Speaker may replace: *mā nansakh min āyatin aw nunsihā naʾti bi-khayrin minhā aw mithlihā a-lam taʿlam anna Allāha ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr*, “Whatever verse We abrogate or cause to be forgotten, We bring one better than it or like it. Do you not know that Allah is over all things capable?” *Naskh* in the language is removal, copying, transferring; in the *uṣūl* of this *ummah* it is the later address lifting a earlier *ḥukm*, wholly or in part. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, and the mass of the *salaf*, took the verse as a statement of that lifting. Some of the People of the Book, and some of the pagans, said: a Lord does not change His mind; if Muḥammad (ﷺ) brings a ruling and then another, he is inventing. 16:101 records the taunt: “When We replace a verse in place of a verse — and Allah knows best what He sends down — they say, ‘You are only a forger.’” 2:106 answers with capability, not with apology.

The *fuqahāʾ* of the *salaf* named kinds. A verse whose wording and ruling both lift, remembered only as a report — ʿUmar (may Allah be pleased with him) spoke of a verse of stoning whose recitation was lifted and whose *ḥukm* remained in the practice of the Prophet (ﷺ). A verse whose wording remains and whose ruling lifts — 2:240’s waiting of a widow beside 2:234, in the famous discussion; the change of qiblah from Bayt al-Maqdis to the Kaʿbah, 2:142–150, which this passage is already preparing; the stages of wine until 5:90. A verse replaced by a like. *Aw nunsihā*, or We cause it to be forgotten — a reading, and *nansaʾhā*, We postpone it, another. Forgetting as a divine act is not a defect of the Messenger (ﷺ). 87:6–7: “We shall make you recite, so you will not forget, except what Allah wills.” He (ﷺ) was human under a will. The *ummah* was not left without a replacement. *Naʾti bi-khayrin minhā aw mithlihā*, We bring better than it or like it. Better in ease, or in reward, or in fit to a later hour. Like it in wisdom. The Speaker is not poorer after a lifting. He is *qadīr*.

*A-lam taʿlam anna Allāha ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr*, do you not know that Allah is over every thing capable? The question is 2:80’s *qul ātū* turned inward: you already know capability. A Lord who created the heavens and the earth, 2:107 will say, is not trapped in a first drafting. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *naskh* is not *badāʾ*, the appearance of a new knowledge to the Lord. *Badāʾ* is a slander the Rāfiḍah and some of the Jews of the taunt used. *Naskh* is a wisdom timed: a medicine for a stage, then another medicine, while the Physician knew both hours from the start. Al-Rāzī made the same distinction with the tools of *kalām*. Ahl al-Sunnah hold that Allah’s knowledge is eternal, His speech is uncreated in its being His, and the *naskh* is in the *ḥukm* attached to a time, not in a Lord who learns.

The Prophet (ﷺ) lived *naskh* as a mercy. He said, “I had forbidden you from visiting graves; now visit them” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “I had forbidden you from storing the meat of sacrifice; now store” (the reports of the *aḥādīth* of concession). The Companions did not hear a forger. They heard a teacher whose Lord timed a tightness and a looseness. ʿĀʾishah (may Allah be pleased with her) said the Fātiḥah of the Book and the *mufaṣṣal* were not among what was forgotten in the well-known discussions of recitation; the point for 2:106 is that forgetting, when it happened as He willed, did not leave a hole. The *mutawātir* recitation we have is what He taught us to keep.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, warns the amateur: *naskh* is not a key for every reader to retire a verse he dislikes. It is established by knowledge — a later verse, a known practice of the Prophet (ﷺ), a consensus of those who saw the two hours — not by a mood of a century. This *ummah* is tried by two excesses: a denial of *naskh* that makes the Law a frozen first sitting, and a wild *naskh* that makes the Law a newspaper. 2:106 is neither. It is the capability of a wise Speaker.

Ibn Kathīr gathers that some of the *salaf* said *āyah* here can also be a miracle, a sign, replaced by another. The two meanings kiss. A sign of a qiblah is replaced by a sign of a qiblah. A ruling is a sign of a stage. The better or the like is always from Him.

Al-Ṭabarī insists that *naʾti bi-khayrin minhā* is not a belittling of a lifted verse. Both are His speech. The better is a better for a later servant, a lighter load, a clearer *ḥukm*, a qiblah of a House Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him) raised. 2:143 will call the middle *ummah* a witness. A witness needs a timed law, not a frozen first week of Madinah as a prison. This *ummah* is also warned by the verse’s abuse: some modern tongues say “naskh” upon ḥudūd they dislike, upon 4:11 they dislike, without a later text. That is not 2:106. That is 2:79’s writing with their hands. Capability belongs to the Speaker, not to the restless.

For the reader the verse is a trust in the Physician’s timing. Do not let a mocker of “change” make you ashamed of a Lord who eases after a hardness, or who hardens after an ease, as 2:187 eased the nights of fasting. He is *qadīr* over every thing. The next verse will ground that capability in a kingdom: do you not know that to Allah belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth, and you have besides Him no guardian and no helper?

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:107

> Do you not know that the kingdom of the heavens and the earth belongs ˹only˺ to Allah, and you have no guardian or helper besides Allah?

**Commentary:**

The capability of 2:106 is given its throne: *a-lam taʿlam anna Allāha lahu mulku al-samāwāti wa al-arḍi wa mā lakum min dūni Allāhi min waliyyin wa lā naṣīr*, “Do you not know that to Allah belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth, and you have besides Allah no guardian and no helper?” The question is the same *a-lam taʿlam* of the verse before, a knowing they already have as a creedal fact and have not yet spent as a patience with *naskh* and with envy. *Lahu mulk*, His is the kingdom — *mulk* of 2:102’s slandered *mulk Sulaymān*, now the unslanderable *mulk* of the two worlds. A prophet’s kingdom was a loan. This kingdom is not a loan. 3:26 will teach the prayer: “Say: O Allah, Owner of the kingdom, You give the kingdom to whom You will and You strip the kingdom from whom You will.” 2:107 is that ownership as an argument: He who owns the two houses owns the timing of a verse.

*Al-samāwāti wa al-arḍ*, the heavens and the earth — the pair of 2:22, 2:29, 2:116–117 soon. Nothing in the pair is a partner in legislation. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, read the verse as a consolation to the Prophet (ﷺ) and the believers: if a party of the Book and the polytheists do not love your *khayr*, they do not sit on a throne that could stop it. Al-Qurṭubī hears a legal fruit: no *wali* and no *naṣīr* besides Allah (الله) means that a ruling of His is not to be vetoed by a helper you fear or a guardian you love. 2:105’s envy and 2:106’s *naskh* both die here, if the knowing is used.

*Wa mā lakum min dūni Allāhi min waliyyin wa lā naṣīr*, and you have not, besides Allah, of a guardian nor a helper. *Wali* is a near protector, a patron, the word of 2:257’s “Allah is the *Walī* of those who believe.” *Naṣīr* is a helper in a fight. The two cover the peace of patronage and the war of aid. 2:120 will repeat the pair as a threat: if you follow their desires after knowledge, you will have no *wali* and no *naṣīr*. 2:107 gives the pair as a fact before the threat. Ibn Kathīr treats the *mā* and the *min* as a double emptying: no guardian at all, no helper at all, besides Him. A believer who still keeps a secret patron — a magician of 2:102, a tribe, a donor who owns a fatwa, a state that must be pleased before a verse is recited — has not yet answered *a-lam taʿlam*.

The Prophet (ﷺ) taught this emptying as a living *tawakkul*. He said, “If you ask, ask Allah; if you seek help, seek help from Allah” (al-Tirmidhī, the ḥadīth of Ibn ʿAbbās). He said, “Be mindful of Allah, He will be mindful of you. Be mindful of Allah, you will find Him before you” (the same). He said, on the authority of Ibn ʿAbbās, that seventy thousand of his *ummah* would enter the Garden without reckoning, those who do not seek *ruqyah* as a profession of dependence, do not brand, do not see omens, and upon their Lord they rely (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:107 is that reliance as a cosmology: the two worlds are His *mulk*; your file of helpers is empty except Him.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd al-mulk* and *tawḥīd al-walāyah*. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that a servant’s *istiʿānah* must match his *ʿibādah*: you do not worship Him and then hire another as *naṣīr*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the heart has a throne; if *mulk al-samāwāt wa al-arḍ* is not seated there, a smaller *mulk* will be — a spouse, a wage, a party of the Book whose pleasure 2:120 forbids. Al-Rāzī noted the turn from “He is capable of every thing” to “His is the kingdom”: capability without ownership could be a hired power; ownership without capability could be a title. The two verses give both.

This *ummah* is spoken to in hours when a qiblah changes, a ruling is timed, a gift is envied, a mocker says the religion is unstable. 2:107 says the religion is as stable as the Owner. The heavens have not been given another king since 2:29’s *istiwāʾ* upon the *ʿarsh* in the language of the Book. “He is Allah, there is no god but He, King, Holy…” (59:23). A helper besides Him is 2:22’s *nidd*.

The Prophet (ﷺ) taught, in the opening of the daily *ṣalāh* in some of the reports of *duʿāʾ al-istiftāḥ*, a praise of a King of the heavens and the earth. 2:107 is that King as an argument, not only as a praise.
