Al-Ṭabarī reads the question *a-lam taʿlam* as a knowing already planted in the *fiṭrah* and in 2:29’s creation: a kingdom this wide is not co-owned by a rabbi’s objection or a pagan’s idol. 3:189 will repeat the *mulk* and add that Allah (الله) is over all things capable, stitching 2:106 and 2:107 into one later verse. Ibn Kathīr treats *min waliyyin wa lā naṣīr* as a comfort in a Madinah of tribes: your *walāʾ* is not Aws or Khazraj as a lord, not a Jewish neighbor as a shield, not Quraysh as a market. 5:51, 5:57 will later time a *walāʾ* of religion. 2:107 plants the root: besides Him, the file is empty. A servant who has understood this will still keep 2:109’s pardon. Emptiness of a patron is not fullness of a hate.

For the reader the verse is a census of patrons. Who, besides Allah (الله), do you treat as able to veto a verse, to stop a *khayr*, to save you from a Decree? Empty the file. You have no *wali* and no *naṣīr* besides Him. The next verse will warn the believers not to fill that empty file with demands upon the Messenger (ﷺ) like the demands Israel put upon Mūsā (peace be upon him). Whoever exchanges faith for disbelief has strayed from the even way.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:108

> Or do you ˹believers˺ intend to ask of your Messenger as Moses was asked before? But whoever trades belief for disbelief has truly strayed from the Right Way.

**Commentary:**

The emptied file of helpers is not to be refilled with a prophet treated as a vending of signs: *am turīdūna an tasʾalū rasūlakum kamā suʾila Mūsā min qablu wa man yatabaddali al-kufra bi-al-īmāni fa-qad ḍalla sawāʾa al-sabīl*, “Or do you intend to ask your Messenger as Mūsā was asked before? And whoever exchanges disbelief for faith has already strayed from the evenness of the way.” *Am turīdūna* is a question of will, of wanting. The addressees, Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, al-Ḥasan, as al-Ṭabarī gathers, are in one line the believers who might imitate Israel’s questions, and in another the People of the Book and Quraysh who demanded a Book from the sky, a gold of Ṣafā, a seeing of Allah (الله) openly. Ibn al-Jawzī listed the reports: Rāfiʿ ibn Ḥuraymilah asking that a book be sent down from heaven; Quraysh asking that Ṣafā be gold; a demand that this prophet’s atonement be like Israel’s; Ibn Abī Umayyah asking that Allah and the angels be brought as a guarantee; springs and mountains moved. 17:90–93 is the dossier of those demands. 4:153 is Israel’s: show us Allah openly. 2:55 was that seeing, and the thunderbolt. 2:67–71 was the cow’s questions that almost did not slaughter. 2:108 says to this *ummah*: do not make your Messenger (ﷺ) a second Mūsā in the trial of *suʾāl*.

*Rasūlakum*, your Messenger — a mercy of ascription. He is yours as 2:104’s sitting is yours. Asking him as Israel asked Mūsā is a misuse of “yours.” The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Leave me with what I have left you. Those before you were destroyed by their excessive questioning and their differing over their prophets” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, the ḥadīth of the pilgrimage’s *ḥajj*). He said, “The most criminal of the Muslims is he who asks about a thing not forbidden, then it is forbidden because of his asking” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 5:101: “O you who believe, do not ask about things which, if made clear to you, would distress you.” 2:108 is that *adab* as a comparison with a destroyed pattern.

*Wa man yatabaddali al-kufra bi-al-īmāni*, and whoever exchanges *kufr* for *īmān*. *Tabaddala* is the trade of 2:16, 2:61, 2:86. The asking, if it is the asking of a challenge, of a condition of faith, of a “we will not believe until,” is a trade. Ibn Kathīr reads the clause as a naming of where such asking ends: not in more knowledge, in *kufr*. *Fa-qad ḍalla sawāʾa al-sabīl*, he has already strayed from the evenness of the way. *Sawāʾ* is the middle, the even, the straight of 1:6 without the *ʿalayhim* and the *ḍāllīn*. 2:108 puts a man who conditions faith upon a custom sign onto that lost evenness.

Theologically the verse is the difference between a question of learning and a question of bargaining. The Companions asked. “Is the Hajj every year?” ʿUmar asked, and was told to leave it (the reports). They asked about the crescent, the spending, the fighting in the sacred month, and verses came. 2:189, 2:215, 2:217. Those questions were *suʾāl* of heirs. Israel’s seeing, Quraysh’s gold, Rāfiʿ’s book from the sky, were *suʾāl* of creditors. A messenger is not a debtor of miracles. He is a bearer of 2:119’s good news and warning. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that a heart that will not move until the mountain moves has already chosen *kufr* as a possibility it is comfortable with. Al-Rāzī noted *am turīdūna* as a check on the believers: you have seen Israel’s questions in this sūrah; do not envy them that style.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, hears a rule of *iftāʾ*: do not press a scholar for a tightness the text has left wide, and do not invent a fifth *ṣalāh* by a curiosity. The *dīn* is easy. “I have been sent with the ḥanīfiyyah, the easy” (the well-known meaning of the reports). 2:108 is ease protected from the cow’s four questions.

This *ummah* is tried by a modern *suʾāl*: we will not believe until a laboratory, until a political win, until a custom of our fathers is baptized, until a verse is rewritten to our century. That is *tabaddul*. The even way is 2:2’s *hudan lil-muttaqīn*, a Book, a prayer, an unseen. The Prophet (ﷺ) split the moon, and they said sorcery (54:1–2). A sign does not create a will. 2:6 has already said as much.

The even way of 1:6 is walked with a Book already in the hand, not with a mountain demanded in the sitting. A question of an heir builds a *fiqh*; a question of a creditor builds a Fire. Do not envy Israel its style of *suʾāl*. Al-Ṭabarī gathers that some of the believers, newly arrived from a Makkah of signs demanded, might have wished a sign of their own to silence a neighbor. 2:108 says that wish is a resemblance to Israel, and resemblance there is a *ḍalāl*. Ibn Kathīr notes *sawāʾ al-sabīl* as the middle of 1:6, the evenness a man leaves when he makes a prophet a vending-place. The cow of 2:67 was a command that questions made hard. This *ummah* was given a ḥajj without a yearly obligation after a question that was almost a tightness. The Prophet (ﷺ) praised a man who did not ask. 2:108 is that praise as a warning. Knowledge in 2:120 will have come; do not spend it on a theatre of *suʾāl*.

For the reader the verse is a fast from bargaining. Ask to obey. Do not ask to stall. Whoever trades faith for covering has left the even road. The next verse will name another pressure upon that road: many of the People of the Book wish to turn you back after faith, out of envy, after the truth has become clear. Pardon and overlook until Allah (الله) brings His command. He is over all things capable.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:109

> Many among the People of the Book wish they could turn you ˹believers˺ back to disbelief because of their envy, after the truth has been made clear to them. Pardon and bear with them until Allah delivers His decision. Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything.

**Commentary:**

The even way is walked under a wish from next door: *wadda kathīrun min ahli al-kitābi law yaruddūnakum min baʿdi īmānikum kuffāran ḥasadan min ʿindi anfusihim min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahumu al-ḥaqqu fa-ʿfū wa iṣfaḥū ḥattā yaʾtiya Allāhu bi-amrihi inna Allāha ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr*, “Many of the People of the Book would love to turn you back after your faith into disbelievers, out of envy from their own souls, after the truth has become clear to them. So pardon and overlook until Allah brings His command. Indeed Allah is over all things capable.” *Wadda kathīrun*, many would love — not all, the justice of 2:83, 2:101’s *farīq*. *Law yaruddūnakum min baʿdi īmānikum kuffāran*, if only they could return you, after your faith, as disbelievers. The wish is not a debate. It is a return. 3:100, 3:149, 4:89 will repeat the wish. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, is associated with reports of Ḥuyayy ibn Akhṭab and Abū Yāsir his brother, who could not bear the faith of the Anṣār they had once briefed; of Kaʿb ibn al-Ashraf; of an invitation to Ḥudhayfah and ʿAmmār to return. The names are a *sabab*. The verse is a standing law of chests: a truth that has become clear and is not taken becomes a wish that others also not take it.

*Ḥasadan min ʿindi anfusihim*, envy from at their souls. *Ḥasad* is 2:90’s *baghy*, 2:105’s *mā yawaddu*, now named. *Min ʿindi anfusihim* is from the presence of their own souls — not from a Book, not from a proof. The *ʿinda* they lacked for a numbered-days covenant in 2:80 is here an *ʿinda* of envy, a private mint. *Min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahumu al-ḥaqq*, after the truth has become distinct to them. *Tabayyana* is 2:109’s cruelty and its justice: they see. 2:89, they recognized. 2:146, they know him as they know their sons. Envy after clarity is a worse *kufr* than envy in a fog.

*Fa-ʿfū wa iṣfaḥū*, so pardon and overlook. Two verbs of a wide chest. *ʿAfū* is to erase a mark. *Iṣfaḥū* is to turn a page, to give the broad side of a thing, not the edge. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī record that many of the *salaf* took this as a command of a stage, *ḥattā yaʾtiya Allāhu bi-amrihi*, until Allah brings His command — and that the command came as the leave to fight, 22:39, as 9:29, as the sentence upon Banū Qurayẓah and the exile of Banū al-Naḍīr, as 2:190’s *qitāl* in its conditions. Others held that pardon of a personal insult remains even after a political command. The two readings can live: a state may be given a *ḥukm* of war; a chest is never given a *ḥukm* of *ḥasad* in return. The Prophet (ﷺ) in Makkah pardoned; in Madinah he fought who fought, and he still said, “The strong is not the one who throws in a wrestling; the strong is he who controls himself in anger” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He pardoned the man who had killed his uncle Ḥamzah when that man came in *islām*. 2:109’s *ʿafū* and *ṣafḥ* are not naivety. They are a timed *akhlāq* under a capable Lord.

*Ḥattā yaʾtiya Allāhu bi-amrihi*, until Allah brings His command. The believers are not the clock. *Inna Allāha ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr*, indeed Allah is over all things capable — the close of 2:106 again, so that pardon will not feel like weakness. Capability in the hand of a man who has been told to pardon is a test of *tawakkul*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *ʿafū* before a command of fighting is patience, and *ʿafū* after a victory is *iḥsān*, and both are worship. Al-Rāzī noted *kathīrun* as a relief: you are not surrounded by a monolith; many, not all.

The Prophet (ﷺ) taught the daily *ʿafū*. He said, “Charity does not decrease wealth. Allah does not increase a servant for his pardon except in honor. And no one humbles himself for Allah except that Allah raises him” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “Whoever does not show mercy will not be shown mercy” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:109 is that mercy aimed at a specific fire: the envy of a lettered neighbor who wants your *īmān* reversed.

This *ummah* is tried by two misreadings. One is a pardon that never names envy, never guards a convert, never sees 3:118’s “do not take intimates from outside yourselves; they will spare nothing to ruin you.” One is a war of chests that never heard *fa-ʿfū wa iṣfaḥū* and treats every Jew and Christian as 2:109’s *kathīr*. The verse forbids both. Many wish. Pardon until the command. He is capable.

Al-Ṭabarī records that *ḥasad min ʿindi anfusihim* is a *ḥasad* not required by a Torah, not paid as a wage by a Lord — a private fire. 42:14 said they did not divide until after knowledge had come, *baghyan baynahum*. 2:109 is that *baghy* aimed at a new *ummah*’s faith. Ibn Kathīr treats *ḥattā yaʾtiya Allāhu bi-amrihi* as a schooling in clocks: the believers in early Madinah were not yet given 2:190. A man who fights before a leave is not more pious than a man who pardons under a leave. A man who refuses to fight after a leave, citing 2:109 as if the *ḥattā* never arrived, has frozen a stage. The verse teaches both the wideness and the clock.

For the reader the verse is a timed wideness. Do not return envy as envy. Do not call a wish to unbelief a small social fact. Pardon, overlook, wait upon the *amr*. The next verse will give the believers work while they wait: establish the prayer, give the *zakāh*; whatever good you send forth for yourselves you will find with Allah (الله). He sees what you do.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:110

> Establish prayer, and pay alms-tax. Whatever good you send forth for yourselves, you will ˹certainly˺ find ˹its reward˺ with Allah. Surely Allah is All-Seeing of what you do.

**Commentary:**

Pardon is not an empty waiting. It is filled with the two pillars that 2:3 named in the *muttaqūn*: *wa aqīmū al-ṣalāta wa ātū al-zakāta wa mā tuqaddimū li-anfusikum min khayrin tajidūhu ʿinda Allāhi inna Allāha bimā taʿmalūna baṣīr*, “And establish the prayer and give the *zakāh*. And whatever good you send forth for yourselves, you will find it with Allah. Indeed Allah is, of what you do, Seeing.” *Aqīmū al-ṣalāh* is not “perform a prayer.” It is make it stand, as a tent is made to stand, as 2:3, as 2:43 to Israel, as 2:83. Establishment is times, stillness, *khushūʿ*, a recitation, a facing. *Ātū al-zakāh* is give the purifying due, 2:43, 2:83, the due ʿAlī (may Allah be pleased with him) said is a due beside *zakāh* also in 2:177. Here the two are named together as the believers’ work while 2:109’s *ḥattā* has not yet arrived. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, read the verse as a turning from the neighbor’s envy to the servant’s file. You cannot control a wish in another chest. You can stand a prayer. You can give a due.

*Wa mā tuqaddimū li-anfusikum min khayrin tajidūhu ʿinda Allāh*, and what you send forward for yourselves of good, you will find it with Allah. *Tuqaddimū* is to send ahead, as a traveler sends a mount. 2:95 said they will never wish death because of what their hands had sent forward. 2:110 says send forward *khayr* and you will find it. The same verb, two cargos. *Li-anfusikum*, for yourselves — the good is not a leak. It is a deposit in your own name. *ʿInda Allāh*, with Allah — the *ʿinda* of 2:103’s *mathūbah*, the presence that 2:80 could not produce as a numbered covenant. Finding is 99:7–8: “Whoever does an atom’s weight of good will see it.” The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “When a servant dies, his work is cut off except from three: a continuing charity, knowledge benefited from, a righteous child who prays for him” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “Protect yourselves from the Fire even with half a date” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:110 is that sending as a law of finding.

*Inna Allāha bimā taʿmalūna baṣīr*, indeed Allah is, of what you do, Seeing. *Baṣīr* is the Name of a seeing that does not miss a *khushūʿ* in a dark room, a date in a hidden palm, an envy you did not return, a pardon you did not advertise. 2:96 ended *baṣīr* upon a clinging. 2:110 ends *baṣīr* upon a sending. Ibn Kathīr treats the close as both promise and warning: He sees the prayer that is established and the prayer that is a peck of 107:5; He sees the *zakāh* given and the *zakāh* delayed with 9:34’s gold. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, hears the pairing of *ṣalāh* and *zakāh* as the two that Abū Bakr (may Allah be pleased with him) would not separate: “By Allah, I will fight whoever distinguishes between prayer and *zakāh*” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:110 is that pairing in a verse of waiting.

Theologically the verse is the *ummah*’s answer to 2:105’s *mā yawaddu*. They do not love that *khayr* be sent down upon you. You send *khayr* forward anyway, and you find it with the One who chooses mercy for whom He wills. Envy cannot rob a deposit at *ʿinda Allāh*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the servant’s true wealth is what he has sent, not what he has stored, and that *ṣalāh* is a sending of a day, *zakāh* a sending of a year, a hidden *ṣadaqah* a sending no neighbor need clap. Al-Rāzī noted *li-anfusikum* as a killing of the boast: you are not feeding Allah (الله). You are feeding a self that will arrive.

This *ummah* is spoken to in every pause between a harm and a command. The pause is not a scroll. It is a prayer that stands. It is a due that leaves the pocket. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The first of what the servant is reckoned for on the Day of Rising is the prayer; if it is sound, the rest of his work is sound” (al-Tirmidhī, al-Nasāʾī, in the well-known reports). He said *zakāh* is a bridge, a *qanṭarah*, in the speech of some of the *salaf*. 2:110 puts both under a Seeing.

A waiting that has no *ṣalāh* and no *zakāh* is a waiting that 2:8 could wear. Send the two pillars while the *ḥattā* of 2:109 has not yet struck the clock. Al-Ṭabarī reads *tajidūhu* as a finding that will not miss, in a grave’s first night and on a Day of a scale. 73:20: whatever good you send forth for yourselves, you will find it with Allah, better and greater in reward. 2:110 is that later verse’s seed. Ibn Kathīr notes that *ṣalāh* and *zakāh* are named because they are the two that a hypocrite finds heavy, 9:54, and the two that a believer sends when a neighbor’s envy has no handle. You cannot send a neighbor’s chest to *īmān* by force. You can send a rakʿah and a date. *Baṣīr* is a Name that makes a hidden date public in a Book of a Day, and makes a public prayer empty if the limbs pecked. This *ummah*’s waiting-rooms — a hospital, a delay of a ruling, a harm not yet judged — are 2:110 rooms.

For the reader the verse is a work-order. While you pardon, stand. While you overlook, give. Send *khayr*. You will find it. He sees. The next verses will turn from this work to a claim of the neighbors: the Jews say none will enter the Garden except a Jew; the Christians say none except a Christian. Those are their wishes. Say: bring your proof if you are truthful. 2:111 will ask for a *burhān* the way 2:80 asked for a covenant.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:111

> The Jews and Christians each claim that none will enter Paradise except those of their own faith. These are their desires. Reply, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Show ˹me˺ your proof if what you say is true.”

**Commentary:**

The work of prayer and *zakāh* is set beside a slogan of exclusive gardens: *wa qālū lan yadkhula al-jannata illā man kāna hūdan aw naṣārā tilka amāniyyuhum qul hātū burhānakum in kuntum ṣādiqīn*, “And they say: none will enter the Garden except one who is a Jew or a Christian. Those are their wishes. Say: bring your proof if you are truthful.” *Qālū* is they said, a they of two camps that do not agree with each other and agree in a grammar of exclusion. Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, Qatādah, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, said the Jews of Madinah said none enters except a Jew, and the Christians of Najrān said none enters except a Christian. 2:113 will make their mutual negation explicit. 2:111 records the shared form: *lan yadkhula*, none will enter, *illā man kāna hūdan aw naṣārā*. *Hūd* is Jews as 2:62, 2:135. *Naṣārā* is Christians as 2:62. Each camp hears the *aw* as its own name. The Garden of 2:25, 2:82 is treated as a tribal lease.

*Tilka amāniyyuhum*, those are their wishes. *Amānī* is 2:78’s word: the unlettered who do not know the Book except wishes. Here the lettered have wishes too. A wish that has put on the clothes of a creed is still a wish until a *burhān* sits under it. Al-Qurṭubī notes the demonstrative *tilka* as a pointing that belittles: those, not a revelation, not a covenant of 2:80 that they could not produce. Ibn Kathīr treats *amānī* as hopes without wings. 4:123 will say it to this *ummah* as well: “It is not by your wishes, nor by the wishes of the People of the Book. Whoever does evil will be recompensed for it.” 2:111 is that verse’s first half.

*Qul hātū burhānakum in kuntum ṣādiqīn*, say: bring your proof if you are truthful. *Hātū* is the same bring of 2:80’s *qul hātū burhānakum*. *Burhān* is a proof that shines, a demonstration, not a heritage, not a census of fathers, not 2:94’s “if the Abode is yours, wish death.” They did not wish death. They will not bring a *burhān*. The Prophet (ﷺ) is told *qul*, say — a short classroom. Truthfulness is the condition they claim; the proof is the homework they skip. 21:24: “Or have they taken gods besides Him? Say: bring your proof.” 27:64: “Bring your proof if you are truthful.” The Qur'an is not afraid of evidence. It is afraid of *amānī* dressed as evidence.

Theologically the verse is the killing of salvation by slogan. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that entry to the Garden is by *īmān* and *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* as the Book repeats, not by a *nisbah* to a prophet. A Jew who rejects a prophet his Book named, a Christian who makes a son of a servant, a Muslim who recites and does not do, are three kinds of *amāniyy*. 2:112 will give the positive: whoever submits his face to Allah (الله) and is a doer of *iḥsān*. Al-Rāzī noted that *lan yadkhula* is a claim about the unseen, and a claim about the unseen without a report from the Owner of the unseen is a wish. 2:80 was numbered days; 2:111 is a numbered people. Both are *amānī*.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “By Him in Whose hand is the soul of Muḥammad, any of this *ummah*, Jew or Christian, who hears of me and then dies without believing in what I was sent with, will be of the people of the Fire” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, the ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah). He also said, “The deeds are only by intentions” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He did not make the Garden a Muslim slogan in the style of 2:111. He made it a following. “All of my *ummah* will enter the Garden except whoever refuses.” They said, Who refuses? He said, “Whoever obeys me enters, whoever disobeys me has refused” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:111’s *burhān* in this *ummah* is that obedience, not a passport.

This *ummah* is tried by its own *lan yadkhula illā*. A school that says none is saved except our *madhhab*, a people who say none is saved except our ethnicity, a preacher who says none is saved except those who love him, have sat in 2:111’s grammar. *Hātū burhānakum*. The Book is the *burhān*. 2:2. 4:174. A wish is not.

Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *aw* as the narrator’s joining of two slogans, not as one slogan of a coalition. They did not love each other into a shared Garden. They each loved themselves into an exclusive one. 2:113 will show the love’s other face: each says the other has nothing.

A Garden leased by a slogan is 2:80’s numbered days in a new grammar. Al-Ṭabarī notes that *hūdan aw naṣārā* is two slogans joined by a narrator, not a coalition. Each camp would be angry to be named in the other’s breath, and both are named in one *amānī*. 2:94 had already asked the Jews to wish death if the Abode was theirs. They did not. 2:111 asks for a *burhān* they will not bring. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as a mercy to this *ummah*: you are not told to answer a slogan with a slogan. You are told to ask for proof, then 2:112 will give the Owner’s proof. A religion that cannot survive a *hātū burhānakum* is a wish. A religion that answers with 2:2’s Book and 2:112’s face is not.

For the reader the verse is a fast from inherited heavens. If your hope of entry is a name of a community without a *burhān* of submission and *iḥsān*, you have *amānī*. Bring proof. The next verse will bring the proof from the Owner: no — whoever submits his face to Allah and is a doer of good, his reward is with his Lord, and no fear upon them, nor shall they grieve.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:112

> But no! Whoever submits themselves to Allah and does good will have their reward with their Lord. And there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve.

**Commentary:**

The wish is broken with a *balā*, a yes that is a no to exclusion: *balā man aslama wajhahu lillāhi wa huwa muḥsinun fa-lahu ajruhu ʿinda rabbihi wa lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa lā hum yaḥzanūn*, “Yes — whoever submits his face to Allah while he is a doer of good, his reward is with his Lord, and no fear shall be upon them, nor shall they grieve.” *Balā* is the particle that affirms a negated truth. They said none will enter except a Jew or a Christian. *Balā*: entry is real, and its door is not that slogan. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, read *aslama wajhahu* as he made his *wajh*, his face, his self, his direction, over to Allah (الله) in *tawḥīd*. The face is the noblest of the surfaces; to submit it is to submit the rest. 3:20, 4:125, 31:22 repeat the phrase. 4:125: “Who is better in religion than one who submits his face to Allah while he is a *muḥsin* and follows the creed of Ibrāhīm ḥanīfan?” 2:112 is that better religion as the answer to 2:111.

*Wa huwa muḥsin*, while he is a doer of *iḥsān*. *Iḥsān* is the third of the ḥadīth of Jibrīl (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim): that you worship Allah as if you see Him, and if you do not see Him, He sees you. It is also the excellence of a deed, the beauty of a dealing, 16:90’s command. The *wāw* of *wa huwa muḥsin* is a *ḥāl*, a state: submission of a face is not a slogan either. It is a state of doing well. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, hears both *islām* of the heart and *islām* of the limbs. A face in the dust without a *muḥsin*’s work is 2:8. A work without a face submitted is 2:264’s reminder-charity. Both are required.

*Fa-lahu ajruhu ʿinda rabbihi*, so his reward is with his Lord. *Ajr* is the wage of 2:62, 2:103’s *mathūbah*. *ʿInda rabbihi*, with his Lord — the *ʿinda* again, a presence, a storage that envy cannot rob. *Rabb* is the Lord of 1:2, the Nurturer, not a tribal deity of *hūd* or *naṣārā*. The wage is ascribed to a Lord, not to a party. *Wa lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa lā hum yaḥzanūn*, and no fear upon them, nor shall they grieve — the refrain of 2:38, 2:62, 2:277, 5:69, 10:62. Fear is of a future; grief is of a past. The Garden’s people are emptied of both. Ibn Kathīr treats the dual as the full safety 2:38 promised Adam’s children if a guidance came and they followed.

The Prophet (ﷺ) was asked which Islam is best, and he said, “That you feed food and greet with the *salām* those you know and those you do not” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “*Iḥsān* is that you worship Allah as if you see Him” (the ḥadīth of Jibrīl). He said, “Allah has written *iḥsān* upon every thing; so when you kill, kill well, and when you slaughter, slaughter well” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:112’s *muḥsin* is that written *iḥsān* upon a submitted face. He also recited that Allah (الله) said, “I have forbidden oppression upon Myself and made it forbidden among you” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, ḥadīth qudsī). A submitted face that oppresses has not submitted.

Theologically the verse is the universal of 2:62 made precise after 2:111’s slogan. 2:62 named those who believe, Jews, Christians, Sabeans, who believe in Allah and the Last Day and do ṣāliḥ — they have a wage, no fear, no grief. 2:112 names the inner form: *islām* of the face, *iḥsān* of the state. After the sending of Muḥammad (ﷺ), that *islām* is the *islām* he brought; a rejection of him is not a submitted face. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that 2:62 and 2:112 do not save a man who knows this Messenger and then prefers a wish. They save the *muḥsin* who meets Allah having submitted in the truth that reached him, and they close the door of *amānī*. Al-Rāzī noted *man* as a whoever: the door is a quality, not a genealogy.

This *ummah* is spoken to with a warning inside the comfort. You have the right slogan if you say *islām*. You do not have 2:112 if the face is not submitted and the state is not *iḥsān*. “The Muslim is he from whose tongue and hand the Muslims are safe” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A face in *sujūd* and a hand in harm is a split the verse does not bless.

Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the face is the map of the heart: a heart that has *aslama* will show in a face that does not turn to every *rabb* of a trend. 30:30’s *wajh* for the *dīn* is 2:112’s *wajh* for the Lord.

Al-Ṭabarī reads *aslama wajhahu* as *akhlaṣa*, he made his *dīn* sincere. 98:5: they were not commanded except to worship Allah, making the *dīn* sincere to Him, ḥunafāʾ. 2:112 is that sincerity as a face. Ibn Kathīr notes the refrain *lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa lā hum yaḥzanūn* as the opposite of a slogan’s anxiety: a tribe that has leased a Garden is always afraid the lease will be inspected. A *muḥsin* who has submitted stores a wage with a Lord who does not break a promise. 39:10: the patient will be given their wage without reckoning. 2:112’s *ajr* is that trust. This *ummah* must not hear *balā* as a compliment to a passport. Hear it as a work of a face and a state.

For the reader the verse is the *burhān* 2:111 asked. Not a tribe. A face. A state of *iḥsān*. A wage with a Lord. No fear, no grief. The next verse will show the two camps of 2:111 at war with each other: the Jews say the Christians have nothing; the Christians say the Jews have nothing; both recite the Book; those who have no knowledge say the same. Allah (الله) will judge between them on the Day of Rising.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:113

> The Jews say, “The Christians have nothing to stand on” and the Christians say, “The Jews have nothing to stand on,” although both recite the Scriptures. And those ˹pagans˺ who have no knowledge say the same ˹about people of faith˺. Surely Allah will judge between them on the Day of Judgment regarding their dispute.

**Commentary:**

The exclusive gardens become exclusive emptinesses: *wa qālati al-yahūdu laysati al-naṣārā ʿalā shayʾin wa qālati al-naṣārā laysati al-yahūdu ʿalā shayʾin wa hum yatlūna al-kitāba kadhālika qāla alladhīna lā yaʿlamūna mithla qawlihim fa-Allāhu yaḥkumu baynahum yawma al-qiyāmati fī-mā kānū fīhi yakhtalifūn*, “The Jews say, ‘The Christians are not upon anything,’ and the Christians say, ‘The Jews are not upon anything,’ though they recite the Book. Likewise those who do not know said the like of their saying. Allah will judge between them on the Day of Rising concerning that in which they used to differ.” *Laysat ʿalā shayʾ*, not upon a thing — a total emptying. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, said that when the Christians of Najrān came to the Prophet (ﷺ), the Jews said they were upon nothing; and the Christians said the Jews were upon nothing. Each camp’s *amānī* of 2:111 has a shadow: if only we enter, then they enter upon nothing. The grammar is a war of *shayʾ*, of somethingness.

*Wa hum yatlūna al-kitāb*, and they recite the Book. The *wāw* is a *ḥāl* of scandal. Recitation should have taught them that Mūsā (peace be upon him) is true and that ʿĪsā (peace be upon him) is true, that a Book is not a license to empty a people who also recite. Al-Qurṭubī hears the clause as a rebuke of recitation without *inṣāf*, fairness. 2:44 asked those who recite the Book and forget themselves. 2:113 asks those who recite and empty one another. The Torah praises a Messiah; the Injīl does not unwrite Mūsā. A reciter who says the other is upon nothing has used a Book as a flag.

*Kadhālika qāla alladhīna lā yaʿlamūna mithla qawlihim*, likewise those who do not know said the like of their saying. The unlettered pagans of ʿArabah, Ibn ʿAbbās and Qatādah said, said of Jews and Christians, and of the Muslims, that they were upon nothing. Ignorance imitates the lettered when the lettered have modeled a total emptying. Ibn Kathīr treats the line as a humiliation of the reciters: your sentence has become a pagan’s sentence. Knowledge that produces the same mouth as no-knowledge is 2:78 and 2:101’s as-if-they-did-not-know again.

*Fa-Allāhu yaḥkumu baynahum yawma al-qiyāmati fī-mā kānū fīhi yakhtalifūn*, so Allah will judge between them on the Day of Rising in what they used to differ. 2:113 does not appoint the believers as a final court over every dispute of Jews and Christians in this world. It appoints the Day. 3:55, 5:48, 10:93, 16:124, 22:69, 32:25, 39:3, 45:17 — the refrain of a Lord who judges *ikhtilāf*. A *daʿwah* remains. A *jizyah* may remain. A fairness of 5:8 remains. The last word on a synagogue and a church’s quarrel about *shayʾ* is His. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that this clause cools a chest that wants to finish every argument in a sitting. Some arguments are timed for a Day.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “I am the nearest of people to ʿĪsā son of Maryam in this world and the next. Prophets are half-brothers; their mothers differ and their *dīn* is one” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He did not say ʿĪsā was upon nothing. He said, “Do not prefer me over Mūsā” in a well-known ḥadīth of humility (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), while the Book prefers in 2:253 as Allah (الله) prefers. 2:113’s emptying is not his *sunnah*. His *sunnah* is 2:285: we do not distinguish between any of His messengers. This *ummah* is warned: do not become a fourth camp that says the three are upon nothing while reciting a fourth Book. Recite, and do not empty a truth the Book has named.

Al-Rāzī noted that *ikhtilāf* here is the difference of a claim, not the difference of a *fiqh* inside a shared *qiblah*. 4:59’s returning of a dispute to Allah and the Messenger is for this *ummah*’s inner law. 2:113’s *ikhtilāf* is the larger one of *millah*. Both have a judge. One is judged in a Book we have; one is judged on a Day we wait.

Theologically the verse is a lesson in *inṣāf*. A truth-teller does not need the other to be upon nothing. He needs the other to be upon what he is upon, and not upon what he is not. Jews are upon a remnant of a Book and upon a rejection. Christians are upon a remnant and upon a son. Muslims who stray are upon a recitation and upon a *bidʿah*. The sentence “upon nothing” is a pagan’s convenience.

Al-Ṭabarī records that some of the pagans said of the Prophet’s (ﷺ) *dīn* the same *laysū ʿalā shayʾ*. A lettered emptying had taught an unlettered emptying. 22:17 will list those who believe, those who are Jews, the Sabeans, the Christians, the Magians, those who associate, and say Allah (الله) will judge between them on the Day of Rising. 2:113 is that list in a quarrel of two. Ibn Kathīr treats *yatlūna al-kitāb* as the scandal that should have produced *inṣāf* and produced a void instead. Recitation is not a guarantee of fairness. 62:5’s donkey of books is a neighbor of this verse. This *ummah* recites. Let it not empty a truth the recitation has named.

For the reader the verse is a check of your emptying. Whom do you call “upon nothing” while you recite? Leave the last judgment to the Day. Speak a truth that does not need a total void next door. The next verse will name a worse wrong than a slogan: who is more unjust than one who prevents the mention of Allah’s Name in His mosques and strives to ruin them? They should not enter except in fear. For them disgrace in this world and a tremendous punishment in the next.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:114

> Who does more wrong than those who prevent Allah’s Name from being mentioned in His places of worship and strive to destroy them? Such people have no right to enter these places except with fear. For them is disgrace in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter.

**Commentary:**

Slogans about a Garden are followed by a crime against a house of mention: *wa man aẓlamu mimman manaʿa masājida Allāhi an yudhkara fīhā ismuhu wa saʿā fī kharābihā ulāʾika mā kāna lahum an yadkhulūhā illā khāʾifīna lahum fī al-dunyā khizyun wa lahum fī al-ākhirati ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm*, “And who is more unjust than one who prevents the mosques of Allah from having His Name mentioned in them and strives in their ruin? Those — it was not for them to enter them except in fear. For them in this world is disgrace, and for them in the Hereafter is a tremendous punishment.” *Man aẓlamu*, who is more unjust — the superlative of 2:20’s storm, of 6:21’s *ifk* upon Allah (الله). Preventing a Name in a house of Allah is placed in that rank. *Masājid* is plural of *masjid*, a place of *sujūd*. The ascription *masājid Allāh* is the honor: His, not a tribe’s. *An yudhkara fīhā ismuhu*, that His Name be mentioned in them — prayer, recitation, *tahlīl*, a lesson. *Wa saʿā fī kharābihā*, and strove in their ruin — a physical wreck, or a emptying of mention that is a wreck of meaning.

The *salaf* differed on the first *sabab*. Ibn ʿAbbās and Qatādah, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, spoke of the Romans, or of Nebuchadnezzar and the ruin of Bayt al-Maqdis, a house of *sujūd* before this *ummah*’s five. Others, including Ibn Zayd, spoke of Quraysh at al-Ḥudaybiyyah, barring the Prophet (ﷺ) and the believers from the Masjid al-Ḥarām. 22:25 names that barring. 8:34: “What have they that Allah should not punish them, while they bar from the Sacred Mosque?” Both readings teach: a house of Allah is not to be barred, not in Jerusalem, not in Makkah, not in a village masjid of this *ummah*. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, drew the standing *ḥukm*: to close a mosque without right, to wreck a *miḥrāb*, to forbid a Name, to turn a house of *sujūd* into a market of 24:36’s opposite, is a branch of this *ẓulm*. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever builds a mosque for Allah, Allah builds for him a house in the Garden” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The inverse is 2:114’s *saʿā fī kharāb*.

*Ulāʾika mā kāna lahum an yadkhulūhā illā khāʾifīn*, those — it was not for them to enter them except afraid. Ibn ʿAbbās read it as a *ḥukm*: they should not enter except in fear, as a *dhimmī* in some of the *fuqahāʾ*’s discussion of the Ḥaram, or as a criminal who enters a mosque under a law. Others read it as a report of what will be: they will enter only as frightened men, or they will not enter at ease. Ibn Kathīr prefers a meaning that they have no right of a confident entering. A bar-er of a mosque does not then stroll it as a lord.

*Lahum fī al-dunyā khizyun wa lahum fī al-ākhirati ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm*, for them in this world is disgrace, and for them in the Hereafter a tremendous punishment. *Khizy* is shame, a stripping of cover. Quraysh’s barring was followed by Fatḥ Makkah. Bayt al-Maqdis’s ruin was followed by a later mention, and by 17:4–7’s cycle. The *ʿadhāb ʿaẓīm* is 2:7’s adjective upon a worse crime than a sealed hearing: a sealed house. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Give good news of a complete light to those who walk to the mosques in the dark” (Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī). He said the prayer in congregation is better than a prayer alone by twenty-seven (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:114 is the opposite of that light: a striving that the dark stay dark.

Theologically the verse is the sanctity of a space of *dhikr*. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that the masjid is the house of the *ummah*’s heart, and that a people who ruin their mosques have begun to ruin their *ṣalāh*, and a people who ruin their *ṣalāh* have begun to ruin their *dīn*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *khizy* in this world is often a *qadar* that fits the crime: he who shames a house of Allah is shamed. Al-Rāzī noted *man aẓlamu* as a warning to this *ummah* not to think the superlative is only a Roman or a Qurashī. A lock on a masjid for a feud, a pulpit used to empty 2:113’s *shayʾ* upon a believer, a wrecking of a waqf, is a family of the verse.

This *ummah* is tried by a polite *kharāb*: mosques open and empty, Names mentioned as a whisper under a market’s noise, a generation that does not know the way to *sujūd*. 24:36 praised houses Allah has allowed to be raised, in which His Name is mentioned. 2:114 curses the prevention. Between them is a life of keys.

Al-Ṭabarī allows both *asbāb* — a ruin of Bayt al-Maqdis and a barring of the Ḥaram — so that the *ummah* will not fence the verse inside one century. 22:40 named those who were expelled for saying “Our Lord is Allah,” and named cloisters, churches, synagogues, and mosques in which His Name is much mentioned. 2:114 is the crime against that mention. Ibn Kathīr treats *khizy* as a worldly first payment, so that a bar-er will not think the tremendous punishment is the only invoice. This *ummah* must also hear: a masjid locked for a feud of a family, a pulpit used to bar a Name of a mercy, is a cousin of *saʿā fī kharāb*.

For the reader the verse is a guard of every masjid you can walk to. Do not bar a Name. Do not strive in a ruin. Enter in *khushūʿ*, not as a lord. Disgrace and a tremendous punishment sit on the other side of a locked *dhikr*. The next verse will console a people who might be barred from a direction, or lost in a dark, or on a camel in a *nāfilah*: to Allah belong the east and the west; wherever you turn, there is the Face of Allah. He is All-Encompassing, All-Knowing.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:115

> To Allah belong the east and the west, so wherever you turn you are facing ˹towards˺ Allah. Surely Allah is All-Encompassing, All-Knowing.

**Commentary:**

The barred mosque is answered with a Lord who is not barred: *wa lillāhi al-mashriqu wa al-maghribu fa-aynamā tuwallū fa-thamma wajhu Allāhi inna Allāha wāsiʿun ʿalīm*, “To Allah belong the east and the west, so wherever you turn, there is the Face of Allah. Indeed Allah is All-Encompassing, All-Knowing.” *Lillāh* is the *lām* of possession, 2:107’s *mulk* in two directions of the sun. East and west are His as 2:115’s opening, so a people cannot own a sunrise against Him. *Fa-aynamā tuwallū*, so wherever you turn your faces — a wherever of travel, of night, of fear, of a mistaken qiblah, of a *nāfilah* on a mount. *Fa-thamma wajhu Allāh*, there is the Face of Allah. *Wajh* is Face, as 2:112’s servant submitted his face; here the Lord’s Face is the direction of a seeking. Ahl al-Sunnah affirm what He affirmed, without a how, without a likeness, without a stripping. “Everything perishes except His Face” (28:88). “The Face of your Lord, of Majesty and Honor, remains” (55:27). 2:115 is that remaining as a comfort to a turning servant.

The *salaf* named more than one *sabab*, and they can live together as the verse’s width. Ibn ʿUmar (may Allah be pleased with him) said the verse came about the *nāfilah* upon a camel: the Prophet (ﷺ) would pray on his mount, facing wherever it faced, in the reports of Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (including Muslim’s 700 in its wordings). Ibn ʿAbbās and others spoke of a travel by night when the qiblah was not known, and they prayed, and it was lifted from them. Qatādah spoke of the prayer upon the Negus, al-Najāshī, when the Prophet (ﷺ) prayed the *janāzah* in Madinah and the Companions prayed with him though the body was in Abyssinia, a qiblah of a different land. Some of the *salaf* tied it to the coming change of qiblah, 2:142–150, a consolation to those who had prayed toward Bayt al-Maqdis: those prayers were not lost; east and west are His. Al-Qurṭubī gathers the legal fruit: the obligatory prayer has a qiblah, the Kaʿbah, when a man can face it; the verse is not a retirement of 2:144. It is a mercy in the *nāfilah* of travel, in a dark, in a fear, in a past direction that was commanded. Ibn Kathīr insists on that order so that no one will pray *ẓuhr* toward a whim.

*Inna Allāha wāsiʿun ʿalīm*, indeed Allah is All-Encompassing, All-Knowing. *Wāsiʿ* is wide, a Name of a mercy that east and west cannot fence. *ʿAlīm* is Knowing of a face that turned in a dark, of a *nāfilah* on a saddle, of a *janāzah* over a king who died unseen, of a first qiblah that He commanded and then replaced. The two Names kill a small religion: a religion that thinks Allah (الله) is only in one bearing of a compass. He commanded a bearing as a *ubūdiyyah*. He is not housed in it.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The earth has been made for me a mosque and a purification” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:115 is that hadith as a cosmology. He said, “Do not prefer me by saying a direction is His house as if He were absent from the other.” He taught a facing, and he taught a *wāsiʿ*. He said Allah (الله) descends to the lowest heaven in the last third of the night, in the ḥadīth of the descent (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), and Ahl al-Sunnah take it as they take *wajh*: a truth of His, not a body in a room. 2:115’s *wajh* is of that file.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd* of a direction and *tawḥīd* of a vastness together. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that the servant is commanded to face the House, and the Lord is not a body inside the House; the facing is a law, the Face is an attribute, the vastness is a Name. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that a heart that has found *fa-thamma wajhu Allāh* will not be wrecked when a door of a masjid is locked, though 2:114 remains a crime. Al-Rāzī noted east and west as a metonym of all directions, 2:177’s “it is not piety that you turn your faces toward the east and the west.” 2:115 and 2:177 are sisters: a facing is commanded; a facing is not the whole of *birr*.

This *ummah* is spoken to in airports, in hospitals, in prisons, in a sickbed that cannot stand a *qiblah* of the feet. *Aynamā tuwallū*. A doctor has turned your bed. There is the Face, as a *wāsiʿ* Knowing, while you keep 2:144 when you can. Do not make a vastness an excuse for a lazy *fard*. Do not make a *fard* a denial of a vastness.

Al-Ṭabarī prefers a meaning that gathers the reports without retiring the qiblah of 2:144. A vast Lord still commands a House. Ibn Kathīr warns the lazy traveler: *aynamā tuwallū* is not a license to skip a *fard*’s facing when the sun and a compass and a mosque’s wall can teach you. It is a license of a dark, a mount’s *nāfilah*, a past command, a *janāzah* of a hidden body. 2:177 will soon say piety is not the turning itself. 2:115 says the turning, when it is His command or His excuse, finds a Face. *Wāsiʿ ʿalīm* is a pair for a man who feared his prayer was lost in a night: He is wide enough to receive it, Knowing enough not to mix it with a whim.

For the reader the verse is a map that cannot be confiscated. East and west are His. Turn. The Face is there. He is wide, Knowing. The next verse will shut a claim that would make the Owner of east and west a father: they say Allah has taken a son. Glory be to Him! Rather, to Him belongs what is in the heavens and the earth; all are devoutly obedient to Him.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:116

> They say, “Allah has offspring.” Glory be to Him! In fact, to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth—all are subject to His Will.

**Commentary:**

The Owner of east and west is cleared of a household of gods: *wa qālū ittakhadha Allāhu waladan subḥānahu bal lahu mā fī al-samāwāti wa al-arḍi kullun lahu qānitūn*, “And they say, ‘Allah has taken a son.’ Glory be to Him! Rather, to Him belongs what is in the heavens and the earth. All are devoutly obedient to Him.” *Ittakhadha* is He has taken — a verb of adoption and of manufacture, as if a son were a policy. *Walad* is a child, a son, an offspring. The *qālū* is a they of several mouths, and the verse takes them in one net. Some of the Jews said ʿUzayr is the son of Allah, 9:30. The Christians said the Messiah is the son of Allah, 9:30. The pagans of Quraysh said the angels were daughters of Allah, 16:57, 37:149–153, 53:21–27. Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, Qatādah, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, named these camps. 2:116 does not need a footnote of a single *sabab*. It needs *subḥānahu*.

*Subḥānahu*, glory be to Him, far removed is He. *Tasbīḥ* is the Fātiḥah’s praise in another root: a distancing of the Lord from what does not befit. A son is a need, a continuation, a partner in a species, a claimant of inheritance. “He begets not, nor is He begotten” (112:3). “It is not for Allah to take a son. Glory be to Him. When He decrees a matter, He only says to it ‘Be,’ and it is” (19:35) — 2:117 will say that *Be*. *Tasbīḥ* here is a creed in one word. The Prophet (ﷺ) said that Allah (الله) said, “The son of Adam has denied Me, and it was not for him to deny Me; and he has insulted Me, and it was not for him to insult Me. As for his denial, his saying I will not be raised as I was begun; as for his insult, his saying I have a son. I am One, the Eternal. I beget not, nor am I begotten, and there is none like Me” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, the ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah). 2:116 is that insult named, and that distancing commanded.

*Bal lahu mā fī al-samāwāti wa al-arḍ*, rather, His is what is in the heavens and the earth. *Bal* is a turning: not a son; a possession of all. A father among men possesses a child as a kind of himself. This Lord possesses the two worlds as *mulk*, 2:107. A son would be a rival in the *mulk* or a hole in the *ṣamadiyyah*. 4:171 to the People of the Book: “Do not say ‘Three.’ Cease — it is better for you. Allah is only One God. Glory be to Him that He should have a son. His is what is in the heavens and what is in the earth.” 2:116 is 4:171 in a Madinan brevity.

*Kullun lahu qānitūn*, all are to Him devoutly obedient. *Qunūt* is obedience, stillness in prayer, a standing that does not fidget. 2:238 will command *qānitīn* in the middle prayer. 3:17, 33:35. Here the *kull* is every creature of the two worlds: angels, mountains, a Messiah in his true servant-hood, ʿUzayr in his true servant-hood, the angels the pagans named daughters. 19:93: “There is none in the heavens and the earth but comes to the All-Merciful as a servant.” 2:116’s *qānitūn* is 19:93’s ʿabd. The Messiah said, in what Allah (الله) taught, “I am the servant of Allah” (19:30). A servant is not a son. Ibn Kathīr treats *qānitūn* as a proof: if all are obedient, a “son” is either inside the all — then he is a servant — or outside the all — then he is a second god, and 2:22’s *nidd* has returned.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a theologian of law, hears the verse as the *aqīdah* that a *nikāḥ*, a birth, a need of a helper, are of the creation. The Creator is *al-Ṣamad*, the One to whom need is directed, who does not need. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *walad* implies a species shared, and a species shared implies a likeness, and “there is nothing like Him” (42:11). Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that the most unjust sentence of a tongue is 2:116’s *ittakhadha*, because it takes from the Lord what 112:3 denied and gives to a servant what 19:93 denied.

This *ummah* is tried by softer *walad*: a language of “God has no choice but to love as a father,” a piety that cannot praise except in a borrowed Trinity, a philosophy that makes the world an emanation of a son. *Subḥānahu*. His is what is in the two worlds. All are *qānitūn*. The Prophet (ﷺ) taught a man his Lord in 112, and said it is equal to a third of the Qur'an (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:116 is that third as a rebuke of a sentence.

Al-Ṭabarī reads *qānitūn* as obedient, and some of the *salaf* as standing in a *qunūt* of creatures that do not claim a lineage. 30:26: to Him belongs whoever is in the heavens and the earth; all are devoutly obedient. 2:116 is that verse’s twin. Ibn Kathīr treats *subḥānahu* as a word the servant should increase when he hears 9:30. The Prophet (ﷺ) taught *subḥān Allāh* as a filling of the scales. 2:116 is that filling aimed at a specific filth of a sentence. This *ummah* must not borrow a “Son” as a metaphor of love. Love is 2:165’s *ḥubb* of a Lord, 3:31’s following of a Prophet (ﷺ). It is not a begetting.

For the reader the verse is a washing of the mouth. Do not say a son. Say Glory. Say possession. Say the obedience of all. The next verse will explain how He needs no son to act: He is the Originator of the heavens and the earth; when He decrees a matter, He only says to it “Be,” and it is.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:117

> ˹He is˺ the Originator of the heavens and the earth! When He decrees a matter, He simply tells it, “Be!” And it is!

**Commentary:**

The denial of a son is given its creative grammar: *badīʿu al-samāwāti wa al-arḍi wa idhā qaḍā amran fa-innamā yaqūlu lahu kun fa-yakūn*, “The Originator of the heavens and the earth. When He decrees a matter, He only says to it ‘Be,’ and it is.” *Badīʿ* is the Originator, the one who makes a thing with no prior model, from *b-d-ʿ*, to begin in a way not seen before. 6:101 repeats the Name with the same argument: “The *Badīʿ* of the heavens and the earth — how should He have a child when He has no companion, and He created all things?” 2:117 is that how as a *kun*. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, read *badīʿ* as the Creator of the two worlds without a precedent. A father among men is not *badīʿ* of his son; he copies a species. This Lord originated the species.

*Wa idhā qaḍā amran*, and when He decrees a matter. *Qaḍā* is a completing, a judging, a willing that has gone forth. *Amr* is a matter, a command, 2:109’s *amr* that the believers wait for, here the *amr* of a creating. *Fa-innamā yaqūlu lahu kun fa-yakūn*, He only says to it “Be,” so it is. *Innamā* is a restriction: not a tool, not a spouse, not a son as an instrument, not a tiredness of 50:38. A word. *Kun* is Be. *Fa-yakūn* is and it is. 3:47, 3:59, 6:73, 16:40, 19:35, 36:82, 40:68 — the refrain of a speech that does not lag. 3:59: “The likeness of ʿĪsā with Allah is as the likeness of Ādam. He created him from dust, then said to him ‘Be,’ and he was.” 2:117 is that *kun* under 2:116’s *subḥān*. If Ādam is a *kun*, ʿĪsā is a *kun*. A *kun* is not a son.

Ahl al-Sunnah hold that Allah (الله) speaks with a speech that is His attribute, not a created letter in a throat, and that *kun* in the Qur'an is an expression of a will that does not fail. They do not make *kun* a partner. They do not make it a magic of 2:102. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that the Lord’s *kun* is not like our *kun*; our *kun* is a wish, His is a bringing. Al-Rāzī, in the tools of his *kalām*, still lands on a power that needs no helper. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *fa-yakūn* is in the imperfect: it is, it comes to be — a being that follows a saying without a gap of weakness.

The Prophet (ﷺ) taught this origin in the opening of revelation’s law: “Allah existed and there was nothing other than Him” in the meanings of the ḥadīth of ʿImrān ibn Ḥuṣayn (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). He taught *istighfār* of a tiredness the Jews ascribed, when they said He rested, and 50:38 denied a weariness. He taught that hearts are between two fingers of the All-Merciful; He turns them as He wills (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A turning of a heart is a *kun* of a guidance. 2:105’s *yakhtaṣṣu bi-raḥmatihi* is a *kun* of a mercy. 2:117 is the grammar of every gift they did not love.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd al-khalq* and *tawḥīd al-kalām* together. He originates. He speaks. The originated does not counsel Him. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that a servant who has understood *kun fa-yakūn* will not ask with 2:108’s bargaining, because a sign is not extracted from a Lord as a son extracts a gift from a father; a sign is a *kun* He may say or not. 36:82: “His command, when He intends a thing, is only that He says to it ‘Be,’ and it is.” 2:117 and 36:82 are one *aqīdah*.

This *ummah* is spoken to in an age that cannot imagine a beginning, that makes a universe a fatherless father of itself, or that keeps a son in a creed to make God “personal.” 2:117 is more personal than a son: a speech to a matter. *Kun*. He is nearer to a servant than a jugular (50:16), and He is *badīʿ* of what the servant cannot model. Do not reduce Him to a cause among causes. Do not empty Him of a will.

Ibn Kathīr gathers that some of the *salaf* paused on *badīʿ* as a Name by which a servant asks. A Name of originating is a Name of hope for a man whose story feels copied and trapped. He who originated the two worlds can originate a relief. 2:186’s nearness is that hope’s door.

A Lord who originated without a model does not then hire a child as a tool. Ādam was a *kun* from dust; ʿĪsā was a *kun* in a womb; both are servants, neither is a son. The Originator needs no instrument of a household. Al-Ṭabarī notes that *badīʿ* is a creating without a sample, so that a son — who is always a sample of a father — is grammatically impossible as a need. Ibn Kathīr ties 2:117 to 3:59 so that a Christian ear will hear Ādam and ʿĪsā in one *kun*. This *ummah* is also spoken to: a man who thinks his *tawbah* needs a mediator of a begotten kind has not heard *kun*. 39:53 is a *kun* of a mercy for the despairing. 2:186 is a *kun* of an answer for the asker. 2:117 is the grammar of both. Do not wait for a son. Wait for a saying.

For the reader the verse is a short creed after a slander. He originated. He decrees. He says Be. It is. No son is required. The next verse will return to the mouths that still want a custom sign: those who do not know say, if only Allah would speak to us, or a sign would come. Those before them said the like. Hearts have resembled. We have made the signs clear for a people who are certain.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:118

> Those who have no knowledge say, “If only Allah would speak to us or a sign would come to us!” The same was said by those who came before. Their hearts are all alike. Indeed, We have made the signs clear for people of sure faith.

**Commentary:**

The Lord who says *kun* is asked, by the unknowing, to speak on their terms: *wa qāla alladhīna lā yaʿlamūna lawlā yukallimunā Allāhu aw taʾtīnā āyatun kadhālika qāla alladhīna min qablihim mithla qawlihim tashābahat qulūbuhum qad bayyannā al-āyāti li-qawmin yūqinūn*, “And those who do not know said, ‘If only Allah would speak to us, or a sign would come to us.’ Likewise those before them said the like of their saying. Their hearts have resembled. We have made the signs clear for a people who are certain.” *Alladhīna lā yaʿlamūn* is 2:113’s phrase, the pagans in Ibn ʿAbbās’s reading, and in other readings a party of the People of the Book who demanded a speech or a custom miracle as a condition. Ibn al-Jawzī gathered: Rāfiʿ saying let Allah speak to us; the pagans asking a sign; the resemblance of a demand. 2:108 forbade asking as Mūsā was asked. 2:118 names the asking as a sentence of those who do not know.

*Lawlā yukallimunā Allāh*, if only Allah would speak to us. He has spoken. The Qur'an is His speech, uncreated in the creed of Ahl al-Sunnah as an attribute, recited in created sounds. 2:75 said a party of them heard the speech of Allah then distorted. The demand is not for speech. It is for a speech that will not bind, a private theatre. *Aw taʾtīnā āyah*, or a sign would come to us. Signs have come: a Book, a split moon, a night journey, a camel of Ṣāliḥ in the memory of ʿAdnān, a staff of Mūsā in the memory of Israel. 6:109: even if every sign came they would not believe unless Allah willed. 2:6 has already closed a kind of chest. 2:118 is that chest speaking.

*Kadhālika qāla alladhīna min qablihim mithla qawlihim*, likewise those before them said the like of their saying. 2:113 used *kadhālika qāla* for a pagan echoing a reciter. Here the echoing is backward: this generation’s demand is last year’s demand, last *ummah*’s demand. 4:153, 17:90–93, 20:133. *Tashābahat qulūbuhum*, their hearts have resembled. Not their clothes, not their century — their hearts. 2:70’s *tashābaha ʿalaynā* was a cow that looked like another cow; 2:118’s *tashābahat qulūbuhum* is a heart that looks like a dead heart of a former people. Ibn Kathīr treats the clause as a *taṣliyah* of the Prophet (ﷺ): you have not failed a unique exam; you have met a repeated heart. Al-Qurṭubī hears a warning to this *ummah*: a heart can resemble a heart you recite as history.

*Qad bayyannā al-āyāti li-qawmin yūqinūn*, We have already made the signs clear for a people who are certain. *Bayyannā* is 2:109’s *tabayyana* from the other side: He has clarified. *Yūqinūn* is they have *yaqīn*, a certainty, 2:4’s *yūqinūn* of the Hereafter. Signs are clear for a people who have that certainty as a capacity. A people who have demanded a sign as a bargain will call a clear sign a *siḥr*, 54:2. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “There is no prophet except that he was given of signs what the like of which people believed, and what I was given is a revelation Allah sent to me, so I hope to be the most followed on the Day of Rising” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). His greatest *āyah* is this *bayān*. 2:118 says it is enough for *mūqinūn*.

Theologically the verse is the justice of *hidāyah*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that signs do not create *yaqīn* in a heart that has chosen resemblance to the old deniers; they increase it in a heart that has 2:2’s *taqwā*. Al-Rāzī noted *lā yaʿlamūn* as a knowledge they could have had: the Book is in their language, the Messenger (ﷺ) is among them. Ignorance here is 2:101’s as-if. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *taklīm* of Allah (الله) is of kinds: a speech to a messenger, a speech of a Book recited, a speech of a dream of a prophet, and the demand of 2:118 is a fourth that He has not owed to every chest — a direct address as a condition of *īmān*. Mūsā was spoken to, and a party still said 2:55.

This *ummah* is tried by a laboratory *lawlā*: if only a verse would match a journal, if only a *ṣalāh* would be proven in a scan, if only a voice from the sky. Hearts resemble. The signs are clear for *mūqinūn*. Certainty is 2:4’s work: spend, pray, believe the unseen. It is not a prize for a clever demand.

A demand that ignores a Book already spoken is not a hunger for speech; it is a hunger for a speech that will not bind. Al-Ṭabarī reads *tashābahat qulūbuhum* as a resemblance of *kufr*, not of faces. 2:14’s hypocrites had a resemblance of speech; 2:118’s deniers have a resemblance of demand. Ibn Kathīr treats *qad bayyannā* as a past that has already done enough: the signs are not waiting for a committee. They are clear for *mūqinūn*. 2:4 named those who believe in the unseen and the Book and what was before and the Hereafter. 2:118 says the *bayān* is for them. A man who withholds *yaqīn* until a custom sign is 2:108’s trader. This *ummah* must not resemble a heart it recites as a warning.

For the reader the verse is a fast from *lawlā*. Do not wait for a private *taklīm*. Recite what He has spoken. Sit with signs already made clear. Ask for *yaqīn*, not for a theatre. The next verse will tell the Prophet (ﷺ) his job in front of those hearts: We have sent you with the truth as a bringer of good news and a warner, and you will not be asked about the companions of the Blaze.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:119

> We have surely sent you with the truth ˹O Prophet˺ as a deliverer of good news and a warner. And you will not be accountable for the residents of the Hellfire.

**Commentary:**

The resembling hearts are given a messenger whose job is not their conversion as a debt: *innā arsalnāka bi-al-ḥaqqi bashīran wa nadhīran wa lā tusʾalu ʿan aṣḥābi al-jaḥīm*, “Indeed We have sent you with the truth as a bringer of good news and a warner, and you will not be asked about the companions of the Blaze.” *Innā arsalnāka*, We have sent you — the *innā* of a sure sending, 2:119 as a *taṣliyah* after 2:118’s demand. *Bi-al-ḥaqq*, with the truth: not with a wish of 2:111, not with a *siḥr* of 2:102, not with a son of 2:116. The truth is the Book, the *dīn*, the news of a Garden and a Fire. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, read *bashīran* as a bringer of good news to the believers of 2:25, 2:82, 2:112, and *nadhīran* as a warner to 2:6, 2:24, 2:39, 2:81. The two names are 33:45, 34:28, 35:24, the job-description of every messenger. 2:119 gives them to this Prophet (ﷺ) after a verse of hearts that will not hear.

*Wa lā tusʾalu ʿan aṣḥābi al-jaḥīm*, and you will not be asked about the companions of the Blaze. *Jaḥīm* is a name of the Fire, a blaze, 2:119’s hard word after 2:24’s fuel. *Aṣḥāb* is companions, people of a place, 2:39’s *aṣḥāb al-nār*. He (ﷺ) will not be asked as a man is asked about a trust he failed. The sending was with truth, good news, warning. The entry of a chest is 2:7’s seal or 2:2’s *taqwā*, by a will of the Lord. 2:272: “You are not responsible for their guidance, but Allah guides whom He wills.” 28:56: “You do not guide whom you love, but Allah guides whom He wills.” 2:119 is that mercy upon a prophet who, 18:6, would nearly kill himself with grief if they did not believe. Ibn Kathīr treats *lā tusʾalu* as a lifting of a burden. Some read it as a prohibition in another vocalization, *lā tasʾal*: do not ask about them. The reading of the *muṣḥaf* in our hands is *tusʾalu*, you will not be asked. Both meanings kiss a grief: do not carry what is not yours; you will not be billed for it.

A report that the verse came because the Prophet (ﷺ) asked about his parents, or about the Jews’ punishment, is among what Ibn Ḥajar and others treated as not standing as a sound *sabab*. The verse’s face does not need it. A warner is not billed for a blaze he warned of. Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of *daʿwah*, hears a rule: convey, then sleep the sleep of a man who has paid. The ḥadīth of the *balāgh* is 5:67: convey what has been sent down. 3:20: if they turn away, upon you is only the conveying.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “My likeness and yours is a man who lit a fire, and moths and locusts began to fall into it, and he swats them away, and I am holding you back from the Fire, saying, Come away from the Fire, and you slip from my hands” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the meanings of the reports). 2:119 is that holding, and that not-asked. He will still have a *ḥawḍ*, a basin; he will still intercede by a leave; he will still say *ummatī ummatī*. The not-asked is not a coldness. It is a justice: a blaze is not his invoice.

Theologically the verse is the limit of a servant, even a messenger. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *nubuwwah* is a *balāgh*, not a creating of *hidāyah*. Al-Rāzī noted *bi-al-ḥaqq* as the content that makes good news true and warning true. A false good news is 2:80’s numbered days. A false warning is a priest’s invention. This sending is with *ḥaqq*. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that love of the Prophet (ﷺ) is not a claim that he owns a chest’s seal. It is a following of a *bashīr* and *nadhīr*.

This *ummah*’s callers are spoken to. You are not sent as he was sent, but you inherit a *balāgh*. 2:119’s *lā tusʾalu* is a medicine against a despair that 2:6 already named, and against a pride that counts conversions as a private *mulk*. Convey. He sees 2:110. He guides whom He wills.

A prophet’s grief is worship; a prophet’s invoice for a blaze would be a *ẓulm* the verse lifts. Convey, and leave the seal of 2:7 to the Sealer who sees. Your job is the *balāgh*; His job is that chest and its appointed seal. Al-Ṭabarī reads *bashīran wa nadhīran* as the two faces of one *ḥaqq*: a Garden that is not 2:111’s wish, a Fire that is not a priest’s invention. Ibn Kathīr treats *lā tusʾalu* as a kindness after 18:6’s grief. The Prophet (ﷺ) would still weep for his *ummah*. The verse does not forbid a tear. It forbids an invoice. 88:21–22: so remind; you are only a reminder; you are not a controller over them. 2:119 is that *sūrah* in a Madinan clause. This *ummah*’s teachers inherit a reminder, not a control, not a billing for a blaze. Convey 2:110’s work. Leave 2:7’s seal to the Sealer.

For the reader the verse is a job and a mercy. He was sent to you with truth, good news, warning. Take the news. Take the warning. Do not make his grief your excuse to stay with the blaze’s people. He will not be asked about them. You will be asked about you. The next verse will shut a last diplomacy: the Jews and the Christians will never be pleased with you until you follow their creed. Say: the guidance of Allah is the guidance. If you follow their desires after the knowledge that has come to you, you will have no guardian and no helper against Allah (الله).

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:120

> Never will the Jews or Christians be pleased with you, until you follow their faith. Say, “Allah’s guidance is the only ˹true˺ guidance.” And if you were to follow their desires after ˹all˺ the knowledge that has come to you, there would be none to protect or help you against Allah.

**Commentary:**

The sending with truth is now guarded from a diplomacy of pleasure: *wa lan tarḍā ʿanka al-yahūdu wa lā al-naṣārā ḥattā tattabiʿa millatahum qul inna hudā Allāhi huwa al-hudā wa la-in ittabaʿta ahwāʾahum baʿda alladhī jāʾaka min al-ʿilmi mā laka min Allāhi min waliyyin wa lā naṣīr*, “And never will the Jews be pleased with you, nor the Christians, until you follow their creed. Say: indeed the guidance of Allah — that is the guidance. And if you were to follow their desires after what has come to you of knowledge, you would have against Allah no guardian and no helper.” *Lan tarḍā* is a *lan* of a never in this bargain. *ʿAnka*, with you, about you — the Prophet (ﷺ) first, and his *ummah* in the following of a law. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, and the mufassirūn, named *asbāb*: a hope that a qiblah toward Bayt al-Maqdis would please; a hope of a truce of creeds; a call to their *millah* as 2:135 will name it. 2:120 says pleasure is not priced at a qiblah. It is priced at a following. *Ḥattā tattabiʿa millatahum*, until you follow their *millah*. *Millah* is a religion as a path, 2:130, 2:135’s *millat Ibrāhīm*. They do not ask a compromise. They ask a following. Two *millahs* that 2:113 said of each other “upon nothing” agree in 2:120 that this Messenger (ﷺ) should follow. The agreement is not a unity of truth. It is a unity of a demand.

*Qul inna hudā Allāhi huwa al-hudā*, say: indeed the guidance of Allah — that is the guidance. *Huwa al-hudā* is a restriction: the guidance is His, not theirs, not a blend. 2:2’s Book is *hudan*. 1:6’s path is a asked guidance. 2:120 makes the answer to a *millah*’s pressure a sentence of *tawḥīd*. Ibn Kathīr treats *qul* as a command to refuse a smile that costs a *dīn*. Al-Qurṭubī hears a *fiqh* of *muwālāh*: a kindness of 60:8 toward those who do not fight you for religion is not 2:120’s *ittibāʿ*. Pleasure of a creed is not a human right upon a prophet.

*Wa la-in ittabaʿta ahwāʾahum baʿda alladhī jāʾaka min al-ʿilm*, and if you followed their desires after what has come to you of knowledge. The address is to the Prophet (ﷺ) as the strongest warning, so that every lesser chest will shake. He (ﷺ) would not follow. The *ummah* might. *Ahwāʾ* is desires, 2:78’s *amānī* in another root, 2:87’s *ahwāʾ* of a proud party. Their *millah*, when it contradicts what has come of knowledge, is named *hawā*. *Baʿda alladhī jāʾaka min al-ʿilm*, after the knowledge — a knowledge that has arrived, 2:109’s *tabayyana*, 2:89’s recognition. Following a desire after knowledge is 2:108’s exchange of *kufr* for *īmān* in a diplomatic coat.

*Mā laka min Allāhi min waliyyin wa lā naṣīr*, you would not have against Allah a guardian nor a helper. 2:107’s fact becomes 2:120’s threat. The pair *wali* and *naṣīr* emptied besides Him is now emptied against Him: no patron will stand between you and Him if you sell a knowledge for a pleasure. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that this close is among the most frightening in the sūrah, because it is said to the most beloved of creation (ﷺ) as a law of a universe: knowledge arrived; desire invited; a following would leave you without a *wali*. Al-Rāzī noted that a prophet is taught his own need of Allah (الله) in the same breath as he is taught not to need a *millah*’s smile.

The Prophet (ﷺ) lived 2:120 as a spine. He refused Quraysh’s kings and women and gold for a word. He refused a peeling of ʿUzzā. He said, “If they placed the sun in my right hand and the moon in my left on condition that I leave this matter, I would not leave it until Allah made it prevail or I perished therein” (the well-known report of Ibn Isḥāq, with the meaning the *sīrah* has carried). He wrote to Heraclius and to Kisra a *daʿwah*, not a request for a creed’s pleasure. He said, “Whoever seeks to please Allah by displeasing people, Allah is pleased with him and makes people pleased with him; and whoever seeks to please people by displeasing Allah, Allah is displeased with him and makes people displeased with him” (al-Tirmidhī, Ibn Ḥibbān, in the well-known meaning). 2:120 is that seeking as a verse.

This *ummah* is tried by a thousand *lan tarḍā*. A workplace will not be pleased until a *ṣalāh* is hidden. A media will not be pleased until a verse is retired. A relative of another *millah* will not be pleased until a child is raised in a blend. 2:120 does not forbid 31:15’s kindness to a mushrik parent. It forbids *ittibāʿ al-millah*. Guidance is His guidance. After knowledge, a following of *hawā* is a file with no *wali* and no *naṣīr*.

Theologically the verse is *al-walāʾ wa al-barāʾ* in a precise dose: not a hate of persons 2:109 told you to pardon until a command; a refusal of a *millah* as a price of a smile. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that 2:120 is the verse that kills the religion of pleasing as a *manhaj*. A caller who trims 2:116 to keep a Christian friend, who trims 2:111 to keep a Jewish neighbor, who trims 2:102 to keep a market of charms, has begun the *la-in ittabaʿta*. The knowledge has come. The empty file of 2:107 waits.

For the reader the verse is a last wall of this twenty. They will not be pleased until you follow. Do not follow. Say the guidance of Allah is the guidance. Keep 2:110’s prayer and *zakāh*, 2:112’s submitted face, 2:115’s vastness, 2:117’s *kun*, 2:119’s good news and warning. If you sell after knowledge, no helper. The next verses will praise those who are given the Book and recite it with its true recitation; they believe in it. And whoever covers — those are the losers. 2:121 will open the reciters who did not fling.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:121

> Those We have given the Book follow it as it should be followed. It is they who ˹truly˺ believe in it. As for those who reject it, it is they who are the losers.

**Commentary:**

The flung back of 2:101 is answered with a recitation that keeps the Book in front of the face: *alladhīna ātaynāhumu al-kitāba yatlūnahu ḥaqqa tilāwatihi ulāʾika yuʾminūna bihi wa man yakfur bihi fa-ulāʾika humu al-khāsirūn*, “Those to whom We have given the Book recite it with the right of its recitation. Those believe in it. And whoever covers it — those are the losers.” *Ātaynāhum* is We gave them — a giving, not a finding in a market. The Book is a trust placed in a chest. *Yatlūnahu* is they recite it, they follow it: the root *t-l-w* is both a reading of letters and a walking behind a leader. 2:102’s satans *tatlū* a recitation of magic; 2:121’s people *yatlūna* a recitation of a Book. Two recitations, two imams. *Ḥaqqa tilāwatihi* is the right of its recitation, the due, the *ḥaqq* that a thing is owed. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, said it is they follow it as it should be followed: they make lawful its lawful, they forbid its forbidden, they do not distort a word from its place, they do not recast a *ḥukm* as a wish. ʿUmar (may Allah be pleased with him) said, in the report of Saʿīd ibn Manṣūr, “Do not be deceived by one who recites the Qur'an; it is speech that is spoken. Look to the one who acts upon it.” 2:121 is that looking, written as a verse.

The *salaf* differed on *al-kitāb* here, and both readings live without a war. Some said it is the Torah and the Injīl in the hands of those who did not fling — ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām and those with him, a party of Najrān who heard and followed — who recited their Book with its due and found in it this Prophet (ﷺ) as 7:157 named him, and then believed. Al-Biqāʿī, as later *naẓm* made explicit, read the verse as a contrast with 2:113’s reciters who empty one another while they recite. Recitation without following is 2:44. Recitation with its *ḥaqq* is 2:121. Others said the Book is the Qur'an, and those given it are the Companions who took it as 2:63 took a mountain, with *quwwah*. Both meanings kiss: a due recitation of a former Book leads to this Book; a due recitation of this Book is *īmān* in it. Al-Qurṭubī notes that *ulāʾika yuʾminūna bihi* is a restriction: true *īmān* in a Book is this recitation, not a heritage-name, not 2:78’s wishes, not 2:111’s slogan of a Garden.

*Wa man yakfur bihi fa-ulāʾika humu al-khāsirūn*, and whoever covers it, those are the losers. *Kafara* is to cover what has been given. A Book given and then covered is 2:89’s recognition turned to *kufr*, 2:101’s fling, 2:87’s proud *ahwāʾ*. *Al-khāsirūn* is 2:27’s losers, 2:121’s last word after 2:120’s empty file of helpers. Loss is not a small miscount. It is a capital spent on a flung back. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The Qur'an is a proof for you or against you” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “The example of a believer who recites the Qur'an is a citron, its scent and taste good; the believer who does not recite is a date, its taste good and no scent; the hypocrite who recites is a sweet basil, scent good and taste bitter; the hypocrite who does not recite is a colocynth, bitter and without scent” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:121’s *ḥaqq tilāwah* is the citron, not the basil. He said, “The one who is skilled in the Qur'an is with the noble, righteous scribes; and the one who recites and it is hard for him, and he stammers, has two rewards” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). Skill of letters is a mercy. Skill without *ḥaqq* of following is 62:5’s donkey of books.

Theologically the verse is the ethics of a given speech. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *īmān* in a Book is not a claim of a library; it is a *tilāwah* that binds the limb. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that every *āyah* has a right: a knowledge, a state, a deed; a people who take the knowledge and leave the deed have recited a half-*ḥaqq*. Al-Rāzī noted *ātaynāhum* as a reminder of a gift so that *kufr* will be named as ingratitude, not as a philosophy. This *ummah* is spoken to with a harder *ḥaqq* because its Book is preserved (15:9). A preserved Book flung is a worse *nabdh* than a distorted Book flung. 25:30: the Messenger will say, “O my Lord, my people took this Qur'an as a thing abandoned.” 2:121 is the opposite people: they recite it with its due.

A due recitation is also a *tajwīd* of a life: 73:4 said recites the Qur'an with *tartīl*, and the *salaf* did not make *tartīl* a melody only. Ibn Masʿūd (may Allah be pleased with him) said, “Do not scatter it as *nashr* of sand, and do not chant it as a chant of poetry; stop at its wonders, move the hearts with it.” 2:121 is that stopping. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *ḥaqqa tilāwatihi* as a *maṣdar* of a right, not a decoration of a voice. A beautiful *qirāʾah* that sells a *ḥukm* is not this verse. A plain *qirāʾah* that stands a prayer and a *zakāh* of 2:110 is this verse. 35:29–30 praised those who recite the Book of Allah, establish the prayer, and spend; they hope a trade that will not fail. 2:121 is that trade named as *īmān*.

For the reader the verse is a due you owe a *muṣḥaf*. Do you follow it, or only voice it? Those who cover it lose. The next two verses will turn again to Israel with the same two calls of 2:47–48 — remember the favor, fear the Day — so that a people given a Book will not imagine that a genealogy can replace a *tilāwah*. 2:122 will open that return.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:122

> O Children of Israel! Remember My favours upon you and how I honoured you above the others.

**Commentary:**

The reciters of a due Book are followed by a call that this sūrah has already spoken, almost word for word: *yā Banī Isrāʾīla udhkurū niʿmatiya allatī anʿamtu ʿalaykum wa annī faḍḍaltukum ʿalā al-ʿālamīn*, “O Children of Israel, remember My favor which I bestowed upon you, and that I preferred you over the worlds.” 2:47 said it with a small difference of Khattab’s English; 2:122 says it again after eighty verses of a dossier. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī treat the return as a *taʾkīd*, an emphasis: a people who have heard the calf, the mountain, the distortion, the fling, the magic, the envy, the slogan of a Garden, are called once more by the name of a favor, not by the name of a curse.
