The Lord still says *niʿmatī*, My favor. He still says *faḍḍaltukum*, I preferred you. The door of 2:62, 2:112 is not shut by a repetition. It is held open by one.

*Udhkurū* is remember, mention, keep in a chest that can still thank. *Dhikr* of a favor is 2:40’s first command to this people in the sūrah, and 2:47’s, and now 2:122’s. Three rememberings, one disease: a favor treated as a property. *Niʿmatī allatī anʿamtu ʿalaykum* is the favor which I bestowed upon you — ascription upon ascription. The rescue from Firʿawn, the sea, the cloud, the manna, the prophets from among them, the Book — 2:49–61 named the file. 5:20: He made you kings and gave you what He had not given anyone in the worlds. *Wa annī faḍḍaltukum ʿalā al-ʿālamīn*, and that I preferred you over the worlds. The *salaf* read *al-ʿālamīn* as the worlds of their hour, not as a forever-lease against Muḥammad’s (ﷺ) *ummah*. 3:110 will say of this *ummah*, “You are the best *ummah* brought forth for people,” when they command good and forbid evil and believe. Preference is a trust. 2:124 will soon say that Ibrāhīm’s covenant is not for the wrongdoers. A preferred people who wrong have not inherited a throne. They have inherited a case.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of *niʿmah*, hears the verse as a law of gratitude: a favor remembered is a favor that binds; a favor forgotten is 14:7’s opposite of *shukr*. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “He who does not thank people does not thank Allah” (Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī). He said, “Look at those below you, and do not look at those above you, for it is more likely that you will not despise Allah’s favor upon you” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:122 is that looking aimed at a people who had been above many and then treated the above as a blood-right. He recited that Allah (الله) said, “I divided the prayer between Myself and My servant” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim): even a *Fātiḥah* is a favor to be remembered, not a slogan.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd al-munʿim*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that a people who remember a prophet and forget the Giver have made the prophet a tribe-god; a people who remember a Book and forget the Speaker have made the Book a relic. 2:122 names the favors as *My* favors. Al-Rāzī noted the placement: after 2:120’s “they will never be pleased until you follow,” and 2:121’s reciters who believe, Israel is not discarded as a file. They are called. A call after a dossier is a mercy that 2:64 already named: were it not for *faḍl* and *raḥmah*. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *tafdīl* is by *īmān* and *ʿamal*, and that a later *ummah* that recites 2:122 as a taunt upon Jews and does not hear it upon itself has not understood why Allah (الله) repeats a verse. This *ummah* was preferred with a preserved Book. If it flings, 2:101 is its mirror.

A favor remembered after a dossier is heavier than a favor remembered in an opening. 2:40 asked a fulfilling of a covenant; 2:47 asked a remembering; 2:122 asks the remembering again because a people can hear a history and still treat a *niʿmah* as a blood-right. The Prophet (ﷺ) taught *al-ḥamdu lillāh* as a filling of a scale; 2:122 is that *ḥamd* as a national *dhikr*. The return of 2:47 at 2:122 is also a hinge. What follows is no longer only Israel’s calf. It is Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him), the House, a *millah* that Jews and Christians and pagans all claim, and a qiblah that will turn. The sūrah is about to take the father both camps name and ask who his children are. A people told to remember a favor are being prepared to remember a father who was not a Jew and not a Christian (3:67). Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *yā Banī Isrāʾīl* as a naming that still honors Yaʿqūb (peace be upon him). The name is not a slur. It is a summons.

This *ummah* is spoken to whenever it treats a golden age as a blood-right: we are the people of a *madhhab*, a land, a *sīrah* we do not live. *Udhkurū niʿmatī*. Preference is 3:110’s work, not 2:111’s slogan. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever is not grateful for the small will not be grateful for the large” (the well-known meaning of the reports). 2:122 is that gratitude as a national address.

2:211 will ask Israel how many a clear sign they were given, and that whoever changes a favor of Allah after it has come, Allah is severe in penalty. 2:122 is the favor named before that severity, so that a remembering might still be a *shukr* and not a *tabdīl*.

For the reader the verse is a second remembering. The first, at 2:47, opened a history. This one opens a father. Remember the favor. You were preferred. Preference is not a Garden-lease. The next verse will repeat 2:48 with a changed order of ransom and intercession: guard yourselves against a Day when no soul helps another, no ransom is taken, no intercession accepted, no help given. A favor remembered is for that Day.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:123

> And guard yourselves against the Day when no soul will be of any help to another. No ransom will be taken, no intercession accepted, and no help will be given.

**Commentary:**

The favor is tied at once to a Day: *wa ittaqū yawman lā tajzī nafsun ʿan nafsin shayʾan wa lā yuqbalu minhā ʿadlun wa lā tanfaʿuhā shafāʿatun wa lā hum yunṣarūn*, “And guard yourselves against a Day when no soul will avail another in anything, nor will a ransom be accepted from it, nor will intercession benefit it, nor will they be helped.” 2:48 said the same Day with a different inner order: there, intercession first, then ransom, then help; here, ransom first, then intercession, then help. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, did not make the swap a contradiction. A sūrah that teaches a people twice may change the door it knocks. 2:48 spoke to a people who trusted a *shafāʿah* of fathers. 2:123 speaks, after 2:111’s exclusive Garden and 2:80’s numbered days, to a people who might trust a price. *ʿAdl* here is a ransom, an equivalent, a compensation brought as if a soul were a debt that gold could settle. 3:91: if they had the earth in gold to ransom themselves, it would not be accepted. 5:36. 2:123 shuts that market first.

*Ittaqū yawman*, guard yourselves against a Day — *taqwā* of a time, not only of a sin. The Day is 1:4’s *yawm al-dīn*, 2:4’s Hereafter, 2:8’s Last Day the hypocrites named and did not believe. *Lā tajzī nafsun ʿan nafsin shayʾan*, no soul avails another in anything. *Jazā* is to suffice, to stand in place. A father will not stand in a son’s place, 31:33; a child will not stand in a father’s, 80:34–37. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “O Fāṭimah daughter of Muḥammad, I cannot avail you from Allah anything” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said to his clan, “Buy yourselves from Allah; I cannot avail you from Allah anything.” 2:123 is that ḥadīth as a verse upon a people who said, “We are the children of prophets.” *Shayʾan* is in anything — a total emptying of a proxy.

*Wa lā yuqbalu minhā ʿadlun*, nor will a ransom be accepted from it. *Qubūl* is the acceptance 2:127 will beg for a House. Here it is refused to a price. *Wa lā tanfaʿuhā shafāʿatun*, nor will an intercession benefit it. 2:48 said no intercession will be accepted; 2:123 says it will not benefit. Ahl al-Sunnah hold that a *shafāʿah* by Allah’s leave is real — the Prophet’s (ﷺ) intercession for the people of the great standing, for the people of the grave sins among those who said *lā ilāha illā Allāh*, as the mass of the *aḥādīth* in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim establish — and that a *shafāʿah* claimed as a tribal right, without *tawḥīd*, without a leave, is 2:48 and 2:123’s shut door. 2:255: who is there that will intercede with Him except by His leave? The verse does not deny the *shafāʿah* of a leave. It denies the *shafāʿah* of a slogan. *Wa lā hum yunṣarūn*, nor will they be helped — 2:107’s empty file of *naṣīr*, 2:120’s threat, now a Day’s verdict.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of a Last Day, hears a killing of three contracts a man makes with fear: I will be bailed, I will pay, I will be spoken for. 2:123 tears all three. What remains is 2:110’s sending of *khayr*, 2:112’s submitted face, 2:121’s due recitation. Ibn Kathīr treats the repetition after 2:122 as a pairing: a favor in the world, a fear of a Day. A people who take the favor and postpone the Day have 2:8’s sentence. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The clever one is he who subjects his soul and works for what is after death, and the helpless one is he who follows his soul’s *hawā* and puts his hope in Allah” (al-Tirmidhī). 2:123 is that cleverness as a *taqwā* of a date.

Theologically the verse is *ʿadl* of a gathering. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that a Lord who is *ʿAdl* will not let a soul buy another soul’s crime, and will not refuse a *shafāʿah* He has permitted. The two are not at war. Al-Rāzī noted the four clauses as four emptied hopes of 2:80 and 2:111. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that this *ummah* is warned by the same Day: a *nisbah* to the Prophet (ﷺ) without a following is Israel’s *nisbah* in another coat. “Whoever obeys me enters the Garden, whoever disobeys has refused” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī).

2:254 will say a Day when there is no sale, no friendship, no intercession. 2:123 is that Day in Israel’s ear after a favor. A people preferred in a world are not preferred as a proxy in a gathering. They are preferred as a people who were given a Book to send *khayr* of 2:110 before the Day arrives. This *ummah* is tried by a soft ransom: a donation that is meant to stand in for a prayer, a tear at a grave that is meant to stand in for a *tawbah*, a love of *Ahl al-Bayt* that is meant to stand in for a *ṣalāh*. 2:123 does not forbid a donation, a tear, a love. It forbids them as a *jazāʾ* of a soul for a soul. Work. Send. Recite with a due. The Day will not take a substitute you did not live.

2:48 taught the Day at the opening of Israel’s address; 2:123 teaches it at the hinge toward Ibrāhīm. A sūrah that times a Day twice is a sūrah that knows a people will trust a father as a ransom. The next verses will honor a father and then forbid that trust.

For the reader the verse is a stripping of proxies. No soul, no ransom, no useful intercession of a slogan, no help. Guard the Day. The next verse will open the father both camps claim: Ibrāhīm was tested with words, he fulfilled them, he was made an imam for the people; he asked about his offspring; the covenant is not for the wrongdoers. Blood will not buy 2:123’s Day. A fulfilled test might make an imam.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:124

> ˹Remember˺ when Abraham was tested by his Lord with ˹certain˺ commandments, which he fulfilled. Allah said, “I will certainly make you into a role model for the people.” Abraham asked, “What about my offspring?” Allah replied, “My covenant is not extended to the wrongdoers.”

**Commentary:**

The Day that takes no proxy is followed by a man who was himself a test, then an imam: *wa idh ibtalā Ibrāhīma rabbuhu bi-kalimātin fa-atammahunna qāla innī jāʿiluka lil-nāsi imāman qāla wa min dhurriyyatī qāla lā yanālu ʿahdī al-ẓālimīn*, “And when his Lord tested Ibrāhīm with words, and he fulfilled them, He said, ‘I am making you an imam for the people.’ He said, ‘And from my offspring?’ He said, ‘My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers.’” *Ibtalā* is He tested, from a trial that reveals a metal. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The people most severely tried are the prophets, then the most like them, then the most like them” (al-Tirmidhī, Ibn Mājah, the ḥadīth of Saʿd). Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him) is that severity made a story. *Bi-kalimāt*, with words — commands, rites, sentences of a Lord that a servant completes. Ibn al-Jawzī gathered the *salaf*: Ibn ʿAbbās, from Ṭāwūs, named ten of *fiṭrah* — five in the head, five in the body: parting the hair, rinsing, sniffing water, trimming the moustache, the *siwāk*; nails, pubic shaving, armpit, *istinjāʾ*, circumcision. Another line from Ibn ʿAbbās named *manāsik*. Al-Ḥasan named the star, the sun, the moon, the *hijrah*, the fire, the son, the circumcision. Qatādah named the rites of ḥajj. The Qur'an does not freeze the list. It names a fulfillment: *fa-atammahunna*, he completed them. *Tamām* is a finishing without a remainder. 53:37: “and Ibrāhīm who fulfilled.” 2:124 is that fulfillment as the door of an *imāmah*.

*Innī jāʿiluka lil-nāsi imāman*, I am making you an imam for the people. *Imām* is a leader who is stood in front, a *qiblah* of a life, not only a *miḥrāb*. 16:120: Ibrāhīm was a *ummah*, devoutly obedient, ḥanīf, not of the associators. 2:124 makes that *ummah* an imam for *al-nās*, the people — not a tribe. Ibn Kathīr treats the honor as given after the test, not before it. Allah (الله) trains, then He entrusts. A man who wants the title without the *kalimāt* has not read the order. *Qāla wa min dhurriyyatī*, and from my offspring? The question is a father’s *raḥmah*, 14:40’s *duʿāʾ* for children who establish prayer, 14:35’s keep them from idols. It is not a demand of a throne. Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him) loved a *dhurriyyah* that would stand as he stood. The answer is a law: *lā yanālu ʿahdī al-ẓālimīn*, My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers. *ʿAhd* here, the *salaf* said, is the *imāmah*, the prophecy, the special *wilāyah* of a lead — not the general mercy of rain. *Al-ẓālimūn* are those who wrong by *shirk*, by blood, by a flung Book. 31:13: *shirk* is a tremendous *ẓulm*. A grandson who associates is not Ibrāhīm’s imam though he is Ibrāhīm’s blood.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of leadership, hears the verse as a killing of *imāmah* by genealogy. Quraysh claimed the House as heirs. Jews claimed prophecy as heirs. 2:124 says the covenant is a *ʿahd* of a Lord, and *ẓulm* breaks it. This *ummah*’s *khilāfah* is of the same metal: “The most honorable of you with Allah is the most mindful” (49:13). The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Hear and obey, even if an Abyssinian slave whose head is like a raisin is appointed over you, so long as he leads you by the Book of Allah” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, in the meanings). Blood is not the *ʿahd*. The Book is.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd* of a covenant. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *nubuwwah* and a just *imāmah* are not inherited as a house is inherited; they are placed where He knows (6:124). Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that Ibrāhīm’s question is a model of a father’s *duʿāʾ*, and the answer is a model of a father’s fear: do not assume a child is inside a *ʿahd* because he carries a name. 11:46: “O Nūḥ, he is not of your family; he is a deed not righteous.” 2:124 is 11:46 as a law of *imāmah*. Al-Rāzī noted *atammahunna* as the secret: the words were completed by a servant, then the making was spoken by a Lord. A servant does not appoint himself.

The Prophet (ﷺ) was the most complete fulfillment of this imam’s *millah*. 16:123: “Then We revealed to you: follow the *millah* of Ibrāhīm, ḥanīf.” 3:68: the nearest of people to Ibrāhīm are those who followed him, and this Prophet, and those who believe. 2:124 is the root of 2:135. A test, a fulfillment, an imam, a shut door upon *ẓālimūn*. This *ummah* is tried by a reverse reading: we are *dhurriyyah*, therefore we are imams. The verse says: you may be *dhurriyyah* and *ẓālimūn* together. Then the *ʿahd* does not reach.

37:103–107 will show the son laid down and a ram substituted: a *kalimah* fulfilled without a drop of a prophet’s blood, and a *dīn* of a slaughter that 22:34 made for every *ummah*. 2:124’s *atammahunna* includes that lying-down. A man who wants Ibrāhīm’s *imāmah* and refuses Ibrāhīm’s knife-hour has not completed the words. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *kalimāt* as whatever a Lord says to a servant as a trial. Your *kalimāt* are not Ibrāhīm’s fire. They may be a wage, a diagnosis, a child who does not pray, a qiblah you must keep when a workplace frowns. Fulfill. The imam-making is His. The covenant is His.

For the reader the verse is a curriculum before a title. Tests first. Fulfillment. Then, if He wills, a lead. Do not lease the *ʿahd* to a wrongdoer because he shares a grandfather. The next verse will name the House that this imam was given: a place of return and a sanctuary; take the standing-place of Ibrāhīm as a prayer-site; purify My House for those who circle, those who remain, those who bow and prostrate.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:125

> And ˹remember˺ when We made the Sacred House a centre and a sanctuary for the people ˹saying˺, “˹You may˺ take the standing-place of Abraham as a site of prayer.” And We entrusted Abraham and Ishmael to purify My House for those who circle it, who meditate in it, and who bow and prostrate themselves ˹in prayer˺.

**Commentary:**

The imam is given a House: *wa idh jaʿalnā al-bayta mathābatan lil-nāsi wa amnan wa ittakhidhū min maqāmi Ibrāhīma muṣallan wa ʿahidnā ilā Ibrāhīma wa Ismāʿīla an ṭahhirā baytiya lil-ṭāʾifīna wa al-ʿākifīna wa al-rukkaʿi al-sujūd*, “And when We made the House a place of return for the people and a sanctuary — ‘Take the standing-place of Ibrāhīm as a place of prayer’ — and We entrusted Ibrāhīm and Ismāʿīl to purify My House for those who circle, those who remain, and those who bow and prostrate.” *Al-bayt* is the House, definite, the Kaʿbah, 3:96’s first House set for people at Bakkah. *Jaʿalnā*, We made — a making of a function, not a making of a stone’s lordship. *Mathābah* is a place of return, from *th-w-b*, to come back: people leave and they come back, Ibn ʿAbbās said, they do not remain as residents of a tourism; they visit and their chests remain tied. *Wa amnan*, and a sanctuary. 3:97: whoever enters it is secure. 29:67: have they not seen that We made a Ḥaram secure while people are snatched around them? The security is a *sharʿ* and a *qadar*: a law of not hunting, not fighting a wrong inside without right, and a history of a town that stayed when others fell. 2:126 will ask for that security as a *duʿāʾ*. 2:125 states it as a making.

*Wa ittakhidhū min maqāmi Ibrāhīma muṣallan*, and take of the standing-place of Ibrāhīm a prayer-site. *Maqām* is a place of standing. Ibn al-Jawzī gathered: the whole Ḥaram, said Ibn ʿAbbās in one line; ʿArafāt, Muzdalifah, the *jamarāt*, said ʿAṭāʾ; the stone, said Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, and Ibn al-Jawzī preferred it. The stone is the one the Prophet (ﷺ) prayed behind after *ṭawāf*, and ʿUmar (may Allah be pleased with him) said, “My Lord agreed with me in three: I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, if only we took the *maqām* of Ibrāhīm as a place of prayer,’ and the verse came” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). The *ummah* prays two rakʿahs after *ṭawāf* with 109 and 112, facing the House, the *maqām* between them and it when they can. A standing of a prophet becomes a *muṣallā* of a people. That is 2:124’s *imām* as a geography.

*Wa ʿahidnā ilā Ibrāhīma wa Ismāʿīl*, and We entrusted to Ibrāhīm and Ismāʿīl. *ʿAhd* here is a command, a charge, 2:124’s covenant in another use: a charge to purify. *An ṭahhirā baytiya*, that you both purify My House. *Baytī*, My House — ascription of honor, as 2:114’s *masājid Allāh*. Purification is from idols, from filth, from *shirk* of a rite, al-Ḥasan said; Ibn Jurayj asked ʿAṭāʾ and heard: from the idols. The Prophet (ﷺ) on the day of the Fatḥ entered and broke what was in the House, and recited 17:81: “The truth has come, and falsehood has vanished” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:125’s *ṭahhirā* is that vanishing as a charge upon two prophets, then upon every caretaker after them.

*Lil-ṭāʾifīna wa al-ʿākifīna wa al-rukkaʿi al-sujūd*, for those who circle, those who remain in *iʿtikāf*, and the bowing, the prostrating. Three works of a House: a *ṭawāf* that is not for any other house on earth as a *ḥajj* rite; a remaining that 2:187 will time to the mosques in Ramaḍān; a *ṣalāh* of *rukūʿ* and *sujūd*. Al-Qurṭubī hears the order as a *fiqh*: the House is not a museum. It is for these three. A tourism that photographs and does not circle, remain, or bow has not used *mathābah*. Ibn Kathīr treats Ismāʿīl’s naming beside his father as a lifting of a son the People of the Book often drop. The House is Ismāʿīl’s as well. The Arabs of the Ḥaram are in the charge. The Prophet (ﷺ) is Ismāʿīl’s answer in 2:129.

Theologically the verse is the sanctity of a space and the *tawḥīd* of its Owner. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that a House ascribed to Allah (الله) is not worshipped; it is faced, circled, purified. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *mathābah* is a name of a mercy: a servant is allowed to return after a wandering. Al-Rāzī noted *amnan* as a sign that a *dīn* of fear is also a *dīn* of a sanctuary. This *ummah* is tried by a *kharāb* of 2:114 in reverse: a House open and a rite empty, a *maqām* photographed and not prayed behind, a *ṭawāf* as a crowd without a *dhikr*. 22:26 repeats the charge: purify My House for the circlers, the standers, the bowing, the prostrating.

22:26–29 will command a proclamation of ḥajj, a coming from every deep mountain-pass, a witnessing of benefits, a mention of Allah’s Name over cattle. 2:125 is that House before the proclamation, already a *mathābah* and an *amn*. A sanctuary that is then filled with idols is 9:28’s later cleaning. The charge *ṭahhirā* does not expire. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “One prayer in this mosque of mine is better than a thousand elsewhere except the Sacred Mosque” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said the Ḥaram was made sacred the day the heavens and the earth were created (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:125 is that sacredness as a making and a charge. Purify. Circle. Remain. Bow.

Ibn ʿUmar (may Allah be pleased with him) loved to pray at the *maqām* after *ṭawāf* as the Prophet (ﷺ) had done. A *sunnah* of two rakʿahs is 2:125 as a limb. A photograph of the stone without those rakʿahs is a relic.

For the reader the verse is a House with a job. Return to it if you can; return your chest to what it means if you cannot. Take a standing-place as a prayer, not as a relic. The next verse will give Ibrāhīm’s *duʿāʾ* for the town: make this a secure city, provide fruits for its people who believe. The Lord will answer with a wider provision, and a Fire for those who cover.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:126

> And ˹remember˺ when Abraham said, “My Lord, make this city ˹of Mecca˺ secure and provide fruits to its people—those among them who believe in Allah and the Last Day.” He answered, “As for those who disbelieve, I will let them enjoy themselves for a little while, then I will condemn them to the torment of the Fire. What an evil destination!”

**Commentary:**

The House is given a town’s *duʿāʾ*: *wa idh qāla Ibrāhīmu rabbi ijʿal hādhā baladan āminan wa urzuq ahlahu min al-thamarāti man āmana minhum bi-Allāhi wa al-yawmi al-ākhir qāla wa man kafara fa-umattiʿuhu qalīlan thumma aḍṭarruhu ilā ʿadhābi al-nāri wa biʾsa al-maṣīr*, “And when Ibrāhīm said, ‘My Lord, make this a secure town, and provide its people with fruits — those of them who believe in Allah and the Last Day.’ He said, ‘And whoever covers, I will let him enjoy a little, then I will drive him to the punishment of the Fire. What an evil becoming!’” *Rabbī*, my Lord — the *duʿāʾ* of a *khalīl*. *Ijʿal hādhā baladan āminan*, make this a secure town. 14:35 says *hādhā al-balada āminan*, this town secure, a later wording when the town had become a town. Al-Ṭabarī and others noted the difference: here a *balad* asked to be; there a *balad* asked to stay secure. Ibn al-Jawzī gathered what security: from killing; from being swallowed or stoned; from drought. All three sit in a Ḥaram’s history. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Allah made Makkah sacred, not the people. It is not lawful for a person who believes in Allah and the Last Day to shed blood in it, or to cut a tree” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the meanings of the sermon of the Fatḥ). 2:126 is that sacredness asked by a father before a son’s descendants filled the valley.

*Wa urzuq ahlahu min al-thamarāti man āmana minhum bi-Allāhi wa al-yawmi al-ākhir*, and provide its people with fruits — whoever of them believes in Allah and the Last Day. Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him) restricted the *rizq* as 2:124 had taught him: the covenant does not reach the wrongdoers. He had seen, in 14:35–36, idols, and he feared a *dhurriyyah*. He asked fruits for believers. The valley was without crops; 14:37: “I have settled some of my offspring in a valley without cultivation by Your Sacred House.” Ibn ʿAbbās narrated the long ḥadīth of Hājar, the running, Zamzam, the Jurhum, the House (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). Fruits came from Ṭāʾif and from a world that still feeds ḥajj. The restriction was a prophet’s *taqwā*. The answer was a Lord’s width.

*Qāla wa man kafara fa-umattiʿuhu qalīlan*, He said: and whoever covers, I will let him enjoy a little. Allah (الله) did not bind the world’s bread to *īmān* as He bound the *imāmah*. 3:196–197: let not the turning of those who cover in the land deceive you; a little enjoyment, then Jahannam. 17:18–20: He extends for the one who wants the world, and for the one who wants the Hereafter, and He supplies both. 2:126 is that *sunnah* spoken to a *khalīl* so that a prophet will not think he may starve a town into *īmān*. *Thumma aḍṭarruhu ilā ʿadhābi al-nār*, then I will drive him to the punishment of the Fire. *Iḍṭirār* is a compelling, a driving. The little enjoyment is not a pardon. It is a rope. *Wa biʾsa al-maṣīr*, and what an evil becoming — 2:126’s last taste after a *duʿāʾ* of fruits.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of *rizq*, hears that a *kāfir* in the Ḥaram may eat, and that a *ḥukm* of a world is not a *ḥukm* of a Garden. Ibn Kathīr treats the answer as a correction of a good restriction: Ibrāhīm’s *adab* was to ask for believers; the Lord’s *faḍl* was to feed a town, then to judge. The Prophet (ﷺ) fed the prisoner, received the gift of a mushrik when it was clean, and still said the Fire is the becoming of whoever dies covering. 2:126 is not a verse of cheap pluralism. It is a verse of a little *matāʿ* and a driven Fire.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd al-razzāq* and *tawḥīd al-ḥukm* together. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that provision in the world is often a common mercy, *raḥmah ʿāmmah*, and that provision of *īmān* is a special mercy, *raḥmah khāṣṣah*. 2:105’s *yakhtaṣṣu bi-raḥmatihi* is the special; 2:126’s fruits upon a mixed town are the common. Al-Rāzī noted Ibrāhīm’s restriction as a learning from 2:124, and the Lord’s expansion as a learning for every caller who would ration bread by a creed. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that a *kufr* fed in a Ḥaram is a worse *kufr* in ḥujjah, not a safer *kufr* in end.

14:37 asked that hearts of people incline toward them, and that they be provided fruits, so that they may give thanks. 2:126 is the fruits; 2:129 will be the messenger who teaches thanks. A town fed and not taught is a town of 28:58’s destroyed plenty. This *ummah* is spoken to in two ditches: a ditch that starves a neighbor to punish a creed, and a ditch that takes 2:126’s fruits as a proof that *kufr* is a small thing. The verse gives a little enjoyment, then a driving. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The world is a prison for the believer and a garden for the kāfir” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:126 is that garden as *qalīl*.

The little *matāʿ* of a kāfir in a Ḥaram is a ḥujjah heavier than a *matāʿ* in a land of no House. He ate from a *duʿāʾ* of a *khalīl* and then covered the *khalīl*’s messenger. 2:126’s Fire is that ḥujjah’s end.

For the reader the verse is a *duʿāʾ* and a width and a warning. Ask security for a town. Ask fruits. Do not think a full market is a full pardon. A little, then a Fire. What an evil becoming. The next verse will put the father’s hands on stones: Ibrāhīm raises the foundations with Ismāʿīl, and they say, Our Lord, accept from us; You are the All-Hearing, All-Knowing.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:127

> And ˹remember˺ when Abraham raised the foundation of the House with Ishmael, ˹both praying,˺ “Our Lord! Accept ˹this˺ from us. You are indeed the All-Hearing, All-Knowing.

**Commentary:**

The town’s *duʿāʾ* becomes a wall’s *duʿāʾ*: *wa idh yarfaʿu Ibrāhīmu al-qawāʿida min al-bayti wa Ismāʿīlu rabbanā taqabbal minnā innaka anta al-samīʿu al-ʿalīm*, “And when Ibrāhīm was raising the foundations of the House, and Ismāʿīl: ‘Our Lord, accept from us. Indeed You are the All-Hearing, All-Knowing.’” *Yarfaʿu* is in the imperfect: a raising that the listener is made to see, not a raising filed as a past. *Al-qawāʿid*, the foundations, the bases. Ibn ʿAbbās, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, said Ibrāhīm built and Ismāʿīl handed the stones; the House had a foundation from before, and they raised upon it. 3:96: the first House set for people is the one at Bakkah. 22:26: We settled for Ibrāhīm the place of the House. 2:127 is the labor. A prophet does not only speak a *duʿāʾ* of fruits. He lifts a stone.

*Wa Ismāʿīl* is named in the raising, a son in a father’s work. The People of the Book who drop Ismāʿīl from a House and from a *millah* are answered by a grammar: the foundations rise with both. The Prophet (ﷺ) is from that son. A father who takes a son to a work of a House, even young, is 2:127’s pedagogy. The Qur'an does not tell the years, the weight, the heat. It tells the sentence they spoke while the stones went up.

*Rabbanā taqabbal minnā*, our Lord, accept from us. *Taqabbal* is a seeking of *qubūl*, the acceptance 2:123 said a ransom will not receive. Here two prophets, building the most honored House on earth, do not say “we have completed a wonder.” They say accept. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that this is the *ʿubūdiyyah* of a deed: the deed is not a claim upon the Lord; it is a gift placed at a door. Al-Ḥasan used to say, “The believer’s concern is the *qabūl*, and the hypocrite’s concern is the deed’s being seen.” 2:127 is that concern in a *khalīl*’s mouth. *Minnā*, from us — both. A son is not a laborer unpaid in a father’s monument. He is in the *duʿāʾ*.

*Innaka anta al-samīʿu al-ʿalīm*, indeed You are the All-Hearing, All-Knowing. *Samīʿ* of a *duʿāʾ* spoken over stones; *ʿAlīm* of an intention a stone cannot show. 2:127 does not ask a Name of force. It asks a Name of hearing and knowledge, because acceptance is a matter of a chest, not of a height. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Actions are only by intentions, and every man shall have what he intended” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “Allah is good and accepts only what is good” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:127 is that *qabūl* asked by men whose deed was good and who still feared it would not be taken. 23:60: they give what they give, and their hearts are fearful.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of *ʿibādah*, hears that a *ḥajj*, a masjid built, a *waqf*, a recitation, should end in *rabbanā taqabbal*, not in a plaque. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as a proof that Ismāʿīl is a prophet in the work, not a footnote. Al-Rāzī noted the imperfect *yarfaʿu* as a making-present: every later builder of a masjid is invited into that raising if he says the same *duʿāʾ*. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *ikhlāṣ* is the condition of *qabūl*, and that a House raised for pride is 2:114’s cousin, not 2:127’s child.

This *ummah* is tried by a building without a *taqabbal*: mosques as competitions of a skyline, projects as a name on a stone, a ḥajj as a photograph. Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him) raised the *qawāʿid* of the Kaʿbah and asked for acceptance as if he had offered a date. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Protect yourselves from the Fire even with half a date” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A half date with *qabūl* outweighs a House without it — though this House was commanded. The point is the chest, not a belittling of a *qawāʿid*.

The *salaf* loved this *duʿāʾ* at the end of a *ṣalāh*, a fast, a building, a lesson: *rabbanā taqabbal minnā*. A deed that cannot bear that sentence is a deed that wanted a plaque. 3:195 will say He does not waste the wage of a doer, male or female. 2:127 asks the wage as *qabūl* before it is counted. A House of stone is still a deed of a chest. If the chest is for a Name, He hears; if the chest is for a tribe, He knows. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *rabbanā* as a pairing of two mouths. A family that builds and does not pray together has split 2:127. A family that prays and does not work has split it the other way. Raise. Ask. He hears. He knows.

A raising of foundations is a slow work. The Qur'an does not praise the speed. It praises the *duʿāʾ*. This *ummah* is addicted to a launch, a opening ceremony, a count of bricks. 2:127 is addicted to *taqabbal*. If He accepts a date, it outweighs a minaret He does not accept. If He accepts a minaret, it is because a chest said *rabbanā* first. The Prophet (ﷺ) built his mosque with palm and mud and asked no plaque. He said, “The most beloved of places to Allah are the mosques” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A mosque is beloved when a *taqabbal* is its air, not when a plaque is its boast. 9:108 praised a mosque founded on *taqwā* from the first day. 2:127 is that founding as a father’s and a son’s breath.

For the reader the verse is a stone and a sentence. Do the work. Do not trust the work. Ask acceptance. He is *Samīʿ*, *ʿAlīm*. The next verse will continue the same breath: make us both muslims to You, and from our descendants a muslim *ummah*; show us our rites; turn to us. You are the Accepter of repentance, the Merciful.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:128

> Our Lord! Make us both ˹fully˺ submit to You and from our descendants a nation that will submit to You. Show us our rituals, and turn to us in grace. You are truly the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful.

**Commentary:**

The asking of acceptance becomes an asking of a state: *rabbanā wa ijʿalnā muslimayni laka wa min dhurriyyatinā ummatan muslimatan laka wa arinā manāsikanā wa tub ʿalaynā innaka anta al-tawwābu al-raḥīm*, “Our Lord, and make us two muslims to You, and from our descendants a nation muslim to You; and show us our rites, and turn to us. Indeed You are the Accepter of repentance, the Merciful.” *Muslimayn* is a dual: Ibrāhīm and Ismāʿīl, already prophets, already raising a House, still asking to be made *muslim*. *Islām* here is the *islām* of 2:112’s face, 2:131’s *aslamtu*, not a passport already framed. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, read it as make us steadfast in submission, increase us in it, keep us in it until we die. A prophet asking *islām* is 12:101’s Yūsuf: “Take my soul as a muslim, and join me with the righteous.” The *duʿāʾ* of a beginning is also the *duʿāʾ* of an ending.

*Wa min dhurriyyatinā ummatan muslimatan laka*, and from our descendants a nation muslim to You. *Min* is partitive: from our descendants, not a claim that every child will be that *ummah*. 2:124 has already taught that the covenant does not reach the wrongdoers. Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him) asks a *ummah*, not a total lease. *Laka*, to You — the *lām* of a direction. A *ummah* muslim to a culture is not this *duʿāʾ*. Al-Qurṭubī hears a father’s *fiqh*: you cannot write *islām* in a bloodline; you can ask it of a Lord. 14:40: “My Lord, make me an establisher of prayer, and from my offspring.” 2:128 is that asking as a *ummah*, not only as a man.

*Wa arinā manāsikanā*, and show us our rites. *Manāsik* are the rites of ḥajj, the slaughter, the *ṭawāf*, the standing, the *fiqh* of a House they are raising. *Arinā*, show us: a seeing that is a teaching. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Take your rites from me” (al-Bayhaqī, and the meaning of “pray as you have seen me pray” in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:128 is that taking asked before a *sharīʿah* of ḥajj was shown to this *ummah*. Jibrīl showed Ibrāhīm the *mashāʿir*, the *salaf* said. A House without rites is a stone. Rites without a House are a wandering. They ask both.

*Wa tub ʿalaynā*, and turn to us. *Tawbah* of a Lord is a turning toward a servant; *tawbah* of a servant is a turning toward a Lord. Two prophets building the Kaʿbah ask *tawbah*. The verse is a killing of a pride that says: prophets do not need a turning. 47:19: “Know that there is no god but Allah, and ask forgiveness for your sin, and for the believing men and believing women.” The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “By Allah, I ask Allah’s forgiveness and turn to Him more than seventy times a day” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:128 is that turning as a *duʿāʾ* of a raising. *Innaka anta al-tawwābu al-raḥīm*, indeed You are the Accepter of repentance, the Merciful. *Tawwāb* is intensive: He turns again and again. *Raḥīm* is the particular mercy of 1:3. A *duʿāʾ* that ends in these two Names is a *duʿāʾ* that knows a House does not save a chest.

Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as the inner work beside 2:127’s outer stones. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *islām*, a *ummah*, *manāsik*, and *tawbah* are four pillars of a father’s will: a state, a people, a law of a rite, a door of return. Al-Rāzī noted that they did not ask a kingdom. They asked submission. 2:258’s Namrūd asked a kingdom. 2:128’s *khalīl* asked *islām*. This *ummah* is the *ummah muslimah* of that *duʿāʾ*, 22:78: “It is He who named you muslims before, and in this.” A naming is a debt.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Every child is born upon the *fiṭrah*” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:128 is a father asking that a *fiṭrah* not be switched. This *ummah* is tried by a *dhurriyyah* given a House-trip and not given a *islām*, a rite taught as tourism, a *tawbah* postponed because a grandfather was a *khalīl*. Ask. He is *Tawwāb*, *Raḥīm*.

22:78 will say: strive in Allah with the striving due Him; He has chosen you; He named you muslims from before, and in this, that the Messenger be a witness over you and you witnesses over people. 2:128 is the *duʿāʾ* that 22:78 answers. A *ummah* that forgets it was asked in a *khalīl*’s mouth will treat *islām* as a ethnicity. It is a making: *ijʿalnā*. He makes. You ask. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *muslimayn laka* as a dual that does not include the stones. The House is not a third muslim. The two men are. A people who love a Kaʿbah and do not submit have raised a *qawāʿid* without 2:128.

A prophet asking *tawbah* while building the Kaʿbah is a curriculum for a pilgrim who thinks a ḥajj is a wipe without a turning. 2:196–203 will later time a ḥajj’s *dhikr* and a *duʿāʾ* of a world and a Hereafter. 2:128 is the *duʿāʾ* under the stones: make us muslim, make a *ummah*, show rites, turn to us. Without those four, a *ṭawāf* is a walk. Ask them in a *ḥajj*, in a *ṣalāh*, in a birth of a child. He is *Tawwāb* enough for a house that keeps slipping; He is *Raḥīm* enough for a *dhurriyyah* you cannot force.

For the reader the verse is a fourfold ask: make me muslim; make a *ummah* from my line; show me the rites; turn to me. You are *Tawwāb*, *Raḥīm*. The next verse will complete the *duʿāʾ* with a messenger: raise among them a messenger from them, who recites Your signs, teaches them the Book and the wisdom, and purifies them. You are the Almighty, the Wise.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:129

> Our Lord! Raise from among them a messenger who will recite to them Your revelations, teach them the Book and wisdom, and purify them. Indeed, You ˹alone˺ are the Almighty, All-Wise.”

**Commentary:**

The *ummah muslimah* is asked a teacher from its own blood: *rabbanā wa ibʿath fīhim rasūlan minhum yatlū ʿalayhim āyātika wa yuʿallimuhumu al-kitāba wa al-ḥikmata wa yuzakkīhim innaka anta al-ʿazīzu al-ḥakīm*, “Our Lord, and raise among them a messenger from them who will recite upon them Your signs, and teach them the Book and the wisdom, and purify them. Indeed You are the Almighty, the Wise.” *Ibʿath*, raise, send — the verb of a *baʿth* of a messenger, and of a *baʿth* of a grave. Ibn al-Jawzī gathered *fīhim*: in their descendants, Mujāhid said; in the people of Makkah, as 2:126’s *ahlahu*. Both are one man. The messenger is Muḥammad (ﷺ). He said, “I am the *duʿāʾ* of my father Ibrāhīm, the good news of ʿĪsā, and the vision of my mother” (Aḥmad, and the well-known meaning of the reports). 2:129 is that *duʿāʾ* written before a *ummah* could say his name.

*Rasūlan minhum*, a messenger from them. From them, not a stranger dropped upon a town as a foreign king. 9:128: “There has come to you a messenger from yourselves.” 3:164: “Allah conferred a favor upon the believers when He raised among them a messenger from themselves.” 62:2: “He is the One who raised among the unlettered a messenger from them.” 2:129 asked it; those verses named the answer. *Minnah* is a favor. A people who wanted a prophet from Banū Isrāʾīl only (2:90) are answered by a *khalīl*’s *duʿāʾ* for Ismāʿīl’s line.

*Yatlū ʿalayhim āyātika*, he recites upon them Your signs. *Tilāwah* of 2:121, now as a messenger’s job. The signs are recited, not invented. *Wa yuʿallimuhumu al-kitāba wa al-ḥikmah*, and he teaches them the Book and the wisdom. The Book is the Qur'an. *Al-ḥikmah*, Ibn ʿAbbās and Qatādah said, is the *sunnah*; al-Shāfiʿī made that a pillar of *uṣūl*: Allah (الله) named a teaching beside the Book, so the Messenger (ﷺ) is a teacher of a *ḥikmah* that is obeyed, 4:65, 59:7. Some said *ḥikmah* is the understanding of the Book, the *fiqh* of a placing. The two live: a *sunnah* is a *ḥikmah*, and a *ḥikmah* is a placing of a Book. *Wa yuzakkīhim*, and he purifies them. *Tazkiyah* is a growth and a cleaning, 91:9’s *qad aflaḥa man zakkāhā*. Purification from *shirk*, from *jāhiliyyah*, from a filth of a custom. 2:151 and 3:164 will reverse the order: purify, then teach. 2:129 puts reciting and teaching before purifying in the *duʿāʾ*’s list; 62:2 matches 2:129. The *salaf* did not make the orders a war. A messenger recites, teaches, purifies; a chest is washed by a word and a word is understood by a washed chest.

*Innaka anta al-ʿazīzu al-ḥakīm*, indeed You are the Almighty, the Wise. *ʿAzīz* is the One who is not prevented. *Ḥakīm* is the One who places. A *duʿāʾ* for a messenger ends in these two Names because a sending is a power and a wisdom, 6:124. 2:129’s last Names answer 2:90’s resentment: He sends where He knows.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of *taʿlīm*, hears four jobs that a later scholar inherits only as a shadow: recite, teach Book, teach *ḥikmah*, purify. A school that recites and does not purify is a half of 2:129. A school that purifies by a mood and does not teach a Book is the other half. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as Ibrāhīm’s greatest *duʿāʾ* after the House, because a House without a messenger becomes an idol, and a messenger without a House still has a *qiblah* in a chest. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *tazkiyah* is the fruit, and that a knowledge that does not purify is 2:102’s learning that harms. Al-Rāzī noted *minhum* as a mercy to a people who would otherwise say: we were not addressed.

The Prophet (ﷺ) lived the four. He recited until his beard wept. He taught a Book and a *ḥikmah* in a sitting, a saddle, a *minbar*. He purified a town of idols, a chest of *riyāʾ*, a market of *ribā*. 2:151 will remind this *ummah* that they were in a clear misguidance before that sending. This *ummah* is tried by loving 2:129 as a history and not as a method: we want a revival without a recitation, a *ḥikmah* without a Book, a purification without a messenger’s *sunnah*. The *duʿāʾ* was not for a mood. It was for a man. Then for a following.

3:164 will call the sending a *mann* upon the believers: he recites, purifies, teaches Book and *ḥikmah*, though they were in a clear misguidance. 2:129 asked that *mann* before the misguidance of a *jāhiliyyah* had a name in this *ummah*. A people who have received the four jobs and still live as if they had not are 2:101’s fling in a new coat. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *yatlū ʿalayhim* as a recitation upon them, a recitation that lands. A recitation in a vacuum of a studio is not yet 2:129 until it is *ʿalayhim*, upon a people.

62:2 will name the unlettered, a messenger from them, reciting, purifying, teaching Book and *ḥikmah*. 2:129 asked it in a valley without a Book. The answer is this *ummah*’s existence. To reject him is to reject a *khalīl*’s *duʿāʾ* that Allah (الله) accepted. That is 2:130’s *safah* in a history. Love him as an answered *duʿāʾ*, not as a interruption of a older Book. The older Book, if kept, would have named him. 7:157 remains.

For the reader the verse is why this Prophet (ﷺ) is not an interruption of Ibrāhīm. He is Ibrāhīm’s ask. Recite. Learn the Book and the *ḥikmah*. Let him purify you. The next verse will name the fool who turns from that *millah*: who would reject the faith of Ibrāhīm except a fool? We chose him in this life, and in the Hereafter he is among the righteous.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:130

> And who would reject the faith of Abraham except a fool! We certainly chose him in this life, and in the Hereafter he will surely be among the righteous.

**Commentary:**

The *duʿāʾ* is sealed with a verdict upon whoever leaves the path it asked: *wa man yarghabu ʿan millati Ibrāhīma illā man safiha nafsahu wa la-qad iṣṭafaynāhu fī al-dunyā wa innahu fī al-ākhirati la-min al-ṣāliḥīn*, “And who turns away from the *millah* of Ibrāhīm except one who fools his own soul? We have certainly chosen him in this world, and in the Hereafter he is surely among the righteous.” *Yarghabu ʿan* is to turn the face from, to dislike as a leaving. *Raghbah* toward a thing is a wanting; *raghbah ʿan* is a wanting away. *Millat Ibrāhīm* is his religion, his way, 2:135’s ḥanīf *millah*, 16:123’s command to the Prophet (ﷺ) to follow it. It is *tawḥīd*, *islām* of 2:131, a House purified, a son not withheld, a fire not worshipped, stars not taken as lords. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, read the verse as a rebuke of pagans who claimed Ibrāhīm while they circled idols, and of a party of the Book who claimed him while they rejected the messenger he asked in 2:129.

*Illā man safiha nafsahu*, except one who fools his own soul. Ibn al-Jawzī gathered: except one who makes a fool of himself; who destroys himself; whose soul is foolish; who is ignorant of his own soul and does not look at it. Al-Zajjāj preferred the last. *Safah* is lightness of a mind, 2:13’s “shall we believe as the fools have believed,” 2:142’s “the fools will say, what has turned them from their qiblah.” 2:130 names the fool: the one who leaves Ibrāhīm. A people who called the believers fools are told where the *safah* sits. *Nafsahu* is his own soul as object: the fooling is not a cleverness upon another. It is a harm of a self. 2:102 sold selves; 2:130 fools a self.

*Wa la-qad iṣṭafaynāhu fī al-dunyā*, We have certainly chosen him in this world. *Iṣṭifāʾ* is a selecting, 3:33’s choosing of Ādam, Nūḥ, Āl Ibrāhīm, Āl ʿImrān over the worlds. 2:130’s choosing is a *khalīl*, an imam, a House, a *duʿāʾ* answered. A man chosen in the world is not a man a later creed may retire as a Jew or a Christian of 2:140. *Wa innahu fī al-ākhirati la-min al-ṣāliḥīn*, and in the Hereafter he is surely of the righteous. *Ṣāliḥīn* is 21:105’s righteous who inherit, 2:130’s last honor. Choosing in the world is not a substitute for *ṣalāḥ* in the next. Ibrāhīm has both. 29:27: We gave him Isḥāq and Yaʿqūb, and We placed in his offspring prophecy and the Book; We gave him his reward in this world, and in the Hereafter he is among the righteous. 2:130 is 29:27 in a question about a fool.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “I have been sent to complete good character” (Aḥmad, al-Bukhārī in *al-Adab al-Mufrad*, in the well-known meaning). He said his *dīn* is easy, ḥanīf. He said, “The most beloved *dīn* to Allah is the ḥanīfiyyah, the easy” (Aḥmad, and the meaning of the reports). 2:130’s *millah* is that ḥanīfiyyah. A turning from it toward a son of 2:116, toward a *siḥr* of 2:102, toward a *millah* of 2:120 that must be pleased, is *safah*. Al-Qurṭubī hears a *fiqh*: a man who leaves *tawḥīd* for a custom of fathers has judged his soul light. Ibn Kathīr treats *iṣṭafaynāhu* as a proof against those who claim Ibrāhīm and then oppose his *duʿāʾ*’s messenger.

Theologically the verse is the measure of a mind. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that intellect in the *sharʿ* is a following of a *millah* that matches the *fiṭrah*, and that a philosophy that leaves Ibrāhīm for a cause without a Lord is a *safah* dressed as a faculty. Al-Rāzī noted the exception *illā man safiha* as a restriction that leaves no third camp: you are upon the *millah*, or you are a fool of a soul. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *millat Ibrāhīm* is *ikhlāṣ* of *ʿibādah* and *barāʾah* from *shirk*, 60:4’s *uswah* in Ibrāhīm and those with him. 2:130 is that *uswah* as a test of a mind.

This *ummah* is spoken to whenever it turns from a *sunnah* of *tawḥīd* toward a fashion of a *millah* it fears. 2:120 said they will not be pleased until you follow. 2:130 says following Ibrāhīm is the mind, and leaving him is the fool. The Prophet (ﷺ) will be told 3:67: Ibrāhīm was not a Jew and not a Christian, but a ḥanīf muslim, and he was not of the associators. 2:130 plants that.

6:161–163 will command the Prophet (ﷺ): I have been guided to a straight path, a *millah* of Ibrāhīm, ḥanīf; he was not of the associators; my prayer and my sacrifice, my living and my dying, are for Allah, Lord of the worlds. 2:130’s fool is the man who hears that path and wants another color. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *nafsahu* as a mercy in a rebuke: the harm is named as self-harm, so that a return is still a repair of a soul, not a negotiation with a tribe.

3:68 will say the nearest of people to Ibrāhīm are those who followed him, and this Prophet, and those who believe; Allah is the *Walī* of the believers. 2:130’s fool is far though he recites a genealogy. Nearness is a *millah*, not a blood. A fool of a soul can be a scholar of a lineage. 2:13 called the believers fools; 2:130 returns the word to its owner. Take the *millah*. Leave the insult. A chosen imam is not a tribal mascot.

For the reader the verse is a question. Are you turning from Ibrāhīm? Then you are fooling you. He was chosen here, and he is righteous there. The next verse will give the *millah* in one exchange: when his Lord said, Submit, he said, I submit to the Lord of the worlds.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:131

> When his Lord ordered him, “Submit ˹to My Will˺,” he responded, “I submit to the Lord of all worlds.”

**Commentary:**

The *millah* is now a single word answered: *idh qāla lahu rabbuhu aslim qāla aslamtu li-rabbi al-ʿālamīn*, “When his Lord said to him, ‘Submit,’ he said, ‘I have submitted to the Lord of the worlds.’” *Aslim* is an imperative without a scene attached. The Qur'an does not here name a fire, a knife, a *hijrah*. It names a command that covers them all. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, read *aslim* as be sincere, be a muslim, surrender. The command is the *dīn*. The answer is the *dīn*. *Qāla aslamtu*, he said I have submitted — a past of a completion, not a promise of a later sitting. Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him) does not ask 2:108’s sign first. He does not say *rāʿinā*. He submits.

*Li-rabbi al-ʿālamīn*, to the Lord of the worlds. The *lām* is a direction of a *islām*. The Name is 1:2’s Name, the Fātiḥah’s first praise. A man who submits to a custom, a king, a fear, a *millah* of 2:120, has not said 2:131. He has said a smaller *aslamtu*. Ibrāhīm’s *islām* is aimed at the *Rabb* of every world: the world of angels, of jinn, of men, of a fire that cooled, of a knife that did not cut, of a wife in a valley, of a star he refused. Al-Qurṭubī hears that the answer names the Lord, not the self: he does not say “I have submitted” as a boast of a faculty; he says “to the Lord of the worlds,” as a placing of a slave.

The Prophet (ﷺ) was commanded the same. 6:162–163: “Say: my prayer, my sacrifice, my living and my dying are for Allah, Lord of the worlds, no partner has He; and of that I have been commanded, and I am the first of the muslims.” 3:20: if they argue, say, I have submitted my face to Allah, and whoever follows me. 2:131 is the father of those sentences. He (ﷺ) said, “None of you believes until his desire follows what I brought” (al-Nawawī’s forty, a ḥadīth disputed in chain and sound in meaning to the *salaf*’s *ittibāʿ*). He said, “The ḥalāl is clear and the ḥarām is clear” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:131 is the clear as a verb.

Theologically the verse is *islām* as a response, not as a mood. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *aslim* is a word that enters a prayer, a wage, a marriage, a death, a fatwa you dislike. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *islām* of the heart is *inqiād*, a yielding, and *islām* of the limb is a doing, and Ibrāhīm’s *aslamtu* is both in one past tense. Al-Rāzī noted the absence of a *lawlā*: 2:118’s people wanted Allah to speak as a condition; 2:131’s imam heard *aslim* and spoke *aslamtu*. Two chests. One is 2:130’s fool. One is 2:130’s chosen.

This *ummah* is spoken to in every *aslim* that arrives without a theatre: a command of a *ḥijāb*, a *ribā* to leave, a prayer that stands when sleep is sweet, a truth that costs a smile of 2:120. Ibrāhīm’s answer is short enough to memorize and wide enough to spend a life. The Prophet (ﷺ) taught a man who came to become muslim with a *shahādah* and a washing and a prayer. 2:131 is that beginning as a prophet’s whole biography.

37:102 showed a son saying, “O my father, do as you are commanded; you will find me, if Allah wills, of the patient.” That son’s *aslim* is 2:131 in a second generation. A household of *aslamtu* is not a household of a debate club. It is a household that has learned a verb. 4:65: they do not believe until they make you the judge of what they differ in, then find no tightness in their souls. 2:131 is that absence of tightness in a father. This *ummah* recites *iyyāka naʿbudu* every *rakʿah*; 2:131 is that *iyyāka* as a biography. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *rabbuhu* as *his* Lord: the Lord who tested him with *kalimāt* in 2:124 is the Lord who said *aslim*. Tests are not another god. They are this Lord’s classroom. A man who submits in a ease and argues in a *kalimah* of a trial has not yet said *aslamtu* as Ibrāhīm said it.

Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as the *tafsīr* of *millat Ibrāhīm*: the *millah* is this answer. 2:135 will refuse Jewry and Christianity as a condition of *hudā* and point to this ḥanīf. 2:131 is the ḥanīf in a verb.

The command *aslim* arrives in a life as a ḥalāl left, a ḥarām dropped, a prayer stood, a pride broken. Ibrāhīm’s answer did not wait for a committee. This *ummah* waits, and calls the wait a *fiqh*. Sometimes it is a *fiqh*. Sometimes it is 2:108’s asking. 2:131 is the short path: He said submit; he said I have submitted to the Lord of the worlds. Keep the sentence in the mouth when a *kalimah* of a trial comes. The fire cooled for a man who had already said it. 21:69: We said, O fire, be coolness and peace upon Ibrāhīm. Coolness is for a *aslamtu* already spoken. A man who waits to submit until a fire is cool has inverted the verse. Submit, then the coolness is His affair. The Lord of the worlds does not share the *aslim* with a smaller lord of a mood, a market, or a *millah* of 2:120. Say *aslamtu* in a wage, in a marriage, in a diagnosis, in a qiblah a workplace dislikes. The sentence is short so that a trial cannot claim it was too long to remember.

For the reader the verse is a command you already know. Submit. Say it to the Lord of the worlds, not to a smaller lord. The next verse will make that answer a will: Ibrāhīm charged his children with it, and Yaʿqūb; Allah has chosen this *dīn* for you, so do not die except as muslims.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:132

> This was the advice of Abraham—as well as Jacob—to his children, ˹saying˺, “Indeed, Allah has chosen for you this faith; so do not die except in ˹a state of full˺ submission.”

**Commentary:**

The *aslamtu* becomes a will: *wa waṣṣā bihā Ibrāhīmu banīhi wa Yaʿqūbu yā baniyya inna Allāha iṣṭafā lakumu al-dīna fa-lā tamūtunna illā wa antum muslimūn*, “And Ibrāhīm charged his children with it, and Yaʿqūb: ‘O my children, indeed Allah has chosen for you the *dīn*, so do not die except while you are muslims.’” *Waṣṣā* is a charge, a *waṣiyyah*, the last serious speech a father puts on a house. *Bihā*, with it — with the *millah*, with the *aslamtu*, with 2:131. Ibn ʿAbbās and the mufassirūn, as al-Ṭabarī records, read the verse as a proof that this *dīn* is not a later invention of Madinah. It is a father’s charge in a house of prophets. *Wa Yaʿqūb* is named so that Israel cannot say: our father did not say this. Yaʿqūb (peace be upon him) said it. 2:133 will show him saying it at a deathbed.

*Yā baniyya*, O my children — a vocative of *raḥmah*, 12:67’s *yā baniyya* when he feared a gate, 12:87’s *yā baniyya* when he asked them not to despair of Allah’s *rūḥ*. A *waṣiyyah* of a *dīn* is spoken as a love, not as a cold law. *Inna Allāha iṣṭafā lakumu al-dīn*, indeed Allah has chosen for you the *dīn*. *Iṣṭifāʾ* of 2:130 upon Ibrāhīm is now an *iṣṭifāʾ* of a *dīn* upon children. 5:3: “I have approved for you *islām* as a *dīn*.” 2:132 is that approval as a father’s news. The *dīn* is chosen *for you*, a gift, 2:105’s *raḥmah*. A child who treats it as a chain has not heard *lakum*.

*Fa-lā tamūtunna illā wa antum muslimūn*, so do not die except while you are muslims. The prohibition is of a death in another state, which is a command of a life in this state, because a man does not time his death. Ibn Kathīr treats it as a command to hold *islām* until the last breath, *ḥusn al-khātimah*. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “One of you does the deeds of the people of the Garden until there is only a cubit between him and it, then the writing overtakes him and he does the deeds of the people of the Fire” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “Do not wish for death” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). He taught the *duʿāʾ*, “O Allah, make the end of our affair good, and save us from disgrace in this world and from punishment in the Hereafter.” 12:101’s Yūsuf asked to die a muslim. 2:132’s fathers command it as a household law.

Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist of *waṣiyyah*, hears that a muslim’s will is not only a gold and a house. It is a *dīn*. 4:11’s shares come after a *dīn* is guarded. A father who leaves a fortune and not a 2:132 has inverted Ibrāhīm. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *lā tamūtunna illā wa antum muslimūn* is the most concise pedagogy of a life: you cannot guarantee the hour, so you keep the state. Al-Rāzī noted *iṣṭafā lakum* as a killing of 2:135’s “be Jews or Christians”: the chosen *dīn* is this *islām*, not a later slogan.

This *ummah* is spoken to in every death it postpones as if *islām* were a retirement project. A young man who says “when I am old,” a scholar who says “when I am free,” a parent who teaches a career and whispers a *dīn*, have not heard *fa-lā tamūtunna*. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The most intelligent of you is the one who remembers death most” (Ibn Mājah, in a weak chain with a sound meaning the *salaf* lived). He said, “Be in the world as a stranger or a traveler” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:132 is that traveler’s passport: muslim, muslim, muslim, until the stop.

3:102: “O you who believe, have *taqwā* of Allah with the *taqwā* due Him, and do not die except while you are muslims.” 2:132’s *waṣiyyah* is given to this *ummah* as a verse of its own. A death in *islām* is not a slogan at a funeral. It is a life of a *ṣalāh* that 4:103 timed, a *tawbah* that 66:8 asked, a *shahādah* that is renewed when a chest slips. The Prophet (ﷺ) loved to ask for a death in al-Madinah, and he died there; the point is not a geography only. It is a state. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on the pairing of Ibrāhīm and Yaʿqūb as two houses: Ismāʿīl’s Arabs and Isḥāq’s Israel are both under one *waṣiyyah*. 2:133 will make Israel’s sons answer it. 2:135 will make a later Israel and a later church try to replace it with a *millah* of a name. The charge is older than both names.

Theologically the verse is *istidāmah*, a remaining. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *īmān* increases and decreases, and that a *waṣiyyah* to die muslim is a *waṣiyyah* to renew. 49:11, 66:8. A state not renewed is a state that 2:8 can wear as a sentence.

A house that talks of a will of gold and not of a will of *islām* has inverted the prophets. Write 2:132 on a chest before you write a notary. The children will inherit a state only if they saw it lived. Speak *yā baniyya* while you can still sit at a table. A *waṣiyyah* left only in a lawyer’s file is not Ibrāhīm’s and not Yaʿqūb’s. Leave a *dīn* that can be seen.

For the reader the verse is a father’s last serious word. The *dīn* was chosen for you. Do not die except in it. Keep it today because you do not own tomorrow. The next verse will ask a people who claim Yaʿqūb: were you witnesses when death came to him? He said, What will you worship after me? They said, We will worship your God and the God of your fathers, Ibrāhīm, Ismāʿīl, and Isḥāq, one God, and we are muslims.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:133

> Or did you witness when death came to Jacob? He asked his children, “Who will you worship after my passing?” They replied, “We will ˹continue to˺ worship your God, the God of your forefathers—Abraham, Ishmael, and Isaac—the One God. And to Him we ˹all˺ submit.”

**Commentary:**

The *waṣiyyah* is now a deathbed the claimants did not see: *am kuntum shuhadāʾa idh ḥaḍara Yaʿqūba al-mawtu idh qāla li-banīhi mā taʿbudūna min baʿdī qālū naʿbudu ilāhaka wa ilāha ābāʾika Ibrāhīma wa Ismāʿīla wa Isḥāqa ilāhan wāḥidan wa naḥnu lahu muslimūn*, “Or were you witnesses when death came to Yaʿqūb, when he said to his children, ‘What will you worship after me?’ They said, ‘We will worship your God and the God of your fathers, Ibrāhīm, Ismāʿīl, and Isḥāq — one God — and we are muslims to Him.’” *Am kuntum shuhadāʾ*, or were you witnesses — a question that empties a claim. A people who say “we are upon Yaʿqūb” are asked if they stood at the bed. They did not. The Lord reports the bed. Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as a criticism of pagans of Ismāʿīl’s line and of covering ones among Israel: both claim a father; both are told the father’s last *dīn*. Al-Ṭabarī reads *mā taʿbudūna* as a *mā* of a what, a asking of an object of *ʿibādah*, the most serious question a dying imam can ask. Not: who will rule. Not: who will keep the well. What will you worship.

*Min baʿdī*, after me. A prophet knows that a house can change a lord when a father’s back is in a grave. 2:133 is a locking of a lord before a burial. The children answer with a chain: *ilāhaka wa ilāha ābāʾika*, your God and the God of your fathers. *Ābāʾ* includes Ismāʿīl, who is an uncle, and the language of the Arabs allows an uncle as a father in a honor of a line. Ibn ʿAbbās and the *salaf* noted Ismāʿīl’s naming among Yaʿqūb’s fathers as a lifting of the dropped son. The God is one: *ilāhan wāḥidan*. 2:163: your God is one God. 112:1. A chain of fathers is not a chain of gods. It is a chain of one *ilāh*. *Wa naḥnu lahu muslimūn*, and we are to Him muslims. The answer of 2:131 is now a children’s chorus. They did not say “we are Jews.” They did not say “we are Christians.” Those names had not been made a *millah* of 2:135. They said *muslimūn*. 2:140 will ask: do you say they were Jews or Christians?

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Prompt your dying ones with *lā ilāha illā Allāh*” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:133 is that prompting as a prophet’s last *fiqh*. He said, “Whoever’s last speech is *lā ilāha illā Allāh* will enter the Garden” (Abū Dāwūd, Aḥmad, in the well-known reports). Yaʿqūb (peace be upon him) did not prompt a name of a tribe. He prompted a *ʿibādah*. Al-Qurṭubī hears a *fiqh* of a muslim’s death: the *waṣiyyah* of gold is *sunnah*; the *waṣiyyah* of *tawḥīd* is the *waṣiyyah*. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *mā taʿbudūna min baʿdī* is the question every parent is asked by a *qadar* even if they do not speak it: what did you leave in the house as a lord? A screen, a wage, a saint, a Book?

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd* as inheritance, not a tribe as inheritance. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that a *nasab* to Yaʿqūb without Yaʿqūb’s *ilāh* is 2:134’s *ummah* that has passed: you do not own their *kasb*. Al-Rāzī noted *ilāhan wāḥidan* as a *ḥāl* that blocks a Trinity and a calf and a son of 2:116 in one accusative. This *ummah* is tried by a deathbed of a hospital that talks of a property and not of a Lord, a funeral of a flag and not of a *shahādah*. 2:133 is the other bed.

12:101’s Yūsuf asked to die a muslim and to be joined with the *ṣāliḥīn*; 2:133’s brothers answer as *muslimūn* while their father still breathes. A family can say the sentence and later sell a brother, and then repent. The verse records the creed of the room, not a sinlessness of twelve biographies. The creed remains the measure. A later Israel that keeps the names and drops the *ilāhan wāḥidan* for a calf, or drops the messenger of 2:129, has left the room. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *shuhadāʾ* as a rebuke of a knowledge they did not have and a claim they still made. 2:140’s hiding of a testimony is the same file. You were not witnesses. He was a witness. He reported. Believe the report.

The twelve sons, the *asbāṭ* of 2:136, are here a *ummah muslimah* in a room. Yūsuf (peace be upon him) is in that room in a history. The Qur'an does not list the twelve in 2:133. It lists the answer. The answer is the *dīn*. A later Israel that rejects 2:129’s messenger has left the room’s sentence while keeping the room’s name.

The question *mā taʿbudūna min baʿdī* is the question of a grave’s first night in a father’s mouth. Teach the answer while you can still hear them say it. A silent house will learn a lord from a feed. Sit, as Yaʿqūb sat, and ask the object of *ʿibādah* before the object of a career. 2:133 is a curriculum of a last hour that must be taught in a first hour, while a house can still answer in a chorus of *muslimūn*. Do not wait for a hospital to teach *tawḥīd*. Teach it at a table, as Yaʿqūb taught it at a bed.

For the reader the verse is a question to put on your own house. What will they worship after you? Teach the One God, the fathers’ God as *muslimūn*, not as a slogan of a blood. The next verse will cut the boast of that blood: that was a *ummah* that has passed; for them what they earned, for you what you earned; you will not be asked about what they used to do.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:134

> That was a community that had already gone before. For them is what they earned and for you is what you have earned. And you will not be accountable for what they have done.

**Commentary:**

The deathbed is closed with a law of *kasb*: *tilka ummatun qad khalat lahā mā kasabat wa lakum mā kasabtum wa lā tusʾalūna ʿammā kānū yaʿmalūn*, “That was a nation that has already passed. For it is what it earned, and for you is what you have earned, and you will not be asked about what they used to do.” *Tilka ummah*, that nation — Ibrāhīm, Ismāʿīl, Isḥāq, Yaʿqūb, the sons who said *muslimūn*. *Qad khalat*, it has already passed, gone empty of this hour’s stage. You cannot pull their *ṣalāḥ* onto your scale as a free credit. You cannot push their mistakes onto a neighbor as a inherited stain in reverse. *Lahā mā kasabat*, for it is what it earned. *Kasb* is 2:79’s earning, 2:81’s earning of a sin, 2:134’s earning of a *ummah*. 53:39: man has nothing except what he strives for. 2:123: no soul avails another. 2:134 is that *ʿadl* as a history.

*Wa lakum mā kasabtum*, and for you is what you have earned. The addressees are Israel in the first line, and this *ummah* in a standing line, and every later grandson who says “my grandfather was a *walī*.” Al-Qurṭubī, as a jurist, hears a killing of two diseases: a pride of a *nasab* that does not work, and a despair of a *nasab* that thinks a father’s sin is a seal. 6:164: no bearer of a burden bears another’s burden. 35:18. 2:134 is both. *Wa lā tusʾalūna ʿammā kānū yaʿmalūn*, and you will not be asked about what they used to do. 2:119: you will not be asked about the companions of the Blaze. 2:134: you will not be asked about a prophet’s household’s deeds as if they were yours. You will be asked about yours.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Whoever is slowed by his deed will not be hastened by his lineage” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, the ḥadīth of Abū Hurayrah in some wordings of a sermon). He said, “O Banū Hāshim, save yourselves from Allah; I cannot avail you from Allah anything” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:134 is that sermon as a verse upon a people who said “our fathers are Ibrāhīm and Mūsā.” Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as a cut between a beautiful history and a present file. Al-Ṭabarī reads *khalat* as a mercy and a threat: a mercy, because you are not billed for their slips; a threat, because you are not paid for their *sujūd*.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd* of a scale. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that a man who lives on a *ummah*’s memory is a man who has not yet begun his *kasb*. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *walāyah* is by *īmān* and *taqwā*, 10:62–63, not by a grave of a grandfather. Al-Rāzī noted the verse’s return at 2:141 as a *taʾkīd* after a long argument: the sūrah will say it twice so that a *nasab* will not sneak back through 2:135–140’s debate.

This *ummah* is tried by a museum-islam: we mention Ṣalāḥ al-Dīn, we mention a Companion, we mention a *madhhab*’s imam, and we do not stand 2:110’s prayer. 2:134 does not forbid a mention. It forbids a mention as a *kasb*. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “When a man dies, his work is cut off except from three: a continuing charity, knowledge benefited from, a righteous child who prays for him” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). A child who prays is a *kasb* the dead still receive as a gift of a Lord, not as a lease of a blood. 2:134 still stands: you will not be asked about their work as if it were yours; they are not asked about yours.

2:141 will say this verse again after the argument of names, so that a *nasab* cannot sneak through a debate. A sūrah that repeats a law is a sūrah that knows a disease returns. This *ummah* mentions Companions, and it should; it does not own their *kasb* as a substitute for 2:110. 59:10 taught a *duʿāʾ* for those who preceded in *īmān*, and a asking that no spite remain. Love of a passed *ummah* is a *dīn*. Living on their scale is a *wahm*. Al-Qurṭubī also hears a *adab* of *ikhtilāf*: do not spend a life prosecuting a dead *ummah*’s inner deeds that 2:134 said you will not be asked about, while your own *kasb* is a flung Book. 2:121 asked a due recitation. 2:134 says the recitation of a father will not be written in your *ṣaḥīfah* unless you recited.

A *nasab* is a *niʿmah* to thank, 49:13, not a scale to sit on. Thank Ibrāhīm by a *islām*, not by a slogan. Work. 53:39 remains. 2:123 remains. 2:134 is those two verses as a history of a family you love and cannot cash. Honor them with a *duʿāʾ*, 59:10. Do not honor them by a laziness that cites their *sujūd* as your *kasb*. The scale will not accept a citation. It will accept a *kasb*. Send 2:110’s *khayr* under your own name, and love the passed *ummah* as a *duʿāʾ*, not as a substitute. Their *sujūd* will not be written in your *ṣaḥīfah* because you cited it in a speech. Your *sujūd* might. Stand. A name on a family tree is a *niʿmah* to thank, not a wage to collect. Collect a wage with a limb, today, under your own name.

For the reader the verse is a scale with your name. That *ummah* has passed. Theirs is theirs. Yours is yours. You will not be billed for their hour. You will be billed for this one. The next verse will name a living slogan that tries to replace that *kasb*: they say, be Jews or Christians and you will be guided. Say: rather, the *millah* of Ibrāhīm, the upright, who was not of the associators.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:135

> The Jews and Christians each say, “Follow our faith to be ˹rightly˺ guided.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “No! We follow the faith of Abraham, the upright—who was not a polytheist.”

**Commentary:**

The passed *ummah* is replaced, in a living mouth, with a slogan of a later name: *wa qālū kūnū hūdan aw naṣārā tahtadū qul bal millata Ibrāhīma ḥanīfan wa mā kāna min al-mushrikīn*, “And they say, ‘Be Jews or Christians and you will be guided.’ Say: rather, the *millah* of Ibrāhīm, ḥanīf — and he was not of the associators.” *Kūnū hūdan aw naṣārā*, be Jews or Christians — two slogans joined, as 2:111 joined two exclusive Gardens. Ibn Isḥāq reported from Ibn ʿAbbās that ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣūriyā said to the Prophet (ﷺ), “Guidance is what we follow; follow us and you will be guided,” and the Christians said the like, and the verse came. *Tahtadū*, you will be guided — they make a *nisbah* the *hudā* of 1:6. 2:120 said they will not be pleased until you follow their *millah*. 2:135 is that following offered as a guidance. The answer is not a third slogan of a tribe. It is a father.

*Qul bal millata Ibrāhīma ḥanīfan*, say: rather, the *millah* of Ibrāhīm, ḥanīf. *Bal* is a turning. *Millata* is in the accusative of a follow we follow, or of a *we hold*. Ibn al-Jawzī gathered *ḥanīf*: one who inclines, al-Zajjāj said — the root is an inclining, even a foot that inclines in; one who is upright, Ibn Qutaybah said. The commentators added: sincere, ʿAṭāʾ; one who makes ḥajj; one who worships the One, makes ḥajj, sacrifices, is circumcised, faces the House. 30:30: set your face to the *dīn*, ḥanīf, the *fiṭrah* of Allah. 6:79: Ibrāhīm turned his face to Him who originated the heavens and the earth, ḥanīf, and he was not of the associators. 2:135 is that ḥanīf as a refusal of two later names.

*Wa mā kāna min al-mushrikīn*, and he was not of the associators. 3:67 will say it in full: he was not a Jew and not a Christian, but a ḥanīf muslim, and he was not of the associators. 2:135 plants the last clause first, because *shirk* is the disease of a pagan who claims Ibrāhīm, and a *shirk* of a son is the disease of a church that claims him, and a *shirk* of a rabbi’s word over a Book is a disease of a party that claims him. Al-Qurṭubī hears that *hudā* is not a baptism of a name. It is a *millah* of a ḥanīf. Ibn Kathīr treats *qul* as a command to refuse a guidance that is a leash.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Every child is born upon the *fiṭrah*; then his parents make him a Jew, a Christian, or a Magian” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:135 is that ḥadīth as a *millah*: return to the *fiṭrah*’s father, not to a parent’s later name. He said he was sent with the ḥanīfiyyah. He faced the House Ibrāhīm raised. He slaughtered. He was upon a circumcision of a *fiṭrah*. 2:135 is not a romantic of a pre-Mosaic desert. It is a *tawḥīd* that Mūsā and ʿĪsā (peace be upon them) also brought, before a *millah* of a name made them flags.

Theologically the verse is *al-walāʾ* to a *millah*, not to a slogan. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *hanīfiyyah* is *ikhlāṣ* and a leaving of *shirk*, and that every prophet was upon it. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *bal* is a mercy: you are not told “be a fourth tribe”; you are told “be upon the father of a *tawḥīd*.” Al-Rāzī noted *tahtadū* as a stolen word: 1:6’s *ihdinā* is asked of a Lord, not bought by a baptism or a *nasab*. This *ummah* is tried by a 2:135 in reverse: be of our *jamāʿah*’s name and you will be guided — a *madhhab*, a party, a shaykh — while 2:121’s due recitation is left. *Millat Ibrāhīm ḥanīfan*.

16:123 commanded the Prophet (ﷺ) to follow Ibrāhīm’s *millah* ḥanīf; 6:161 said he was guided to it. 2:135 is that following as an answer to two *daʿwahs* of a name. A guidance that requires you to unwrite 2:129’s messenger is not *hudā*. A guidance that requires you to take a son of 2:116 is not *hudā*. *Hanīf* is an incline away from both. This *ummah* must also incline away from a *shirk* of a grave, a *shirk* of a ruler, a *shirk* of a self. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *aw* as two invitations, not one. They did not agree with each other, 2:113. They agreed that this Prophet (ﷺ) should leave his *millah*. 2:135’s *qul* answers both with one father.

A ḥanīf is a man who inclines away from a crowd’s lord toward a Lord’s crowd of *qānitūn*, 2:116. 2:135 asks this *ummah* to incline when two older crowds offer a guidance that costs a messenger. Incline. The *millah* is older than both crowds, and younger in every chest that says 2:131. When a voice says “be of us to be guided,” answer with a father who was not of the associators. Guidance is a *millah*, not a membership. Be ḥanīf when both names call. Ibrāhīm was not of the associators, and neither is his path. A guidance that asks you to associate a son, a rabbi’s word, or a stone, is not 2:135’s *hudā*. Turn. Be ḥanīf. Follow the *millah*. Say *bal* when a slogan says *kūnū*. Rather, Ibrāhīm, ḥanīf and muslim.

For the reader the verse is a refusal and a following. Do not take a later name as *hudā*. Take Ibrāhīm’s *millah*, ḥanīf, no *shirk*. The next verse will put that *millah* in a creed this *ummah* must say: we believe in Allah, and what was sent down to us, and what was sent down to Ibrāhīm, Ismāʿīl, Isḥāq, Yaʿqūb, and the tribes, and what was given to Mūsā and ʿĪsā and the prophets from their Lord; we make no distinction; we are muslims.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:136

> Say, ˹O believers,˺ “We believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us; and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and his descendants; and what was given to Moses, Jesus, and other prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them. And to Allah we all submit.”

**Commentary:**

The ḥanīf *millah* is now a sentence this *ummah* is commanded to speak: *qūlū āmannā bi-Allāhi wa mā unzila ilaynā wa mā unzila ilā Ibrāhīma wa Ismāʿīla wa Isḥāqa wa Yaʿqūba wa al-asbāṭi wa mā ūtiya Mūsā wa ʿĪsā wa mā ūtiya al-nabiyyūna min rabbihim lā nufarriqu bayna aḥadin minhum wa naḥnu lahu muslimūn*, “Say: we believe in Allah and what has been sent down to us, and what was sent down to Ibrāhīm and Ismāʿīl and Isḥāq and Yaʿqūb and the tribes, and what was given to Mūsā and ʿĪsā, and what was given to the prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and we are to Him muslims.” *Qūlū* is plural: the believers say it, not only a prophet in a private *qul*. 3:84 will give a sister sentence. This is the *ummah*’s *īmān* as a mouth.

*Āmannā bi-Allāh* is first, always first, 2:3, 4:136. Then *wa mā unzila ilaynā*, what was sent down to us: the Qur'an, the *waḥy* upon Muḥammad (ﷺ). Then the former sendings, named in a line that 2:133’s deathbed already loved: Ibrāhīm, Ismāʿīl, Isḥāq, Yaʿqūb, the *asbāṭ* — the tribes, the sons of Yaʿqūb and their prophetic line. Then *mā ūtiya Mūsā wa ʿĪsā*, what was given to Mūsā and ʿĪsā — a giving, *ītīyān*, of a Torah and an Injīl as they were given, not as a later distortion made them. Then *mā ūtiya al-nabiyyūn min rabbihim*, what was given the prophets from their Lord — a general that no prophet is left outside the *īmān*. 4:164: messengers We have told you, and messengers We have not told you. 2:136’s *nabiyyūn* is that width.

*Lā nufarriqu bayna aḥadin minhum*, we do not distinguish between any of them. 2:285 will make it the believers’ creed: we make no distinction between any of His messengers. Distinction here is a *kufr* of a picking: we take Mūsā and leave ʿĪsā; we take ʿĪsā and leave Muḥammad; we take a prophet of our blood and leave a prophet of a valley. 4:150–151 named those who believe in some and cover some as the covering ones truly. This *ummah*’s honor is a chain without a break. *Wa naḥnu lahu muslimūn*, and we are to Him muslims — 2:133’s last clause, 2:131’s answer, now a people’s *dīn*.

Abū Hurayrah (may Allah be pleased with him) narrated: the People of the Book used to recite the Torah in Hebrew and explain it in Arabic to the muslims, and the Messenger of Allah (ﷺ) said, “Do not believe the People of the Book and do not deny them. Say: we believe in Allah and what was sent down to us…” and he recited this verse (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:136 is a *adab* of a report you cannot check: you do not swallow a *isrāʾīliyyah* as a *dīn*; you do not deny a truth that may be a remnant; you stand on this sentence.
