Al-Qurṭubī hears a *fiqh* of *ḥadīth* of the Book’s people: pause, and hold 2:136.

Ibn Kathīr treats the verse as the opposite of 2:113’s emptying. They said each other were upon nothing. This *ummah* says we believe in what was given to both, from their Lord, and we add what was sent down to us. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *lā nufarriqu* is not a mixing of *sharāʾiʿ*: Mūsā’s *sharīʿah* is not this *ummah*’s law after *naskh* of 2:106; ʿĪsā’s particular is not a son. It is a mixing of *īmān* in persons of prophets as truthful. Al-Rāzī noted *min rabbihim* as a single Lord of all the givings, 2:139’s coming argument.

Theologically the verse is *īmān* in messengers as a pillar. The ḥadīth of Jibrīl (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim) named it. 2:177, 2:285. This *ummah* is tried by a reverse picking: we take this Prophet (ﷺ) and mock Mūsā; we take a Qur'an and sneer at a former Book as if no *nūr* had ever been in it, 5:44, 5:46. 2:136 forbids that sneer. It also forbids a later church’s picking that leaves this Prophet out. *Qūlū*. Say it.

2:285 will make the Messenger believe, and the believers, in Allah, His angels, His Books, His messengers, and they will say we heard and we obeyed. 2:136 is that *īmān* as a *qūlū* of a people. A mouth that picks a prophet to love and a prophet to mock has not said *lā nufarriqu*. 4:152 promised those who believe in Allah and His messengers and do not distinguish a wage. 2:136 is the sentence of that wage. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *asbāṭ* as a naming that honors Israel’s line inside a muslim creed. A *ummah* that recites 2:136 and then hates a *nasab* as a *nasab* has not understood *lā nufarriqu*. 2:120 still stands: you do not follow their *millah*. 2:136 stands: you do not unwrite their prophets.

When a report of a former Book comes, hold 2:136 and the Prophet’s (ﷺ) *adab*: do not swallow, do not deny at random. Stand on what was sent down to you. That standing is not a hatred. It is a *mīzān*. Say the creed when a quote comes, and keep 2:285’s we heard and we obeyed as the limb of the mouth.

For the reader the verse is a creed to keep in the mouth when a Torah is quoted, when a Gospel is quoted, when a prophet is mocked. Believe in Allah. Believe in what was sent to you. Believe in what was given them from their Lord. Do not pick. Submit. The next verse will judge the hearers of that creed: if they believe in the like of what you believe, they are guided; if they turn away, they are in schism; Allah will suffice you against them. He is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:137

> So if they believe in what you believe, then they will indeed be ˹rightly˺ guided. But if they turn away, they are simply opposed ˹to the truth˺. But Allah will spare you their evil. For He is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing.

**Commentary:**

The creed is made a measure: *fa-in āmanū bi-mithli mā āmantum bihi fa-qadi ihtadaw wa in tawallaw fa-innamā hum fī shiqāqin fa-sa-yakfīkahumu Allāhu wa huwa al-samīʿu al-ʿalīm*, “So if they believe in the like of what you have believed in, they have been guided; and if they turn away, they are only in schism. Allah will suffice you against them. He is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing.” *Bi-mithli mā āmantum bihi*, in the like of what you believed in. Ibn al-Jawzī gathered: if they have a belief like yours; if they believe in your Book as you believe in theirs; and that *mithl* may be extra in the language, meaning if they believe in what you believe. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr take the sense: if they believe in all the Books and messengers as you do, without a picking of 2:136’s opposite, they have *ihtidāʾ*. 2:135 offered *hudā* as a name of a *millah*. 2:137 returns *hudā* to a *īmān* like this *ummah*’s *īmān*.

*Wa in tawallaw fa-innamā hum fī shiqāq*, and if they turn away, they are only in schism. *Tawallaw* is they turn the back, 2:101’s gesture in a will. *Shiqāq* is a split, a side taken against, 2:176’s those who differ in the Book are in a far *shiqāq*. *Innamā* is a restriction: do not read their turning as a confusion of a seeker. It is a split. Ibn Kathīr treats the clause as a realism after a wide creed: you have opened 2:136 as a door; a turning from that door is not your failure of *bayān*. It is *shiqāq*. 2:119: you will not be asked about the companions of the Blaze.

*Fa-sa-yakfīkahumu Allāh*, Allah will suffice you against them. *Kifāyah* is a sufficing, 2:137’s mercy upon a prophet who would almost destroy himself with grief, 18:6. 2:107: you have no *wali* and no *naṣīr* besides Him. 2:137: He is enough against a *shiqāq*. The verb is future: He will suffice. A *kifāyah* in Madinah was a *hijrah*, a trench, a Fatḥ, a *ḥujjah* that remained. A *kifāyah* in a later hour may be a proof, a patience, a unexpected opening. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Be mindful of Allah, He will be mindful of you. Be mindful of Allah, you will find Him before you” (al-Tirmidhī, Ibn ʿAbbās). He said, “If you ask, ask Allah; if you seek help, seek help from Allah.” 2:137 is that help named as a *kifāyah* against a schism, not only against a hunger.

*Wa huwa al-samīʿu al-ʿalīm*, and He is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing. 2:127’s Names upon a *duʿāʾ* of stones are here Names upon a *shiqāq* of mouths. He hears the slogan of 2:135 and the creed of 2:136. He knows a chest that turns. Al-Qurṭubī hears a consolation: you are not asked to win a debate by a louder *shiqāq*. You are asked to say 2:136, then to be sufficed. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *kifāyah* is a *tawakkul* that does not retire a *balāgh*. Al-Rāzī noted *mithl* as a standard: guidance is not “a little like you, a little like them.” It is the like of this *īmān*.

Theologically the verse is the *ummah*’s spine after a wide sentence. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *wasatiyyah* is not a blending of creeds; it is 2:143’s middle that witnesses with 2:136. A blending is 2:120’s following. A *shiqāq* is named so that a caller will not call a refusal a “shared journey.” This *ummah* is tried by two ditches: a ditch that never says 2:136’s width, and a ditch that never says 2:137’s *shiqāq*. The verse says both.

The Prophet (ﷺ) wrote to Heraclius “Aslam taslam” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī): submit, you will be safe. 2:137 is that offer as a *in*. If they believe like you, they are guided. If they turn, He suffices. He hears. He knows.

8:62: if they intend to deceive you, Allah is enough for you; He is the One who supported you with His help and with the believers. 2:137’s *kifāyah* is that enough. A schism is noisy; a sufficiency is often quiet: a proof that remains, a chest that does not sell 2:120, a *ummah* that is not emptied. Do not measure *kifāyah* only as a political win. Measure it as a *dīn* kept. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *fa-qadi ihtadaw* as a past of a completion: guidance is not a later mood after a blend. It is this *īmān* when it is taken. 1:6’s path is this.

A *shiqāq* will try to name itself a dialogue. 2:137 allows a believing like yours as guidance, and names a turning as split. Keep the names honest. Then take the *kifāyah* as a *tawakkul*, not as a permission to stop 2:129’s reciting upon people. He hears the *shiqāq*. He knows the chest. He suffices. You convey. That division of labor is 2:119 and 2:137 in one file. Do not despair when a turning is named *shiqāq*. He suffices. You are not billed for a blaze. You are billed for a *balāgh*. Keep 2:136 on the tongue, name a turning honestly, and sleep the sleep of a man who has a *Kāfī*. He hears. He knows. He suffices. Walk with that triad when a schism is loud and a chest is tired. The *Kāfī* does not sleep, and a schism does not own the last word at all.

For the reader the verse is a measure and a sufficiency. Offer the like of your *īmān*. If they take it, they are guided. If they turn, it is schism. He will suffice you. Hear and know that He hears and knows. The next verse will name the dye of that *īmān*: the coloring of Allah — and who is better than Allah at coloring? And we are worshippers of Him.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:138

> This is the ˹natural˺ Way of Allah. And who is better than Allah in ordaining a way? And we worship ˹none but˺ Him.

**Commentary:**

The sufficing is given a color: *ṣibghata Allāhi wa man aḥsanu min Allāhi ṣibghatan wa naḥnu lahu ʿābidūn*, “The dye of Allah — and who is better than Allah in dye? — and we are worshippers of Him.” *Ṣibghah* is a dye, a dip, a coloring that enters a cloth, not a paint that sits on a face. Ibn ʿAbbās and Mujāhid, as al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record, said it is the *dīn* of Allah, the *islām* that dyes a servant as a baptism was claimed to dye a child. The Christians of Najrān, the *salaf* said, dyed a child in a water and called it a *ṣibghah* of a *dīn*. 2:138 answers with a dye that is not a water of a church. It is Allah’s. *Ṣibghata Allāh* is in the accusative of a we take, a we follow, a we hold — like *millata Ibrāhīm* in 2:135. Take Allah’s dye.

*Wa man aḥsanu min Allāhi ṣibghatan*, and who is better than Allah as a dyer? A question that empties a font. A priest’s water, a tribe’s name, a party’s color, a flag’s cloth — none is *aḥsan*. The *fiṭrah* of 30:30 is this dye in a birth; 2:138 is this dye in a *dīn*. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Every child is born upon the *fiṭrah*” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). He said, “Allah created the servants ḥunafāʾ, then the satans turned them” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, in the ḥadīth of ʿIyāḍ). 2:138 is a return to the first color. Al-Qurṭubī hears a *fiqh*: *īmān* is not a badge on a lapel. It is a color in a wool. 2:8’s sentence “we believe” without a dye is a paint.

*Wa naḥnu lahu ʿābidūn*, and we are to Him worshippers. The *lām* of 2:131, 2:133, 2:136 is here a *ʿibādah*. A dye that does not end in worship is a fashion. Ibn Kathīr treats the clause as the fruit of the dye: you can see a *ṣibghah* in a *sujūd*, a *ḥalāl*, a *tawbah*, a refusal of 2:116’s son. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *ṣibghah* is a name of a *iḥsān* that has soaked: the servant is not sometimes Allah’s and sometimes another’s; the wool has taken. Al-Rāzī noted the question *man aḥsanu* as a *tawḥīd* of a pedagogy: He who originated 2:117 is better at coloring a chest than a church is at coloring a infant.

Theologically the verse is *fiṭrah* as a *dīn*. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that the *fiṭrah* is a preparedness for *tawḥīd*, and that *ṣibghah* is the *dīn* that matches it. A *dīn* that paints a Trinity over a *fiṭrah* is a stain. A *dīn* that paints a *shirk* of a grave over a *fiṭrah* is a stain. 2:138 asks: who is better? This *ummah* is tried by a reverse dye: a secular color that claims to be “neutral” while it washes 2:136 out of a child, a school, a law. Neutral is also a *ṣibghah*. Who is better than Allah?

The Prophet (ﷺ) loved good names and good colors of a *dīn* that show: a *ghurrah* of *wuḍūʾ* on the Day (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim), a mark of *sujūd* that 48:29 named. 2:138 is not a command to a uniform of a party. It is a command to a color of a *ʿibādah*. ʿUmar (may Allah be pleased with him) said, “We are a people Allah honored with *islām*; whatever else we seek honor in, Allah will humiliate us.” That honor is a *ṣibghah*.

A dye that has entered will show in a *ḥalāl* of a bite, a lowering of a gaze, a *ṣadaqah* of a hidden date, a refusal of a joke of 2:104. 2:177 will say piety is not a turning of faces only; it is *īmān*, spending, prayer, *zakāh*, keepers of a covenant, the patient. That list is a *ṣibghah* in deeds. A people who take a color of a flag and leave 2:177 have not taken Allah’s dye. They have taken a paint. 30:30 said there is no changing of Allah’s creation; that *fiṭrah* is the wool 2:138 dyes. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on the word as a mercy to a people who were offered 2:135’s “be Jews or Christians.” Be dyed by Allah. Worship Him. The two sentences are one *millah*.

A baptism of a water lasts a minute. A *ṣibghah* of Allah lasts a life and a grave and a gathering. Ask Him to dye you in a *ṣalāh* that 2:3 established, a *zakāh* that 2:110 gave, a face that 2:112 submitted. Then worship Him. A dye that does not worship is a fashion of a season. Dip again in a *tawbah* when the color fades. 2:128 asked *tub ʿalaynā* at a House; 2:138 asks a *ṣibghah* that a *tawbah* can restore. He is better at coloring than you are at staining. Ask a new dip, then worship. A *fiṭrah* is wool; *islām* is dye; *ʿibādah* is the color seen. Who is better than Allah at that work? No church, no tribe, no season. Take His dye in a *wuḍūʾ* that will be a *ghurrah* on a Day, in a *ṣalāh* that dyes a morning, in a *tawbah* that dyes a night. Then worship Him alone. A dye that has entered will not fear 2:135’s two names, because it has taken a better color from a better Dyer, and a worship that matches the wool He created in a *fiṭrah* that 30:30 named and that no later crowd may wash it out.

For the reader the verse is a dip, not a sticker. Let the *dīn* enter. Who is better at coloring? Worship Him. The next verse will take the argument to the Lord they all name: would you dispute with us about Allah, while He is our Lord and your Lord? We have our deeds, you have yours. We are devoted to Him.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:139

> Say, “Would you dispute with us about Allah, while He is our Lord and your Lord? We are accountable for our deeds and you for yours. And we are devoted to Him ˹alone˺.

**Commentary:**

The dye is defended with a Lord they already confess: *qul a-tuḥājjūnanā fī Allāhi wa huwa rabbunā wa rabbukum wa lanā aʿmālunā wa lakum aʿmālukum wa naḥnu lahu mukhliṣūn*, “Say: would you dispute with us about Allah, while He is our Lord and your Lord? For us are our deeds, and for you are your deeds. And we are to Him devoted.” *A-tuḥājjūnanā fī Allāh*, would you argue us in Allah — a *ḥijāj* about the One, about who is nearer, about who owns a *millah*, about a son, about a blood of a prophet. Ibn Kathīr reads the addressees as the People of the Book, and some of the *salaf* included the pagans. The question is a exposing: you argue about Allah as if He were a property of a council. *Wa huwa rabbunā wa rabbukum*, and He is our Lord and your Lord. 2:139 returns 1:2’s *Rabb* as a shared *rubūbiyyah*. They already know a Lord of the worlds. 29:46: our God and your God is one. A dispute that forgets that sharing is a dispute of a *hawā*.

*Wa lanā aʿmālunā wa lakum aʿmālukum*, for us our deeds, for you your deeds. 2:134’s *kasb* is now a *adab* of a argument. 109:6: for you your *dīn*, for me my *dīn* — a Makkan *sūrah* of a refusal, not of a blending. 2:139 is a Madinan refusal that still names a shared Lord. You will not be asked about their deeds; they will not be asked about yours. Stop a *ḥijāj* that is a prosecution of a *nasab* and a forgetting of a *ʿamal*. Al-Qurṭubī hears a *fiqh* of *daʿwah*: convey 2:136; do not live in a courtroom of their fathers. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that *lanā aʿmālunā* is a freeing: a muslim is not owned by a neighbor’s argument; he is owned by a deed he will meet.

*Wa naḥnu lahu mukhliṣūn*, and we are to Him devoted, sincere. *Ikhlāṣ* is 39:3’s *dīn* made sincere, 98:5’s command, 112’s *sūrah*. The Prophet (ﷺ) said that Allah (الله) said, “I am the most free of partners from *shirk*; whoever does a deed associating another with Me, I leave him and his *shirk*” (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). 2:139’s last word is that *ikhlāṣ* as a people’s claim. A dispute about Allah that is a show for a sitting is not *mukhliṣūn*. A deed for a Lord that does not need a neighbor’s applause is.

Theologically the verse is *tawḥīd al-rubūbiyyah* used against a *tawḥīd* of a tribe. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that many of the People of the Book and the pagans affirmed a *Rabb* and then associated in *ulūhiyyah*. 2:139 holds the affirmation and asks the *ikhlāṣ*. Al-Rāzī noted the pairing *rabbunā wa rabbukum* as a blocking of “our God is nearer.” 2:115: east and west are His. 2:107: the *mulk* is His. This *ummah* is tried by a *ḥijāj* that forgets a shared Lord and a private deed: we argue as if winning a comment is a *kasb*, while 2:110’s prayer is thin. *Lanā aʿmālunā*.

The Prophet (ﷺ) did not retire *daʿwah*. He said, “Convey from me even a verse” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:139 is not a silence. It is a *qul* that shortens a quarrel: He is Lord of both; deeds are each’s; we are sincere. Then 2:140 will ask the historical lie. 42:15 commanded: I have been commanded to be just between you; Allah is our Lord and your Lord; for us our deeds, for you your deeds; no argument between us and you; Allah will gather us; to Him is the becoming. 2:139 is that verse’s seed. A just caller does not steal a Lord as a property, and does not retire a *balāgh*. He says a shared *Rabb*, a private deed, a sincerity. Then he leaves the gathering to a Day. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *mukhliṣūn* as a *ḥāl* of a people, not of a moment. Sincerity that is a mood in a masjid and a *shirk* of a showing in a market is not 2:139.

109 taught a clean break of *dīn*. 2:139 teaches a clean break of *ḥijāj* about a Lord who is already shared. Kindness of 60:8 remains for those who do not fight you for religion. *Ikhlāṣ* remains. 2:120 remains: they will not be pleased until you follow. Do not follow. Be *mukhliṣūn*.

A *ḥijāj* about Allah that is a contest of a tribe is a *ḥijāj* He did not ask. He asked a *ikhlāṣ*. 39:11–14: say I have been commanded to worship Allah, making the *dīn* sincere to Him. 2:139 is that command as an answer to a dispute. Leave the win. Keep the deed. He is Lord of both houses. A sincere chest does not need a neighbor to lose in order to be upon a *Rabb*. 2:148: each has a direction; race to *khayrāt*. Racing is not a *ḥijāj*. It is a *kasb*. Dispute less about a Lord you both name; do more of a deed He will weigh. Sincerity is the last word of the verse. Keep it as the last word of a sitting. He is our Lord and theirs. Deeds are each’s. Devotion is to Him. That is enough sentence for a long quarrel, and enough work for a long life. Be *mukhliṣūn* and leave the rest.

For the reader the verse is a shortening of a fight. He is our Lord and theirs. Your deeds are yours. Theirs are theirs. Be devoted to Him, not to a win. The next verse will ask the last historical claim of this twenty: do you say Ibrāhīm, Ismāʿīl, Isḥāq, Yaʿqūb, and the tribes were Jews or Christians? Who knows more, you or Allah? Who is more unjust than one who hides a testimony from Allah? He is not unaware of what you do.

## Sūrah al-Baqarah 2:140

> Do you claim that Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and his descendants were all Jews or Christians?” Say, “Who is more knowledgeable: you or Allah?” Who does more wrong than those who hide the testimony they received from Allah? And Allah is never unaware of what you do.

**Commentary:**

The shared Lord is now asked a shared history: *am taqūlūna inna Ibrāhīma wa Ismāʿīla wa Isḥāqa wa Yaʿqūba wa al-asbāṭa kānū hūdan aw naṣārā qul a-antum aʿlamu ami Allāhu wa man aẓlamu mimman katama shahādatan ʿindahu min Allāhi wa mā Allāhu bi-ghāfilin ʿammā taʿmalūn*, “Or do you say that Ibrāhīm and Ismāʿīl and Isḥāq and Yaʿqūb and the tribes were Jews or Christians? Say: are you more knowing, or Allah? And who is more unjust than one who hides a testimony he has from Allah? And Allah is not unaware of what you do.” Khattab’s English on the page begins at the claim; the Arabic opens with *am taqūlūna*, or do you say. The claim is 2:135’s slogan pushed backward onto a father who died before both names. 3:65: “O People of the Book, why do you argue about Ibrāhīm when the Torah and the Injīl were not sent down until after him? Will you not reason?” 2:140 is that reasoning as a question. Jews as a *millah* of a name are after Mūsā. Christians as a *millah* of a name are after ʿĪsā. Ibrāhīm (peace be upon him) is before both. 2:133’s children said *muslimūn*. 3:67: he was a ḥanīf muslim.

*Qul a-antum aʿlamu ami Allāh*, say: are you more knowing, or Allah? A question that is a *taʾdīb*. 2:30’s angels were told “I know what you do not know.” 2:140’s claimants are asked who is *aʿlam*. Allah (الله) has reported 2:131–133. A claim against a report is a claim of a greater knowledge. Ibn Kathīr treats the question as a closing of a file: you may not baptize a prophet with a later name. Al-Qurṭubī hears a *fiqh* of *nasab* of a *dīn*: a *dīn* is dated by a *waḥy*, not by a later flag.

*Wa man aẓlamu mimman katama shahādatan ʿindahu min Allāh*, and who is more unjust than one who hides a testimony he has with him from Allah. *Man aẓlamu* is 2:114’s superlative, now upon a hiding, 2:42’s mix and hide, 2:159’s those who hide what We sent down of clear proofs. Ibn al-Jawzī gathered the testimony: Allah’s witness that Ibrāhīm and those with him were muslims, which they hid, al-Ḥasan and Zayd ibn Aslam said; or the truth of this Prophet (ﷺ) and of *islām*, which they knew, Abū al-ʿĀliyah and Qatādah said. Both are one *kitmān*: a testimony *ʿindahu min Allāh*, with him, from Allah. 2:146: they know him as they know their sons. 7:157: they find him written with them. Hiding that writing is 2:140’s *ẓulm*.

*Wa mā Allāhu bi-ghāfilin ʿammā taʿmalūn*, and Allah is not unaware of what you do. 2:74, 2:85, 2:140 — the refrain of a *ghaflah* denied of Him. A hidden testimony is not hidden from the One who is *ʿAlīm*. 2:110’s *baṣīr* of a sending, 2:137’s *samīʿ ʿalīm* of a *shiqāq*, 2:140’s not-unaware of a hiding. The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “The two feet of a servant will not move on the Day of Rising until he is asked about his knowledge, what he did with it” (al-Tirmidhī, in the well-known meaning). A knowledge hidden is a knowledge that will ask.

Theologically the verse is the ethics of a testimony. Ibn Taymiyyah wrote that *kitmān* of a *ʿilm* is a *ẓulm* upon a people and upon a Lord who sent it to be shown, 3:187. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote that naming Ibrāhīm a Jew or a Christian is a *kitmān* in the form of a claim: you hide a *islām* by hanging a later sign on a earlier chest. Al-Rāzī noted *a-antum aʿlamu* as a *tawḥīd* of knowledge: the Lord of 2:117 who says *kun* is the Lord who reports a *millah*. This *ummah* is tried by its own *kitmān*: a verse left untranslated because a state dislikes it, a ḥadīth left untaught because a room will frown, a *nasab* of a *dīn* rewritten to please 2:120. *Man aẓlamu*.

3:66–68 will continue the argument: you are those who argued in what you know; why do you argue in what you do not know? Allah knows, you do not. The nearest of people to Ibrāhīm are those who followed him, and this Prophet, and those who believe. 2:140 is that nearness as a hidden testimony some refused to show. A *ummah* that shows it is a *ummah* of 2:121’s due recitation. Al-Ṭabarī pauses on *al-asbāṭ* as a last honor of Israel’s tribes inside a verse that refuses to make them Jews in Ibrāhīm’s meaning. They were *muslimūn* if they were upon Yaʿqūb’s *ilāh*. A later name does not travel backward.

The Prophet (ﷺ) said, “Convey from me even a verse” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī). 2:140 is the opposite of a convey: a hide. He said, “Whoever is asked about a knowledge and hides it will be bridled with a bridle of Fire” (Abū Dāwūd, al-Tirmidhī). 2:140 is that bridle as a *man aẓlamu*.

A testimony hidden to keep a *millah*’s pride is a testimony that will speak on a Day of 2:123 when no ransom is taken. Show it now. 2:159 promised a curse upon who hide what We sent down of clear proofs after We made them clear. 2:140 is that hiding as a *man aẓlamu*.

For the reader the verse is a last wall of this twenty. Do not baptize Ibrāhīm with a later name. Allah is more knowing. Do not hide a testimony. He is not unaware. The next verse will repeat 2:134 almost word for word: that was a *ummah* that has passed; for them what they earned, for you what you earned; you will not be asked about what they did. Then the sūrah will turn a qiblah, and a foolish question will come, and a middle *ummah* will be named.
